WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King and Queen have promised to attend the celebration of the Jubilee of the Crystal Palace. Few people are unfamiliar with the beauty of this famous structure, either from a visit or from pictures, and the Palace is rich in historic memories. It was there that Thanksgiving Day was observed after his Majesty's recovery from typhoid fever thirty-two years ago, and it has also been the scene of numerous Royal visits on special occasions. The building was erected in Hyde Park, for the exhibition of 1851, but it was afterwards removed to Sydenham Hill, and it was in 1854 that it was opened as a popular place of entertainment. It has never been a great financial success, owing chiefly to the want of railway facilities. The Palace is not more than six or seven miles from the very heart of London, and yet it means a journey of an hour,or an hour and a half,or more, to the great majority of the residents of the Metropolis. The building has been far exceeded in size at subsequent exhibitions, but for beauty of design and situation, the Crystal Palace still possesses unrivalled attractions.

The Princess at the Bradford Exhibition.—The Princess of Wales has always made a point of wearing dress fabrics, laces and embroideries, of British manu- facture, and it naturally gave Her Royal Highness much pleasure to attend the opening of the Cartwright Memorial Exhibition at Bradford. This is intended not only as a memorial to the inventor of the power loom, but also to give a display of the beauty and excellence of the productions of the local textile industry. The dress worn by the Princess at tke opening of the Exhibi- tion was in itself, sufficient evidence of the vast improvement that has taken place in recent years in the manufacture of British-made dress fabrics. It was built of Priestleys' " Panneau " face cloth, in a beautiful shade of pale grey, and was trimmed and medallioned with.rich black and white braiding. For a tailor-made fabric, nothing more beautiful has ever been produced than this material, which is so fine and soft as to almost resemble suede in appearance. In addition to textile, there is also a display of costumes, made exclusively of Bradford materials, by the leading fashion houses of London and Paris. These also go to show that Brad- ford can as successfully produce the daintiest fabrics for afternoon and evening toilettes, as the tweeds and sturdy worsteds for which its looms have long been famous.

Norwich Silk.—The Prince of Wales referred at the opening of the Bradford Exhibition, to the early asso- ciation of the weaving trade with his own native county of Norfolk. For many of our textile manufactures, as well as lace making, we are indebted to foreign refugees whosettled in these Isles in olden times. It was the Huguenots for instance, who settled in Dublin and in- troduced the manufacture of Irish poplin and the same people brought the silk industry to London.—But the manufacture of silk at Norwich goes .back to the very ancient times dud still possesses feltures of its own, The King sometimes wears Mufflers of Norwich silk. These spates are usually of the jacquard pattern in every possible colouring. The Prince of Wales is more frequently seen with a silk wrap round the neck,but His Royal Highness prefers the plain white. No. wich silk is also worn by many other members of the Royal Family, the Princesses using it for ties in different colourings according to their fancy. The Early Victorian Revival.—The early Victorian style is the admitted motif cf this summer's fashions, and is also being over accentuated. There was little in early Victorianism that was not intrinsically ugly, and it is the craze of the modern modiste for over-elabora- tion that no doubt accounts for its revival. Some of the models would be well worthy of our grandmother's time, with their redundancy of shapes and combinations of silk, velvet, cloth, chiffon, and lace; flounced and ruched in a manner that was the pride of the "best dresses " of flit), years ago. For summer freaks no treatment can be more suitable than the gauging, tuck- ing, and flouncing,which give beauty to dainty materials, but for tailor-made gowns these devices are out of place.

- Modish Wraps.—All the new cloth wraps are in light shades, pearl greys, champagne, and ivory, being first favourites. This is a mode those among us, who are forced to be economical will appreciate as such neutral wraps can be worn with almost any toilette. In the way of trimming, handsome fringes, rich oriental embroidery, and silk cluny lace are preferred by leading modistes, who are again adding touches of gold in some form or other, to these creations. For race coats shantung silk and taffetas are the most approved fabrics, the trimming consisting of oriental embroidery, and exquisite lace.

Saves Time and Temper.—Shirts and day blouses are now made to fasten down the front. This is a return to a more convenient fashion, for which those who have to fasten and unfasten their own garments will be thankful. The fastenings are not, of course visible, and the whereabouts of such details is sometimes rendered more obscure by the collaeband being made to hook at the side or back. Very high bands are now the correct thing, and these are kept up to the required height, by means of small covered bones.

More Ornamental than Useful.—Paper-made millinery, it is said, is this summer, to prove its practical utility. That most charming creations of this sort are possible is not to be doubted, as many more wonderful things than a woman's hat have been made out of paper. Some time ago we were to have had paper stockings, but they have not yet taken the place of those that require wash- ing and darning, and one cannot be very sanguine about paper millinery, even though it is stated that it will stand all kinds of weather. Amongst other novelties,to which some people are always giving their attention,are fabrics woven of glass or metallic spun threads. One production of this kind, we are told, changes colour with varying atmospheric conditions. and is not only an everlastinr, wearing material, but an excellent baro - meter all time. Wo should then all know whether the weather would be suitable for a light summer frock or a mackintosh, and it would make an ideal covering for a sunshade, since no one would venture out with this if it plainly indicated that leaden skies were to be expected. The Over-done Veil.—Veils continue to be one of the features of dress, and many that one sees at fashionable gatherings might well serve for portieres. or lamp shades, rather than for giving the charm of suggestion and slight mystery to a woman's face. A carefully adjusted veil of the right size, colour and texture, is very suggestive of a dressy appearance, but the impres- sion created by the present elaborate draperies of gauze, chiffon, and lace, borders almost upon the ludic- rous. These veils will, however, no doubt tave a very short life, as they have been exaggerated to a degree, and the fashion has been carried to such extremes as to lose all its charm and dignity. A Useful Hint.—Eiderdown quilts when washed in the following manor will look almost new. Make a lather of boiled soap and warm water,add a little borax, then knead the quilt about well in the suds, wring through a machine and rinse twice in lukewarm water. Shake the eiderdown well, and hang in a good wind to dry.. Shake frequently during this process. MADGE.

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

Another Grievance Against the Corporation.

The monthly meeting of the Trades and Labour Council was held in Bold-street Wesleyan schools on Tuesday evening, Mr. J. Podmore, jun.,presiding.

MR. PIERPOn'T; M.P., AND THE TRADES DISPUTES BILL. Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P. wrote acknowledging the receipt of a letter from the Secretary asking him to support the Trades Disputes Bill. " I have to say " he added "that seeing that a royal commission is now con- sidering the questions involved in the Bill, any present attempt at legislation is in my opinion premature." Mr. JOLLEY moved that the Secretary should reply to Mr. Pierpoint regretting that he. could not see his way to vote for a measure of simple justice to trades unionists. Mr. YORK seconded.

On-a vote being tliken it was decided to take no further action in the matter.

A GRIEVANCE AGAINsT THE WATER COMMITTEE.

JOINERS INDIGNANT AT COUNCILLOR tSTOTT'S ACTION.

The SECRETARY submitted correspondence with reference to the employment of a joiner in the water department of the Corporation. The Town Clerk wrote stating that he had made inquiries and was informed that the joiner was engaged early in March by the water engineer in response to a request from him (Mr. Whittle) to (Ind employment for men out of wnrk. The young man was not yet out of his time and would not be 21 until August next. When engaged he was asked what wages he required and he said Bd. per hour. He (Mr. Whittle) was informed that before coming to the Corporation the man was engaged in a joiner's shop at 7id per hour. Councillor Stott's letter on the matter would be laid before the Water Committee and in the meantime he would be glad to hear what wages the Trades Council considered under the circumstances the young man was entitled to. In his reply Councillor STOTT stated that the com- plaint came from the Carpenters' and Joiners' branch and their delegates were most emphatic in their objec- tion and in stating particulars as to the conditions of employment. The president of the branch informed him only that week that the joiner was 22 years of age. He disliked to make complaints founded on wrong information, but as secretary of the Trades Council it was his duty to carry out their instructions. It seemed to him that the wages paid were satisfactory for an improver, but the Council would have preferred that a fully-qualified joiner should have been employed at the recognized district rate of wages. If it was the inten- tion of the committee to pay the recognized rate when the joiner attained his majority, he saw no cause for complaint, but that was only his personal opinion. Mr. BUCKLEY said copies of the correspondence were lefore the joiners' branch meeting, and the members were extrettely indignant at the Town Clerk's letter and the Secretary's reply. They considered the latter was half an apology for raising the protest, and that Coun- cillor Stott ought to have made further inquiries before writing. They did not consider it was the duty of the secretary to express any opinions of his own on the Council's action. It was not true that the joiner was not twenty-one. The man informed him on making application to join their society that he was twenty-one last Christmas. They did not recognize improvers in their trade. A young man under twenty-one ought to be working at apprentices' wages, but on attaining his majority he should receive 9}d. per hour. Mr. Buckley denied that the man was taken into the service of the department in response tojthe unemployed agita- tion. He obtained the situation through an official who lived near him. Considerable discussion ensued.

The SECRETARY said he had interviewed Mr. Lloyd who told him that the young man's mother said he was twenty-one next August. He would not have employed the man knowing that he was over twenty one. He (Councillor Stott) admitted that he made a serious mistake in referring to an improver, because he now found that the joiners did not recognize improvers. It was decided to make every effort to obtain wages of 9-1 per hour for the joiner. Mr. SMITH objected on the ground that the man had only just joined the society, and contended that a fully qualified journeyman should have the situation. His amendment that the matter be not entertained fell to the ground.

It was further resolved that in future all complaints as to conditions of etaployment must be forwarded to -the Trades Council in writing by the branch secretaries.

THE SEARCH FORA CANDIDATE.

Mr. BucKLEY submitted the report of the Labour Representation Committee. He said that in reply to the letter of the Secretary, asking for the recommenda- tion of a Parliamentary candidate, a letter had been received from Mr. Macdonald, secretary of the National Labour Representation Committee, recommending a name the committee did not yet desire to be disclosed to the press and the public. The suggested candidate was a member of a society which had a branch in Warrington. The society was financing him and Mr. Macdonald considered he would be a very suitable man for the constituency. Correspondence had taken place and the gentleman had been invited to address a meeting of the committee and state his views. In regard to Bewsey ward bye-election, it was not con- sidered advisable to bring forward a candidate. The report was adopted.

THE WIDNES AND RUNCORN TRANS- PORTER BRIDGE. Pinning the Last Cable.

An interesting stage in the construction of the Widnes and Runcorn rransporter Bridge was reached on Mon- day afternoon, when the last cable strand was stretched across the Mersey,and was fastened at the Runcorn end by Sir John T. Brunner, Bart, M.P., the chairman of directors. A meeting of directors had just been held, and amongst those present to witness the ceremony were Mr. H. Seton-Karr. M.P. for St. Helens (vice-chairman). Alderman Quinn (Mayor of Widnes), Alderman Owens (Widnes),Messrs.R.H.Posnett(ex-chairm-in of the Rumorn Urban Council),Fmnk Gaskell(Liverpool),W.Handley(Sir John's polical agent), J. J. Webster (engineer of the bridge), T. L. Chase (resident engineer), J. M. Frith (Runcorn), and Albert Nuttall. (representative of the Arrol Bridge and Roof Company, Glasgow, who sires building the bridge). The stretching of the cables has occupied about eight months. Each of the two cable consists of nineteen steel ropes bound together, each rope being built up of 127 wires 0.16 inch diameter, the whole cable thus consisting of 2,413 wires. Each wire is capable of withstanding a tensile strain of 95 tons per square inch. The diameter of each cable is 12 inches, and the weight of both cables is 243 tons. The cables are anchored into the solid rock, 30 feet from the rock surface. The next woit is the erection of the stiffening girders, from which the transporter car will be sus- pended.

The Examiner Saturday, May 14 1904.

I FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

I That most capricious of all months—April As-being now over, we look forward to a season ett en we may expect more sunshine and fewer showers. Bright skies demand clothes to correspond, and so the dressmakers now have their hands full, and are besieged with orders, for there are many women who never make up their mind till the last minute, and there is consequently an uncomfortable rush and a difficulty in obtaining the dainty toilettes at the time arranged. It is a mistake to permit the idle days of storm and shower to become lost opportunities for deciding as to how we shall be clad in the days of summer sunshine.

I have been marvelling this year at the wonderful evolution of that branch of business which makes it its aim in life to supply ready made skirts of all kinds with material tucked, gauged, or lace inserted for the bodice, matching the particular form of trimming used on the skirt. There are gowns

A NEAT TOILETTE

of this style suited for every possible circum- stance and hour of the day. Those of linen, plain or with a broken pattern or check trimmed with a little coarse lace insertion, offer charming possibilities for morning wear during the next few months and make nicely into bodice and skirt or a loose coatee finished by a lace or linen collar. There are also cloth costumes for colder days such as contin- ually recur during our very variable summer. A dress of this style forms the subject of my first sketch. It is made of navy blue cloth with the skirt of a convenient walking length. The coat is a smart little Eton with handsome revers and a shoulder cape. It can be worn open or closed, as preferred. The revers are faced with cream cloth which is outlined in narrow gold braid. It is worn with a smart hat of black lace and chenille trimmed with a long ostrich feather. For afternoon wear I have seen some charming skirts ready for making up over a foundation. These are of voile or very thin canvas-like material in all sorts of pretty colours, or the ever-useful black. They are gauged down to the knees so as to fit the figure closely all round while a full flowing flounce or frill give the fashionable fulness round the feet. It is wonderful how the leaders of fashion have retained their fancy for a skirt which however elaborate

A PRETTY BODICE.

clearly outlines the curves of the figure. Of course, there are skirts of very thin muslin or lace which are drawn fully into the band, but apart from these skirts, continue of a clinging description, and when designed for wear indoors are trained. For out of doors, how- ever, the skirt of which clears the ground without being as short as the correct trottoir skirt is certainly the most popular. Bodices show long shoulder lines which droop, this effect being enhanced by a falling berthe or flounce, as in the pretty bodice shown in my second sketch. This design is one equally suited to a blouse as it is. loose in style and could be worn over a tight-fitting silk slip. It also offers a suggestion for the making up of the bodice material which is always supplied with the ready-made skirts and which sometimes taxes one's ingenuity as to the prettiest style of a dainty bodice. The sleeves in this instance are loose but are caught into deep cuffs trimmed with lines of insertion which also adorn the yoke and flounce. There, are many pretty styles of making bodices, but it is noticeable that there are very few of the close-fitting order. They are all loose aud unlined, designed to be worn over a tight lining of silk for preference but sateen of a good quality should the silk be too costly. We

PIQUE COSTUME.

still cling also to the bodice which fastens down the back for they seem better suited to the exigencies of fashion even though they do offer considerable difficulties to those of us who do not possess a maid. My last sketch shows a pretty coat and skirt for a little girl. The material used is pique, although the same style could be expressed in a thin woollen material or a nice washing linen. The skirt is pleated, while the saque coat is enhanced by a pretty muslin collar embroidered at the outer edge. Three large ornamental buttons form the fastening, and it should always be remembered that it is best to remove these before sending the coat and skirt to the laundry. It is completed by a pretty washing muslin hat trimmed with lace and a little ribbon. Pleated skirts, of course, are very much worn both by children and their seniors but must be carefully treated when they are of washing material to ensure their successful return from a visit to the laundry. For a slender child no style could be more becoming than that illustrated, and a pretty blouse of embroidered muslin or silk may be worn underneath.

yrignr. ) THE TWO MARRIAGES

BY J. MARSHALL MATHER,

Intlior of "Sign of .the Wooden Shoon," "By

Roaring Loom," Sx.

The little village throbbed with excitement, for it was the wedding-day of Mary Fare.. There was a gay display of bunting down the straggling High- street; knots of neighbours stood around their doors, and a.crowd of children scrambled about the palisades that guarded the old chapel from the road. Everybody was on the alert, and everywhere was in holiday attire. Mary Fare was the daughter of the postmistress, and he to whom she was about to !ink her lot was the Rev. Theophilus Brown, who had been for some time stationed as a minister in that section of the great southern circuit of -. He had come as a young man, inexperienced and enthusiastic, and, meeting with Mary, won her heart and plighted his troth. Two years of happiness passed, and then he removed to other spheres of labour, where he saw fresh faces, formed fresh friendships, and lost his heart on other loves. But he . was bound to Mary. He knew this; and he knew that the authorities of his denomination knew it also, and that they would relentlessly force the climax of the obligation to which he had bound himself. So he was true to his word and true to his bond, and returned on this summer morning to be,united in holy matrimony to Mary Fare. Mary was a lovable girl, simple, trustful, happy. She had no profundity of mind, no accomplish- ments, so-called, no artificiality of manners. She had been born in nature's school, and she was nature's child. She knew nothing of modern literature, nothing of Beethoven and Chopin. Her reading was restricted to simple lays, and her playing to psalmody and country song. In these she found culture and childlike happiness, and in converse with her about these, he too in times past found culture and happiness also. - But a change had come over the spirit of his life. In his sojourn in towns he had become townish ; he had read great books, he had associated with thinkers, he had come face to face with women of mind. Consequently, the glamour of this early love had vanished,; and she whom he had idealised was no longer the object of his romance, but plain little Mary Fare. But yet he was true to his word; he had pledged his vows, and he would fulfil them; and now he stood beside the altar with Mary's hand held in his own, uttering the solemn words: "To love and to cherish till death us do part, according to God's holy ordinance," Mary the while trembling and blushing, and, in her turn, from the depths of her little thobbing heart, responding in accents as fervid as. they were low. The ceremony drew to a close, the carriages drove away, and the crowd was left to its comments, which were of the usual gossipy and prognostic character. . There were many who said they were well matched, prophesying a life of happiness for both; some declaring that Theophilus had found a treasure,. in Mary, and others that Mary had married welV in securing the hand of Theophilus. There wsre two old village crones, however, who demurred from this general approval, and who, shaking their heads, foretold sorrow, declaring that Mary was marrying out of her rank, and that a simple child like her could be no companion for a "scholard " and a man of great learning. But this minority was only laughed at, while the future depicted was treated with scorn. The honeymoon was spent at a quiet seaside resort, where the blue waves lapped the golden sand in crisping rippler, where bronzed fishermen mended their nets and plied their oars, and where little children waded ankle-deep from morn to dewy eve. It was a scene of loveliness and of peace; and in the new-found joy of Mary the hours passed in happy round. Not that her husband was always communicative, for he read books whose names she could not pronounce, and had brooding fits of meditation, as though vexed with the weighty problems of his age. He was indifferent to the objects that claimed her attention, would answer her childlike questions with monosyllables, and smile somewhat disdainfully at the little things that roused her joy. Shells, star-fish, and the many- coloured floating weed towards 'which she would go out with her whole heart, and over which she would clap her hands 'in simple delight, possessed no charm for him; and while she would steal glances at the pages of some perpular serial, he would strain his eyes over the characters of a Greek Testament, or knit his brows over problems suggested by the psychology of Herbert Spencer. Thus it came to pass that though the sky was sunny, and the sea sparkling, and the shore alive wits its wonders, a feeling of loneliness crepe over Mary that she puzzled her head ta account for and define. One morning, as they were seated on the beach, Mary called her husband's attention to a ship that, with its spread sails, manlike, glided over the waters. "Oh, how beautiful!" she exclaimed, laying her hand upon her husband's arm. "Look, Theophilus Could anything be prettier ? " He raised his weary eyes from the book over which lie was poring, and, languidly looking out from over the horizon, muttered:

"Look at what, my dear ? " "At that shipi" she said. "Well, what is there to look at? A ship is but a thing for the transit of merchandise. There is nothing extraordinary in that," and he returned in silence to the book he was reading. A great cloud blotted out the sun for Mary; the sea lost its lustre, the ship its charm. It wasi no longer a white bird gliding over the waters ,• it was a thing for transit of merchandise, a hull, a log moving from port to port. Oh, foolish Theophilus, to put out the poetry of thy little wife's heart, to try and stifle with thy metaphysics the native genius of her soul. Little knowest thou the sorrow thou entailest both on thyself and on her! _ _ _

Mary relapsed into silence, and, for the first time, felt the awful distance there was between her and the man whom she had pledged her word to love, to cherish, and to obey. There, by the music of the sea, which to her was now a dirge, and beneath the light of the sun which to her no longer shone in glory, there came the awful suggestion that she was not the fit partner for his life, and that, as he was beyond her in cleverness, so he was beyond her in sympathy and in love. She—a little country girl, schooled in a village—should never have been so foolish as to suppose she 'could be the companion and helpmeet of one so great and learn. d But she dared follow her thoughts no further; and, bursting inth tears, she wept like a child. But Theophilus was too busy with his book to notice her, and she wept. alone. And so the time passed ; day followed day, Theophilns at his hooks—a poor accompaniment for a honeymoon--and Mary with her foreboding and tears. Neither of them were sorry when the tie for departure arrived, and when the train whirled them away to the distant city which was to be their home. At first they passed through waving fields of corn, spotted here and there with red-tiled farm- steads ; then they came to woodlands, and the stately homes of the rich, their grey stone facades lighted up with the beams of the sun ; then they swept through villages with their quaint, irregular streets, and hoary churches amid the clustering elms. All this was a panorama of delight to Mary who ran from side to side of the carriage that sh. might take in both views of the charming districe through which she was passing. But her excitet rri,mt and flutter were a source of annoyance to her. husband, who the while was greedily hugging his Greek Testament. Gradually the scene changed. Fields gave place to brick crofts, and waving corn to stretches of clay. Instead of the village there came the town. and in place of the spire the chimney vomiting its smoke. The sun took a mor3 hazy •aspect, the white clouds floating over th distant blue lost their former luminousness, and a vapour rose from the earth, no longer sweet and fresh, but pestilential and depressing.. Never before had Mary beheld such gloom in sky or on earth, and, turning to Theophilus, she said: "W here are we ?"

His reply was: "At home." Home! Mary's heart sank within her; and as the cab rumbled over the stony streets it sank deeper and deeper. Street, after street, narrow and gloomy, amid the roar of traffic and the babel of tongues, and then aOsudden pause before a red-brick structnre, bare of all ornament and abutting upon the pavement. Home! Within • was gloomier than without. Sinai! rooms, dimly lighted and pervaded with the wretched city smell—a/03e, stilling, mephitic, the walls glaring with cheap papers, the ceilings sooty with an over use of gas ; the furniture shaky. and clamped with iron clasps to prevent• its sudden collapse when brought into too careless use ; the carpets thretuThare, the windows crying out for a coat of paint—was this home ? Mary looked out of the sitting-room window, and the back of other houses met her view. No sun, no sky, no greenery no little flower to gladden her eye, no Sweet stmuuer bird to

delight her ear. But she could have borne all this, and even more, had the light of love gleamed in Theophilus's eye, and the warmth of passion thrilled in his voice. But she looked and li•tened in vain. nurrienly referring her to the servant tor particulars about the household, he passed into his study—the most comfortable room in the house— and shut to the door: for he was married to his books.

And so the days passed on slowly, wearily and sorrowfully, Mary being left almost entirely to her- self to battle as best she could with the gloom with- out and loneliness within. As a matter of course she had the usual visitors, the dames of the church, many of whom were patronising and inquisitive, ammg these being one of uncertain age who aped a masculine dress and gait, and whose talk was about present-day quections and the problems of the age. Of this lady she saw much, until her presence became a bore. Indeed, if the truth must be told, gentle Mary began to grow jealous of her. And yet she blamed herself for being so foolish, for she knew her husband was as true as steel. But this lady would direct her conversations to Theophilus, read the books which he read, talked about the subjects uppermost in his mind, and sympathised with him in his aims. Mary would sit for hours a silent listener. Now and again she essayed to speak ; only, however, to vex Theophilus and to betray her ignorance. Then she would go into her own room and weep and blame herself for not being clever, and wonder how she 'could fit herself to talk with him whom she loved as this learned lady talked. At last• she determined that she would ask Theophilus to teach her to do so. It was one Sunday evening, and she and Miss Clare—for such was the lady's name.—and Theophilus were seated together in the study. The conversation turned on the morning's discourse, which had been highly philosophic, and duly appreciated by the mind of Miss Clare. Together, she and the preacher went over the arguments in their due order, the lady emphasising their strength and criticising what she deemed to be their weakness ; he, the while, nodding assent and appreciatively listening to all that she had to say. Every criticism front this keen-minded woman and every apprecia- tive • look and word on the part of Theophilus pierced poor Mary's bosom like so many daggers. What had she done to be thus discounted in her own home? Why could not she be his com- panion in thought as well as his associate in the household? Was she to be thus belittled? No wonder he had few words for,her and fewer atten- tions. And as the shadows deepened, darker shadows fell across her sad mind, and she sat in the gloom a sad, a disspirited, and a broken-hearted girl. Thus the minutes passed, and the con- versation continued, but Mary was no longer a listener.

Suddenly she awoke as with a start. Had she heard aright? "To-morrow evening at eight at Centre-Square." What was to-morrow evening at eighlr. at Castre-square ? Was it a meeting? And, if so, who was to meet, and for what purpose? Was Theophilus to meet Miss Clara? Surely it must be so. Would he take her with him ? She would ask him when they were alone, and if he refused she would follow him and so set her tortured mind at rest. But when they were alone the devil of pride entered into her and she remained silent, nursing the bitter feelings of imagined wrong. She, the woman he had vowed to love and to cherish, to whom in days gone by he had spoken tender words, to be set aside for this prig who with her clever talk had withdrawn her husband's atten- tions and affections. No, she.would stand her own ground, she would go her own way, she would be self-sufficient. But she was not strong gmough for her resolve. The meeting in Centre-square vexed her. What did it mean? What was involved in it? She would know, and that for herself; she would know independently of Theophilus. And know she did. And while little came of the knowledge she gained, it brought about the climax of her life.

The whole of that night she spent in restless tossings and heart tortures, and she went about her work on the following day as in a dream. Every- thing was misplaced or lost, breakages were of frequent occurrence, the dinner was badly cooked. Theophilus was in the sulks, and Mary was con- sumed with the fires of jealousy. Time travelled with terrible tedium, and more than once she thought the fingers on the clock had stopped. No, they travelled, but how slowly! Again and again it was upon the tip of her tongue to ask Theophilus as to the meeting. But what if he repulsed her ? What if he told her to heed her own ways? Then her better nature whispered, assuring her that there could be nothing wrong. Theophilus with all his failings was a gentleman, honourable and true; and Miss Clare, with all her book-learning, was a lady and above suspicion. So spake her better mind, her woman's mind. Then the demon of jealousy reasserted itself. It lied to her, and she listened to its lies; she became its thrall, its abject slave. As the day wore on she matured her plans. She would disguise herself as best she could and creep under the shadows of evening to the place of meeting, if possible to see with her own eyes and hear with her own ears. Her husband left home early in the afternoon, and as she watched his form disappear down the street she seemed to see in lines of fire that hateful Miss Clare, who had squeezed the poisoned drop into her cup of life. He was going to her! He would take tea with her! He would accompany her to the meeting But she would follow, she would see all and she would know all, and if needs be she would denounce them; and then she would go back to her village home, to her mother's side, to her roses, and to her geraniums, where she was queen, where she had been loved. She would get away from the horrid town and its smoke, from the horrid street with its gloom, and from horrid women who talked about books she had never heard of, and who appealed with their seductive learning to her husband's mind. So dressing herself a half-past seven she passed out into the street to find her way to Centre-square. It was an evening late in the autumn, and dark clouds were scudding across the sky. Showers of rain fell at intervals and the wind moaned in fitful gusts. Mary knew little of the town, and had no idea as to the whereabouts of Centre-square. She dreaded to make inquiries lest she should rouse suspicion, imagining in her foolish terror that she was known to all, and that her errand was known also. Consequently, in a little while she was lost in a maze of streets, and bewildered with the throng of passers-by. She paused before a large public building; then, supposing that every eye was upon her, she rushed wildly down a hack street, scantily lighted and badly paved. Fear now seized her mind as she thought of the pitfalls and snares of large cities, when suddenly she met a policeman, and summoning courage she asked him to direct her to Centre-square. To her great relief she found that she was within a stone s throw of her destination, and in another mintrie she stood on the threshold of a large paved space across which people were hurrying in a continuous stream. Following in their wake she was soon brought to the front of a public building into which she entered with a rush, being carried by sheer force up a narrow and badly-lighted staircase. In another moment she found herself in a gallery of a huge building looking down over a mass of heads upon an area equally crowded below, at the end of which stood a platform whereon among other ladies and gentlemen were her husband and Miss Clare. Among all those hundreds of faces Mary saw but two. She was oblivious to all else, unconscious that her husband was speaking and that his words were being received by a storm of derision. She felt that she intpt press her way to the front and stand between him and the woman at his side. Brit well for her that it was in vain. The crowd wedged her in • to move was impossible. "Fellow working-men," said Theophilns. But his greeting was received with scornful cheers. "We are here to fight your battle by fighting your bitterestour deadliest foe—strong drink." speaker's voice was drowned by hooting and reb yh e e yells. iyylf a nest of vipers was hidden in et your house-you would thank the man who scotched them for you. We are here to scotch the snake by seeking to prohibit the licence." This utterance was received with clamorous jeers, caterwauling and shouts of "Rob the poor man of his beer." What the epeaker might have proceeded to say we know not, for at this xuncture the-lights were turned out and a general panic ensued. The scene that followed baffles description. Strong men fought one against :mother for the exit, and women fell fainting in the rush. Curses and tries mingled one with the other, and the selfishness of the beast overcame all sense of sacrifice or self- control. Mary felt herself carried towards the stair- case amid a mass of surging, wrastling humanity, and losing consciousness she was torn amid twisted and mangled until flung on the pavement below, where in a little while she was rescued by the hands of strong deliverers and borne off to the infirmary in a distant street.. It-was late when Iheophilus reached his home, breathless, haggard and faint frcrn the risks he had ran and the excitement he had suffered. His first glance was for his wife, and noting her absence he summoned the servant to ask as to her whereabouts. When informed that she had been out since half..

past seven his lite took a still more haggard look, aroi he was seized as with sudden fear. "Been out since hiclf-past seven," he said, partly to himseif and partly to the maid. "Did she leave no message? Did she not say where she was going?" And when assured in the negative he .began to pace the room like a man demented. He looked at his watch and saw thnt If. Wag hal f- past twelve. What should he do? Where should he go? He dared not summon the police; he knew how in vain it was to search in a town so large without a clue. He could conjecture no cause for her disappearance, he could locate no spot as to her whereabouts. In a moment the last three months all flashed before him. Had he been kind to Mary, attentive, affectionate? Had he not neglected her for his studies, and had he not found a charm in the companionship of others rather than in her own? Had she left him? Was she wandering homeless and friendless in the dreary streets, or had she-- Awful thought. No, she could not have hurt herself, she could not have ended her life by violence. And yet of late how silent she had been, how sad. Had lie broken her heart? Had he wasted her young affections which had been showered so lavishly spon him ? As these thoughts rushed madly through his mind he was disturbed by a loud summons at the door, which when opened gave admittance to a burly policeman asking for his presence at the Royal Infirmary, presenting at the same time a card which had been taken from a lady's pocket bearing his own name and address. In a moment he was without and hurrying breathlessly to that house of suffering, and dreading the worst that awaited him there. In through the portals he rushed, blindly searching for the Nvard wherein his wife was lying. At last, after many futile efforts, lie was led to her bedside to behold her unconscious and pale, a mangled mass, dragged from the late panic in Centre-square. Dumb and tearless he stood before her, intuitively understanding all. But Mary did not die. The good God answered the prayers of Theophilus and slowly brought her back to strength and health and beauty. On the day prior to Christmas she was removed to his home, and placed with gentle hands upon the couch in that study to which in the past he had been so wedded. As he knelt by her side and watched the fire-light play upon her pale features his memory went back to the old and forgotten days of his early love, when he first saw her and wooed her. In a moment his conventionalities and his scholasticism fell from him like a disused garment. Books, theories, projects—that had usurped his affections and killed the tender humanities in his being—lost their spell. He was once more the Theophilus of old, full of romance and full of love. Taking up her wasted hand and pressing it to his lips, he whispered: "Mary, my Mary, to love and to cherish till death us do part." And she wound her arm around his neck and drew his face towards hers, receiving the baptism of his love with the tears that fell from his welling eyes, and realising in her heart that they were now for ever one, united by a bond that neither the conven- tionalities of life, nor the theories of science, not the problems of the age could ever put asunder.

WARRINGTON AND THE DRINK TRAFFIC.

Mr. Birchall Deplores Indifference.

Mr. T. BIRCHALL delivered;an interesting:address on the drinking habits of Warrington at a drawing room meeting held at the residence of Mr. and Mrs. William Gill, 20, Winmarleigh-street, on Tuesday afternoon, to aid the funds of the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission and the juvenile work of the Diocesan C.E.T.S. All the religious and social reformers found one great difficulty, said Mr. I3irchall, and that was indifference. They could get people to be earnest about anything almost except what they ought to be earnest about— such things as football, for instance. In that respect people were intensely in earnest, but, notwithstanding this, he said that if they could only open their eyes there were tragedies being enacted round about them which, could they be seen in the mass, would create in them the greatest horror. Mr. Taylor could take them ashe had sometimes taken the speaker, to districts in Warrington where blasphemy was the only language of the people and where babes only lisped in oaths and curses. That was a terrible state of things, but it was not overdrawn, and seeing that such a state of things , existed it ought to rouse them to great earnestness And to try and do something to remedy it. They were getting sadly materialistic. If there was any disease about they '(Tot frightened. They would remember the scare about smallpox ten years ago, when many lives were sacrificed, the authorities never being left in peace until they had adopted great precautionary I measures to prevent a recurrence of the disease. We I spent thousands of pounds and built a sanatorium, but I they had amongst them a moral infection more danger- ous even than smallpox. But there were no startling letters to the newspapers about it. During the last year or two there had been a feeling in Warrington that corrupt influences were creeping into the municipal life of the borough, and that some of their town councillors were personally profiting in a way they ought not to profit. They knew what intense excitement had been aroused, that public meetings had been held, fierce speeches delivered, startling newspAper articles printad, and that a commission was appointed to investigate the whole of the circumstances. For eight months the Commissioners had been engaged in the work. They had examined and discussed hundreds of documents and many witnesses, and had spent he had no doubt, a good deal of money. And now the report was being anxiously awaited. Yet they had „a state of things existing in their midst that if their feelings were not deadened and their consciences blunted would urge them withal! their might to demand an inquiry into the cause and strive for a-remedy. They were very anxious about material things, and yet he did not hesitate to say that there was an army, a perfect army, of evil influence abroad, far more dangerous than smallpox or fever, and tending to produce those very evils of which they were so much afraid. (Hear, hear and applause.) Mr. Taylor could tell them a good many things about the police court but the cases that came before the magistrates were only samples of what was going on outside, and, he was afraid, a very small number of samples. Last year he took the trouble Ito tabulate his experience of the Police Court, but it was not interesting reading when he came to look at it, as he found that out of so-ne 850 cases in which he assisted to adjudicate at least two thirds were caused by this terrible drunkenness of which they had so much to complain. Mr. Taylor told them only a week ago that he had visited for the purpose of giving relief in some districts where the people were said to be out of work, but what did he find? Five or six persons all of them drunk together— people who had no means of living, but still they could get this drink. That was a terrible state of things,but it did not represent the whole evil. The great majority of drunkards did not fall into the hands of the police, and during his 30 years' experiences as a manufacturing manager, having had 500 or 600 men to look after, he knew many great drunkards as never came into a police court, but they managed somehow I or other to keep out of it. He mentioned that fact to show how great the evil was. They wanted to have al tangible result for the money they spent. What was the cost to Warrington on intoxicants ?—R300,000 a year! There was not one person in Warrington but would say that at least half of that amount might he spared for other purposes with advan- tage. (Hear, hear, and applause.) With even £100,600 turned into the coffers of the trades- men of the town what a difference it would make ! Would to God they could get an inquiry to sit for eight days, never mind eight months, to find some remedy for this horrible and growing evil ! There was only one efficient way of dealing with the matter, and the society was an eminent instance of it, namely, by personal sympathy am' by going amongst the people. That was a most effectual way of dealing with them. Their preaching and lecturing scarcely touched the fringe, and the places of worship were too respectable and rather repelled than attracted. A personal appeal was seldom unsuccessful. This society, he said, showed personal sympathy towards the people who came under its care, and the greater the need the greater the society was ready to help The whole New Testament was an illustration of the power of that personal sympathy culminating especially in the teaching of C hrist Himself. Forth. selfsatisfled Scribe and Pharisee he had words of scathing rebuke, but the poor moral or physical wrecks of humanity called forth the exercise of his deepest pity and coin- passion. The calling him," Lord, Lord," was offensive to him unless they were doers of the things he com- manded and the Apostolic description is that "He went about doing good. The object of this association was to send out a number of good Samaritans to succour and help the sick and wounded wrecks of humanity which were ever with us. To them it was no question of sect, creed or nationality. The lower the victim had sunk the more desirous were they to lift him up. The only claim they recognised was his uraentneed.They pleaded in the spirit of brotherly love with the drunkard to leave that which had blighted his life and desolated his home. He (the speaker) knew from long personal experience how valuable wits.the service they rendered in the Police Courts and not infrequently when the terrors of law had failed to teach some foolish ones wisdom the genial kindness of their friend Mr. Taylor had had the desired effect. They could not all do this kind of real Christian service but they could help and encoer- ae those who were engaged in it, and he hoped the effect of that meetirbe7 would be to stimulate Mr. Hirst and his assistants to carry on their Christ-like work with greater zeal and with a success worthy of their efforts. Mr. HENRY TAYLOR, Police Court Missionary at Warrington, said he was extremely obliged to their host I and hostess for the privilege they had extended to them I with regard to the holding of the meeting. Those I Present, he added, could form no adequate idea of the number of wretched homes in the town.

LHASSA, THE SACRED CITY OF TIBET.

THE TIBETAN CAMPAIGN.

The British mission to Tibet has again met -With unexpected opposition, and though in itself not very serious, it delays 'the final 'settlement of things. After the great rout at Caru, where the Tibetans lost -so heavily, the mission was allowed to reach Gyangtse without further opposition, and a time of rest and quiet followed, during which the "Tibetans freely went in and out of our 'camp trading and driving a deal with 'With unusual acuteness. Then last week of 'of a sudden signs of hostility began once 'More to show themselves, and a scogting 4column on the Lhassa road was all but ;ambushed. This recrudescence of hostilities 'was followedratlthe end of the week by a -determined attack on the British camp by a :force of eight hundred men, who were, how- 'ever, easily driven off with the loss of two hundred and fifty killed and wounded to our -casualties of two Sepoys wounded. This -attack shows that the apparent submission of the Dalai Lama was after all only a feint. and It is now almost certain that the expedition will have to go all the way to the secret 'capital. The attack was led by the Lhassa 'General, and the Dalai Lama's clerk was with Gyangtse stands at the junction of the • hassa and Shigatse roads, and it came from the latter direction doubtless with the hope of ;drawing the British force away from the more 'lmportant objectives' but it is hardly likely to have this effect. It is obvious that the 13alai Lama does not yet appreciate the ;situation, and will probably have to be read sharp lesson at Lhassa itself. There is a 'great fascination about Lhassa, the secret capital of Tibet, which no living white man has ever seen, despite many vain attempts on The part of the adventurous. The Forbidden 'City, as it is called, is the headquarters of the Dalai Lama and lies some two hundred zniles, point to point, from the nearest -tritish territory. The sacred city, which is the headquarters of Bhuddism, is in itself mot a very large town, consisting only of .nine twenty thousand inhabitants, and, ex- Tept for present reasons of policy and Tibet's :geographical relation to our Indian frontier, it is not of very great importance. It is Probably the most priest-ridden spot on the -•globe, and it is hedged round with the most marvellous collection of superstitions. It lies in the centre of a circle of high moun- -tains, forming a ring about four miles in -diameter, in which Lhassa is built. Before it can be -reached, these great mountains have to be scaled, and they have hitherto been so zealously guarded as to circumvent ruses of the European explorer. The city -11.,es in the centre of this intramontane X'ain and consists of red-roofed houses, Nv'urrounded by gardens, and streets with featater flowing through them. But the great „h, ure of the forbidden City is the vast rock ch rises sheer in its midst on which *stands the Bhuddist "holy of holies," the Dalai Monastery, and the Potala Palace of the 'Grand Lama. On three sides are precipices, -and it is unapproachable, rising abruptly everal -hundred feet; and on the fourth it is very steep but accessible by road. The "Monastery is a great building with tier upon tier of windows, and beneath it stands the -sacred Pillar of Lhassa, the font of; all the superstitions. Now it looks as though for The _ first time Lhassa will be entered by white men; and we at home may look forward to a wealth of interesting detail with regard to this extraordinarily fascinating .orner of the world which has baffled so trna,ny explorers hitherto.

PRINCE AND PRINCESS OF WALES AT BRADFORD. The visit of the Prince and Princess of Wales to Bradford to open the exhibition of -dress materials in the Cartwright Hall was an event which had been looked forward to liOr many months by the good folk of the vsorstedopolis, and that their Royal High- nesses received a warm welcome is now almost history. It is noteworthythat the success achieved by Bradfora has gone hand in hand with the local develop- ment of techni- cal education. So beautiful. are the dress fabrics now manufac- tured in Brad- ford that a for- eign trade has opened up which promises to be highly lucrative. For-

tunately, . t h.c

citi7ens- of this b thriving metropolis are not disposed to haggle -aout halfpence when the point at issue is t.he. possession of dress materials; and when -it is a question of competing with the finest °°rns on the Continent, the work cannot be One cheaply. In these days when we hear SO much of the decadence of British mann- Jacturers, it is gratifying to find English en- terprise opening up altogether . into spirited °lllPetition with Continental monopolists. —uring their visit north their Royal High- nesses stayed with Lord and Lady Harewood 'at Harewood House, near Leeds, which iA con- Idered one of the most charming residences 411, li,ngland. The Prince and Princess .re- turned-to town on Friday last after paying visit to the famous Lister Mills at Man- Bradford.

face that his literary output embraces over two hundred books and three hundred and fifty collections of poetical works! In style, he was a strange combination of realism and idealism. All his work was marked by an exuberance of imagination and a weird sense of humour, and in places it rose to the level of true genius and inspiration. At the age of nine he published two poems, and at seventeen he wrote a tragedy called "The Infant Jew," which fifty years later was exhumed and republished. After that he plunged into the vortex of things political in his native country and became an ardent revolutionist, .and by twenty-one he was editing a Liberal paper which incited his fellow-countrymen to revolt, with the result that he was arrested and condemned to death, from which he only escaped by going into hiding. When Hungary had won its Parliament, he again blossomed out into journalism, editing, writing and illustrating a paper, which ultimately got him a year in irons for its political cartoons. After that he settled down to literature proper and gained honour in his own country despite the vicissitudes of his early career. His novels have been translated into many languages and are well-known in this country, especially "The Green Book," and "Black Diamonds." In his old age he was granted many honours by his admiring countrymen— degrees, pensions, prest.i..ts and rewards of all sorts, by which they tried to make amends for the cavalier treatment of his early days, when his country all but lost him by its own decree.•

PARLIAMENT AND THE TOBACCO TAX

Matters in the House of Commons during the past week were unexpeciedly lively in connection with the discussion on. the Budget resolution, and whereas it should have been purely formal, it was made distinctly other- wise by Mr. Reginald M'Kenna, the member for North Mon- mouth, by some caustic corn- ments upon the tobacco tax. The hon. mem- ber asked the pointed ques- tion as to whether or no the secret of the Budget was known to any- one in the to- bacco trade be- fore it was an- nounced in the House, and he quoted remark- able change in the volume of tobacco i m - ports in March MR. R. M.KENNA, M.P. as between

stripped an d anstripped tobacco. With this fact for data, Mr. M'Kenna requested to be informed whether the Chancellor of the Exchequer had been " duped " by persons interested in bringing in unstripped- tobacco into bond, and in particular by Mr. Gallaher, of the firm of Messrs. Gallaher, the great manufac- turers of "Irish Roll." He maintained that if this were so, it was only in accordance with the doctrine laid down by Mr. Cham- berlain and accepted by his followers that there should be direct " communication " between the Chancellor of the Exchequer and the Tariff Commission in future. By these remarks it would appear that Mr. Gallaher had been able, through his membership of the Tariff Commission, to influence the Bud- get to his own advantage, and that he had done so in accordance with the principles laid down by the Chancellor of the Exchequer's " father. Mr. Austen Chamberlain resented the accusation of Mr. M'Kenna that he had been communicating with Mr. Gallaher, and quoted as disproof that Mr. Gallaher had since written him criticising the form of tax. The whole matter -rested as to whether Nlr. M'Kenna had made an attack upon the Chancellor's personal honour, and this the member for North Monmouth denied. There were some lively passages-at-arms during the debate, and the Speaker found it necessary to intervene on more than one occasion. The subject of our sketch has been M.P. for North Monmouth since 1895. He was edu- cated at Trinity Hall, Cambridge, and rowed in the 'Varsity eight of 1887.

DE ROUGEMONT AGAIN.

M. Louis de Rougemont is once more in the public eye, recalling five years more ago when he produced his startling series of adventures and tales of unknown lands, which deceived even the British Asso- ciation but were afterwards discredited. True

or not true, his story of a modern Robin- son Crusoe!s life was wonderfully interesting and most cleverly told. When at last the world grew incredu- -lou s, M. de Rougemont. withdrew from it into rustic se- clusion, settling in the little Bed; fordshire village of Flitwick,. where he took a pretty little cot- tage. and has, according t o rumour, been writing more

marvellous and thrilling adventures. He has also travelled widely, like Ulysses, seeing many men and countries. Now he is returning to pubic life in an entirely new and 'original capacity. It is as nothing more nor less than a turtle- rider 1 He is surprised that a statement of his to the effect that he could ride a turtle excited so much contradiction, and now on Monday next, by the courtesy of Mr. Moss, he will proceed to prove his ppint, mouating his turtle at the top of the chute, riding it down the chute into the arena at the Hippo- drome, and there manceuvring his extra- ordinary mount. If unhorsed (or, to be more accurate, if the rider turns turtle), M. de Rougemont will forfeit a hundred paunds. The ignorant may think the turtle a plea- sant mount with its wide, hard back; but not only does it afford a bad knee-grip, but the creature in question has an uncom- fortable knack of diving, porpoise-fashion, several times a minute, and often " sounds " or goes right to the bottom; this, however, M. de Rougemont is confident that he cart prevent, partly by skill and partly by will power. This novel exhibition of "man v. beast," is sure to draw a crowded house.

CONSTIPATION AND HEADACHE. Bile Beans Banish Both.

Constipation is the cause of many of the ailments from which mankind suffers. In most cases it is due to defective liver action, and as Bile Beans act directly on the liver, causinc, it to secrete Nature's own aparient— the bile—they end constipation in the surest way. Mrs. Jones, of Green Street, West Bromwi3h, says: —" All my life I have been a sufferer from constipation In consequence of this, I suffered severely from head- ache. My appetite left me, and no matter how tempt- ing the fond which was placed before me,l had no relish for it. When I went to bed I could not obtain any re- freshing sleep, yet during the day I always felt drowsy and tired. I could not do my work properly, and was very much depressed. "I took various kinds of medicines, but none did me the slightest good. One day I read in the paper about someone who had been cured of similar complaints to mine by Chas. Forde's Bile Beans. "I determined to try them and sent for a box. At the time I was in dreadful pain, and the first few doses of the Beans gave me some relief. I continued with their use and gradually both the headache and constipation loft me. I can now eat and digest food, and I awaken in the morning refreshed after having had a goodnight's rest

_ "You are at liberty to use this information in any way you like, for I think that people should be made aware - of the splendid qualities of Bile Beans." '3d. per box (2s. 9d. box con- tains 3 times is. lid. size), or post free on receipt of prices from the Bile Bean Co., RedCross-street,London, E.C. Bile Beans are the finest family medicine, and a certain cure for all stomach and liver troubles. Avoid substitutes.

CORPORATION AND FAIR CONTRACTS.

Uioists Not Satisfiel.

THE STOURBRIDGE DEPUTATION.

The recent acceptance by the W rrington Town Council of the tender of Messrs. Thomas Rhodes and Son, of Stourbridge, for the supply of galvanised pails and ashbins was again under discussion at the meeting of the Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday evening. Mr. S. Webb, the general secretary of the Amalgamated Union of Iron Plate Workers, was in attendance and gave a lengthy address. The CEIAIRMA.N£Mr.J. Podmore) said the matter had created great public interest and the opinion was ex- pressed in some quarters that their Labour Councillor had received a check. That night they hoped to be able to let the public know how the matter stood, not from the point of view of the employing classes--whose representatives were f.,0 . strong on the Council—but from the trade union standpoint. The SECRETIRY (Councillor Stott) read a letter from Mr. Webb in which, he explained - the purpose of his visit. The Executive Committee of the Union had had the Warrington contract under consideration. They realised that the deputation from Warrington was severely handicapped by the workmen refusing to give the evidence necessary to enable the deputation to arrive at a satisfactory conclusion as to what were the actual wares paid for the making of the goods. The oommittee also feared that, having to rely upon the statements of the employer, the deputation would return to Warrington with the impression that no violation of the Council's standing orders was taking place; and that, therefore, the agitation created by Councillor Stott was altogether unnecessary and uncalled for. Consequently it was thought that Councillor Stott's honour was likely to be impeached by his fellow towasinen. It.was therefore desired that Mr. Webb should address the Trades Council and, by a simple statement of real facts, demonstrate that there was ample justification for the course Councillor Stott had taken.

Councillor ST3TT then went fully into the history of the contract. Incidentally he remarked that they had had on many occasions to co-nplain of the action of the Town Council in accepting the lowest tender without making the least inquiry as to the condi- tions of labour obtaiaing in the works concerned. He proceeded to explain how, in conseatience of Messrs. Rhocres' statement by letter to the Council that they paid the recognized rate of wages for the district and that the men were working the recognized number of hours per week, he and Mr. Warburton (the treasurer) visited the Lye district and saw two men who were employed by the firm. These mea told them that the rate of wages would average about 265. per week. Thc, workmen were, they stated , paid from 21s. to 225. up to about 305., and it was their opinion that not more than one man was paid as high as 30s. An employee had to be a very good man indeed to receive 28s. Councillor Stott then touched upon the Sanitary Works Committee' decision to send a deputation to Stourbridge,consisting of Councillor John Evans and the DeputyeTown Clerk. Daring the week,Councillor Tinnion,whilst on a visit to the Birmingham Exchange was accidentally, they were informed, introduced to Mr. Rhodes. He explained the details of what had transpired in reeard to his (Coun- cillor Stott's) objection, and Mr. Rhodes expressed a desire that he (Councillor Stott) should be included in the deputation.He received a letter from thelrown Clerk asking him to accompany the deputation, and he accep- ted the invitation. Mr. Rhodes met them very fairly and was very plausible. He immediately began to reply to his (Councillor Stott's) statements in regard to the dismissal of a number of men. Tease men, it was stated, were originally paid a day wage at the rate of 24s per week, but they were not earning more than 15s. Mr. Rhodes said there would have to be a change so he dismissed the men, who vere eventually re-engaged by a workman as sub-contractor on the piece-work system—many of them at a reduction of 2s. per week. The men then offered to do the work for the firm at the same piece- work rate that the sub-contractor was receiving, so after some weeks the sub-contractor was dispensed with. The men were now employed by the firm on piece-work and Mr. Rhodes said they were earning—and were perfectly satisfied with it—from 325. to 355., instead of being able to obtain a-maximum of 24s.bu1 only receiving 15s. under the old system. He (Ur. Scott) had been informed many times that 6d. per hour for 60 hours per week was thelrecognised rate for the district. Mr. Rhodes denied that there was a reeognized rate, but said he paid the best wages in the district and had the best workmen Eventually he acknowledged that he did not know what other firms were paying. The workmen he (Mr. Stott) interviewed would not answer any question except to keep repeating that they were satisfied. He sympathised with them, because he fully believed that had any man given information that would have led to the contract being withheld from the firm he would have been dis- missed. The condition of trade in the district was such that they would have great difficulty in obtaining em- ployment elsewhere. Mr. Rhodes took the deputation to the part of the works where the men who did the class of work the Corporation required were employed, and he told them that he paid these men from 245. to 325. One employee who, Mr. Rhodes confessed, was pre- judiced in the firm's favour, said he was receiving 31s. and that three other men in the shop were earning that amount. He said rhea were receiving as little as 18s. but afterwards corrected himself and said only one young man who had been drafted from anther department was getting that sum. Another man, who appeared to have a grievance against the society, merely stated that "he would be very sorry if he could not earn more than 305." The position was that Mr. Rhodes said there was no standard rate of 305., and that he paid his best men from 245. to 324. The Corporation standing orders said that all persons employed on the works must be paid the recognized rate for the district. He had received a statement signed by twelve men working in the district who said they were paid 6d. per hour. He did not feel he had had a ".knock down." He felt he had done his duty and the action of the Coubcil would not prevent him taking the same steps in future if he considered he was justified in doing so. Mr. S. WEBB characterised the matter as one of para- mount importance to trade unionists and to the rate: payers of Warrington. He expressed the sincere grati- tude of his society to Councillor Stott for his invalu- able services. If they had a friend like Councillor Stott on every town and city council the matter of municipal contracts would sp sedily be put on a differ- ent footing. They hoped Warrington workingmen would support Mr. Stott by again electing him when the time came. He (Mr. Webb) was satisfied that when his worth came to be realized in the Council Chamber—as it must be sooner or later—it would not be very long before he was called upon to occupy the mayoral chair. (Hear, hear and laughter.) Dealing with the matter at issue, Mr. Webb said that by the standard rate he understood the rate which by common consent and continued practice had become established. In 1889 there was a great agitation in the district affected by the contract I concerning the wages paid to the inn plate workers. The workmen then joined almost in a body, "The Knights of Labour" and a list of prices was drawn up and submitted to the whole of the em loyers of the Lye and Stourbridge districts. after some revision the prices were agreed to by workmen and employers, and came into operation in 1890. Those prices were based on piecework rates—which were the only rates the trade union recognized—andsettled once and for all the question of wages for the making of galvanised holloware. The question of the day rate was then considered, because it was customary in some factories for the employer to engage men to make odd- meats. It was agreed that Gd. per hour or 30s. per week should be the rate. The 1890 list for piece-work had remained in operation until the present time, with the exception that it was now subject to a 10 per cent discount. The honour- able understanding arrived at with reference to 6d. per hour for general workmen had remained nnaltered and the rate was recognized by the great majority of employers in the district when any day work had to be done. The trade union rate was the piece-work charge. The standard rate of the district for the class of work the Corporation required was 6d. per hour, because Messrs. Rhodes some years ago, when the union was not strong enough to resist it and when the trade organiza- tion was not in a position to dictate the terms, revolu- tionised their factory by abolishing the piece rate and establishing throughout the works a system of day rats with the 'result that there naturally followed a sub- division of labour. In order that they might appear to be abiding by the understanding arrived at in 1890 they paid a few of their hands the recognised rate of 30s, but the others had to come down and down to 18s. He could quite understand that on the common work they would not pay 6d an hour,but the goods made for the Warrington Corporation required skilled work- men. Mr. Rhodes admitted that he paid. these best men 245. to 325. per week. In 1893 a strenuous effort was made on the part of employers and workmen in the district to eetablish a wages board in order to regulate the wages of the workpeople and to determine the selling prices of the goods. Mr. Rhodes desired to stand outside the scope of the board, but eventually promised to attend on the one condition that they would not interfere with his system of working. He had intro- duced the innovation of day work evidently with a desire to cheapen the cost of production, and if attend- ihg the board meeting meant that he should go back to piece rates he was not prepared to attend. Last year the list of prices was revised, and they did not overlook the matter of day work, seeing that one or two firms had followed in 'Messrs. Rhodes's footsteps. Therefore they placed on the list the minimum day rate=-6d. per hour—although they met with strenuous opposition from the Birmingham district because they rightly said it was not enough. Whether a shop was a trade union shop or not, the same rate of wages obtained according to the list. He was bound to declare emphatically that Messrs. Rhodes did not pay the recognized rate of the district with but one or two exceptions. They must consider that the firm employed 40 or 50 men, and when only one or two were receiving the standard rate, they could not say Messrs. Rhodes were paying the recognized rate for the district. In a footnote to their letter the firm said they bought wire fro:u a Warrington works. It should be borne in mind that there was not a particle of wire

in the articles required by the Corporation. As a trade I union they had no spleen at all against Messrs. Rhodes. They had not taken up the matter in a vindictive spirit. If Messrs. Rhodes would pay the recognized rate for the district, although it be the day rate—of which they did not approve—they were prepared to raise no objection, but would wish them well in the contract they had in hand. They had some wise aldermen on the Council who objected to a lecture on trade unionism, although that was a part of their education which had been grossly neglected, (Laughter). He would like them Ito realize that while the trade union rate would be 10s. per dozen, at which a man could earn comfortably .£2 a week, the trade unionists were perfectly satisfied to let Messrs. Rhodes have the contract if they paid the standard day rate of 30s. Mr Webb gave information AS to the strength of the union which he said, had 500 members in the midlands. He contended that there was ample justification for thel course taken by Councillor Stott, and that it was in harmony with trade union principles. He had been taking up a matter of the oppressed and was desirous that the Corporation should be model employers and ' set an example to others by paying a satisfactory rate of wages. (Applause.) Mr. BUCKLEr said that after reading the report of the Town Council debate he came to the conclusion that the Trades Council had no case. He considered Councillor Stott's defence was a poor one, and had he (Mr. Buckley) been on the Town Council he would not have feltln a position to second the amendment. Having heard Mr. Webb's statement he considered they had a strong case; and he moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Webb. Councillor STOTT said Alderman Fairclough had ex- pressed the opinion that seeing that he (Councillor Stott) had misled the Council he ought to pay the expenses of the deputation. Alderman Fairclough never objected to the expenditure of thousands of pounds of ratepayers' money in looking after his interests and the interests of other manufacturers on the river. He (Councillor Stott) did not suggest the deputation; neither did he ask to be included in it. By going to Stourbridge he lost a day's wages. The CHAIRMA.s thought there was not the slightest doubt that Councillor Stott was justified in the stand he had taken. He believed if the town were canvassed the ratepayers would say they were willing to see people well paid for their labour. He, as a ratepayer, objected to be a participant in any sweating. He objected to men in the Town Council who stood on public plat- forms, attended P.S.A. meetings, and talked about justice and brotherhood, and then could not support an action which would bring about brotherhood and justice to a class of workers in the Lye district. He was per- fectly ashamed of some men on the Council that they were not prepared to stand by Coun- cillor Stott's side and second the amendment. ' He hoped Councillor Stott's hands would be strengthenedi by the return of other labour representa- tives n order that theybould teach the men who often looked down upon workingpeople as dirt beneath their feet that they were not all the population of the coun- try, and that there was someone of equal importance to themselves who wanted to live in a proper mannei. Referring to Councillor Maginn's motion in regard to tenders, the speaker said the invariable acceptance of the lowest contract would mean sweated labour and they as trades unionists must oppose the proposition The vote of thanks was heartily accorded. On the motion of Mr. Jolley the secretary was in- structed to write every Alderman and Councillor asking him to oppose Councillor Maginn's motion at the next Council meeting. The motion reads "That, it be an in- struction to each committee of the Corporation, when they have obtained tenders for goods, or for labour re- quired, or both, to accept the lowest tender except only in cases where any person tendering carries on business .within the borough, and in which the committee for special reasons may consider it advisable not to accept the lowest tender, and that in accepting any tender each committee shall require the persons ten- dering to give the names of two responsible sureties who shall guarantee the due execution of the contract. That the standing orders be amended so as to give effect to the above resolution." The SECRETARY said he had circularised the various trades councils and the replies received to date showed that only four borough councils had not a standing order relative to fair contracts. The majority of the councils specially mentioned trade union conditions.
