NEW BAND OF HOPE BUILDINGS.

Councillor Thornton Lays ona of

the Stones.

Before another year is over the Band of Hope and Temperance Union for Lancashire and Cheshire will be housed in a fine new building in Deanszate,Nlanchester, the foundation-stones of which were laid on Saturday, in the presence of a great gathering of temperance workers.

The Lord Mayor and Lady Mlyoress of Mmchester Sir James Hoy, and the Dean of Manchester lent the support of their presence to the proceedings, which were presided over by the Rev. Canon Hicks.

Tne new building-, which will be five storeys in height,is to cost £ls,ooo,and towards this amount about £B,OOO has been already promised. The chairman of the Executive (Councillor El. Thornton, of Warrington) has promised £l,OOO-2500 unconditionally and £5OO if the full sum wanted is raised this year. On the top floor will be a meeting hall to accommodate 500 persons, and the rooms below will be used as offices. The ground floor will contain the publishing department and sev- eral shops, and the bas3ment a restaurant. There will be a room set aptrt for the use of temperance people visiting the city from outtide districts. The first stone was laid by the Lord Mayor, and others by Messrs. A. A. Haworth, H. J. Whitehouse, H. Thornton, T. E. Hallsworth, W. Chandos Wilson, J. Davies (on behalf of the Sons of Temperance), and Master William Hartley Higham (of Accrington), the last named, who is a grandson of Mr. W. P. Hartley (of Aintree) laying what was described as the children's stone.

Before layinga stone Councillor Thornton said that as chairman of the Lancashire and Chenire Band of Hope and Temperance Union, actively associated with its work, and knowing very much of the details of its operations, he could bear emphatic testimony to the need of the building, whose foundation stones they were that day laying. The magnitude of the vrorkings of the Union wa.s surprising. They not only dealt with the local bands of. hope, but in connection with the Union they federated all the band of hope effort of Lancashire, Cheshire and North-West Derbyshire and dealt with the Union as it now stood. There were 81 towns and district unions, comprising 1,777 societies, with a membership of 271,000. He wished to say to the honour and credit of the different com- mittees that there were 7,000 honorary workers. Since the commencement there had been 10,985 lectures given by their agents, attended by 35,839 teachers and 1,206,418 elder scholars, to whom after examination 173,700 certificates of merit had been given. They would see then from these figures what an immense amount of thought and care had to be exercised. They believed that, good as it was to reclaim the unfortunate victims of intemperance, it was much more satisfactory, much less costly, and much surer, to get hold of the child, to train it aright, and to let it know the truth concerning intoxicants. That was the work of the Band of Hope Union. To do that they needed to come into touch with all classes of children's institutions, not only in the Band of Hope, but in the Sunday schools and in the day schools. Their work was supremely educational. It did not stop with the children, but was encouraged and developed by the promotion of conferences among teachers, medical men and others. (Applause.) They both sought to encourage the work and the workers, indeed it was remarkable to notice that the bulk of their work, so necessary and so useful to the community, was done by voluntary workers, for whose help, how- ever, they were bound to engage a number of other workers, both on the premises and outside, as lecturers, &c., &c., who were paid for their labour. That Union was one of the most powerful temperance organizations in the provinces. Certainly there was none that had a greater numerical strength. He questioned if there was one doing better work for the community. They considered—and no doubt the public would agree with them—that they were beginning at the right end. The more their work succeeded, the fewer would be the victims of intemperance. Not only so, but there would be less cost to the community caused through intemper- ance. It was in order to improve that great work they were erecting that building, so as to make the work more efficient, and more productive of good. He wished to press upon them the following point : The amount they were striving to realize was very little indeed, when they considered that for the putting up of the building, and carrying on temperance teaching in the day schools, &c., they were asking for even less than was spent every half-day in intoxicants in the district in which the Union Rorked. All were agreed that intemperance was a great evil, and that it was too late to seek to stop it when men and women had become intemperate and drink slaves. There was undoubted justification and encouragement for the great work 0-ey had in hand, and which he did not hesitate to say did not receive the sympathy and support it deserved. Fov years this great work had been successfully going on in an inconvenient, stuffy. inadequate, and unhealthy centre. The extensive and healthy machinery now at work required and demanded better accommodation, or its workings would be seriously crippled. Solomon had said: "There is a time to gather stones together." They, as labourers, considered that for their work now was the time, and they sought for help, and believed, too, that they would do their best for them on that occasion. (Applause.) If, however, after hearing their explanation and information that day, they felt drawn out to give more than they had with them, they must not be discouraged, as Mr. Wilson, at 3, North-parade, would be very pleased to receive favourable letters any time. The sooner and the more numerous the better. (Laughter and applause.)

At Kettering on Wednesday a boy died from the effects of the injuries resulting from the explosion of nitric acid and mercury, which he had bought at a chemist's shop, and placed in a bottle. He had read in a popular reference book that such a mixture was good for regulating bicycles. A girl hat just been terribly injured at Northampton in exactly similar circumstances.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

WAR. MFAIORIAL AT EARLESTOWN.

A monument to the men from the Newton-in-Maker- field district who volunteered and took part in the South African war is to be erected near Earlestown Town Hall. The design of Messrs. Dring and Man- chester, architects, Earlestown, was selected in open competition, and the tender of Messrs. Stott and Pres- cott, sculptors, St. Helens, for the execution of the monument has been accepted. The memorial will have a masssve granite base and pedestal in various shades of fine-axed and polished Aberdeen granite, and will be surmounted by a life-size figure of a" man in khaki," executed in white Carrara marble. It is expected that. the monument will be completed in six months.

GOOD VEM PLARY.

1.0.G.T. Hope of Earlestown Lodge held the weekly meeting in the new room, Clarke s Assembly room, Bridge-street, on Monday, when the proceedings took the form of a social, presided over by C.T. Bro. J. F. 'Singleton. After a few items of business had been transacted and one new member initiated, refreshments were handed round and the following items were credit- ably rendered :—Concertina solo, Bro. J. Leatherbarrow ; song, "The Echo boy," Sister Mrs. Willis; recitation, Bro. 3. Warburton; song, "Cling to the Cross," Sister J. Hill. A good number of members and one visitor were present and a very pleasant evening was passed. Hearty thanks were tendered to all who had helped to make it successful. WESLEYINS AND THE LICENSING BILL.

At a meeting of the Wesleyan Band of Hope Committee held on Wednesday May 4th the following resolution was unanimously passed : "That this meeting of the Wesleyan Band of Hope Committee expresses its strong disapproval of the new Licensing Bill introduced in Parliament by the Home Secretary on the grounds that it proposes a serious and unwarrantable interference with the discretionary power hitherto exercised by local justices. that it would creates vested interest in licences and would discourage the reduction in their number and that by entrenching the licensed trade it would place a serious hindrance in the path of national temper- ance reform. That copies be sent to the member for this division and local press." _ _ _ _ _

NAVAL APPOINTMENT 'FOR. INTR. FRANK T. ORMAND.

We are pleased to learn that Mr. Frank Thompson Ormand, second son of Mr. Thomas Ormand. J.P., and grandson of Mr. Peter Thompson,of Lowton,has recently been successful in obtaining a cadetship in the Royal Navy. Mr. F. T. Ormand has been appointed to H.M.S. "Prince George" of the Channel Fleet. The appoint- ment dates from the 15th May, 1904, and on the 30th instant he will become a midshipman. Mr. Frank Ormand was educated at the Earlestown Wesleyan school, and gaining a County Council scholarship con- tinued his studies at the Manchester Grammar school. For the past two years he has been studying. for the Navy aboard the "Worcester," and passing the final examination with highest honours has been gazetted to H.M.S. "Prince George." as midshipman.

A Bit'UTAL ASSAULT.

Martin Welsh, collier, residing at B mk-street, Golborne, was on Thursday at Newton-le-Willows Police Court, fined 40s. and costs, or two months' hard labour,in default of paying the fine,for assaulting a little girl named Jane Crawford, residing in the same street, on April 7. The evidence showed that disturbances had taken place between the Crawford and Welsh families, and it was stated that what Welsh could not "take out" of Mrs. Crawford he would "takeout" of her children, and that on the day in question Welsh kicked the girl on the left ankle so severely that she was still under surgical care in consequence of the injuries received. Defendant was wearing pit clogs at the time. The bench characterised the assault as a most cold-blooded and brutal one.

THE EXAMINEE.

MAY 14, 1904.

THE large majority THE LICENSING BILL. for the second read-

ing of the Licensing Bill may be taken to settle the question of corn- pnsation in its broadest aspect. The principle of compensating dispossessed licence-holders out of funds provided by "the trade •' ha q won general approval. Mr. ASQUITH gave it a guarded adhesion on Wednesday evening, but insisted very properly on the need for a time limit. It is not too much to say that the Government got their majority of 157 for the second reading by post- poning this crucial issue of the time limit to the Committee stage. They treated it in the de- date with characteristic vacillation. First the Colonial Secretary pooh-poohed it. Then the Solicitor-General suggested that it should be considered in committee. Poen the Home Seer e- tary declared that it was wholly incompatible with the principle of the Bill. Then on Tuesday the Prime Minister, in answer to a question, denied that the Solicitor-General had expressed any sympathy with the demand for a time limit. And finally, Mr. BALFOUR. on Wednesday, near the close said that he failed to see how the of his speech, advance the cause they all had at heart "—meanink.,,,we presume,the cause of temper- ance, not the cause of the brewers—but that the question was one for Committee, not for the second reading. It would seem from this that Ministers aro not unprepared to yield to pressure, and it remains to be ,seen whether the unionist t3mperance reformers who voted with thorn on Wednesday or the licensed victuallers will press the hardest. This contention that a time limit of compensation would be fatal to Cze principle of the Bill need not be taken seriously. Certainly it carries no weight with some of Mr. BALFOUR'S staunchest supporters, who point out to him that his express desire not to make the license a free- hold can be accomplished only by limiting the period of compensation. This is put very well by the " Pall Mall Gazette," with which journal we do not often find ourselves in agreement.

"It surely amounts to this," says our contem- porary, "that the publican, being compelled by the nature of his business to equip himself and his premises and to employ capital on a scale incom- patible with the terms of a yearly holding, should be given the opportunity of exhausting the value of his outlay before his license is withdrawn. A time limit should be framed necessarily so as to facilitate liquidation of this investment upon which circumstances have compelled him to embark. But, if the law is to recognise his right to a perpetual tenure (with the alternative of compensation), then we are hound to say it seems to confer upon him a right which he has not hitherto in any sensq possessed, and one which is almost indistinguish- able from the 'freehold' which Mr. Balfour repudiates." Our contemporary thus oirpresses the objectiem of Unionist temperance reformers to "conferring on every owner of a licensed house a perpetual vested interest." It is upon this ground that the great battle in Committee will be fought, and if Mr. BALFOUR will not modify his present views, the imposing majority of Wednesday may disappear. The "Pall Mall says that "it rests upon the ques- tion of the time limit whether the Government is to effect a lasting settlement or merely a tempo- rary disposal of the most vexatious of all our social and political problems." We have not much faith in "lasting settlements." The best of them endure brit a little while when they concern some problem that touches the vital interests of the community. It is most unlikely that the machinery devised in this Bill for the administration of the new system will last very long. As Mr. IsQuITH said, the country needs a licensing authority of a much more popular chara3tel than that commending itself to the Government,

IProvided a time limit is introduced, we have little objection to the compensation proposals in the Bill. Without a time limit, however, this measure will set back the clock of temperance pro- gress by a generation. Mr. BALFOUR, who has a perfect genius for getting out of tight corners, announced shortly before the division was taken that:the question of time limit would be postponed for consideration to the committee stage. Only by this means was he able to preserve his majority intact. On this subject we have not the smallest confidence in the Prime Minister and we fully expect when the time comes that he will out- manceuvre the Unionist temperance reformers and manage to drive the Bill through the Houso of Commons minus the time-limit, without which its effect will be disastrous.

It is a pity that Mr. A. H. Crosfield had not a larger audient:e than when he addressed a meeting of the joint committees of the Bewsey and Orford Wards on Saturday evening, for his speech was worthy of a great occasion. With the assistance of our columns Mr. Crosfield will, however, get the wider hearing he deserved. Generally speaking he reviewed the present political situation, and in a manner which must have been both instructive and helpful to his audience. Free Trade, Educa- tion, Trade Union rights, and Chinese Labour, were successively reviewed in such expressive and convincing language than the ward workers must have felt that if ever they were fighting for the right it was now. Liberalism never had so many strong planks in its electioneering platform, and we believe we may say with equal propriety and truth that it never had a more earnest exponent than Mr. Arthur Crosfield. He may not have his respected father's dialectic vigour, and if his blood boils he does not say so, but for all that he is a persuasive speaker. The one word perhaps expresses adequately the difference, as platform men, between father and son. Mr. John Crosfield was forcible—as aggres- sive even as Henry Broadhurst—and the audience loved his straight-hitting and plain speaking. Mr. Arthur Ctosfield employs more polished diction and a quieter delivery and, may be, he errs in the direction both of polish and quietness, but for all that his speeches are full of sound and effective argument. Liberals would have -a difficulty in finding any fault with his treatment of the great issues now before the Party, and if be ultimately "enters the ring" again we hope he may emerge triumphant at the close of what will doubtless be another severe struggle.

More light was thrown on the Chinese Labour question—so far as it is a question with people in this country—during the course of Mr. Crosfield's remarks. He read some most important extracts from the Government Blue Book dealing with the treatment of Kaflirs in the Rand mines,and quoted Mr. Winston Churchill's recent remarks on the same subject. Tne crux of the mine-labour pro- blem appears to be here. There are enough natives in South Afric4 to-day to work double the number of mines, but they have naturally rebelled against the "stimulus of cowhide " and other indignities and brutalities attendant upon work in the com- pounds. Fair treatment of the Kaffirs, instead of adding bullying to starvation wages, would, we believe obviate the need for either white or yellow men in the deoths of the mines. LA our readers only peruse the italicised extracts quoted by Mr. Crosfield, and we are sure they will agree with Mr. Churchill that the Government "might have found in the redress of grievances some solution of the question of the labour supply without going all the way to China."

Warrington has been honoured this week by the presence of numerous Wesleyan ministers and laymen from all parts of the Liverpool district —a district extending from Morecambe on the north- west coast to Aberystwith in beautiful Wales. The occasion has been the Annual Synod, a gathering at which important business is transacted affecting the inner working of Methodism. Steady progress was reported in all branches of work, an increase of membership of over 600 being but one gratifying symptom. This may not seem a very large increase of membership shen it is considered that 150 ministers are at work in the district, but in the present age of religious indiffer- ence in many quarters it is an achievement to make progress of any substantial kind. It is certainly not from any lack of perseverance and enterprise that the advance has rot been greater. A deep spiritual tone pervaded the Synod meetings and there was a quiet determina- tion amongst the delegates to press forward the work, encouraged by a continuous record of pro- gress and inspired by the traditions handed down by the great founder of the denomination.

All who have the best interests of Warrington at heart will hope that the clauses to be inserted in the Manchester Ship Canal Bill for the pro- tection of the Warrington Corporation and traders on the river will bring about a settlement of the long-standing differences which have existed on the vexed question of dredging. It will be to the advantage of all parties concerned if the antagon- ism which has existed for so long gives place to a better understanding. Warrington has never taken full advantage of the excellent waterway which the Ship Canal provides. The company on the one hand has been unaccommodating to the local traders, and they on the other hand, hampered by the restrictions of the river, have not been able to develop the local carrying traffic to its fullest extent. One of the concessions made by the Ship Canal Com- pany in the new clauses to be inserted in the Bill is that of allowing vessels bound for Warrington to leave the Ship Canal at Runcorn and enter the river. The Warrington traders have asked for this concession since the opening of the waterway but it has hitherto been denied. The concession should greatly facilitate the local carrying trade on the river and canal. There has been a marked advance within recent years but with an amicable working agreement between the Canal Company and the traders there is no reason why a larger share of the enormous traffic of this busy manufacturing centre should not be developed fo r the mutual advantage of both parties.

There is a very large mountain growing out of the standard wage molehill in regard to Corpori- tion contracts. The Town Council finished with the question last week by deciding tiat they had no cause to interfere with the letting of the con- tract for pails to Messrs. Rhode.; and Soo of Stourbridge. Now, we see that the Trades Council has been having a lengthy pow-wow on the subject assisted by the Secretary of the Amalgamated Union of Iron Plate Workers. Both he and Councillor Stott made lengthy speeThes, but we cannot obtain from their remarks any informa- tion which proves that Messrs. Rhodes do not pay their workmen a fair day's wage. It is not clear, moreover that there is such a thin.- as a standard wage in the Stourbridge district. 'At anv rate, if there is, we ought to have been informed how far it was recognised by other manufacturers in the same trade and locality. Nothing has beeo said with any semblance of pro if in reference to this important point. We should imagine, if Messrs. Rhodes were not paying a recognised standard wage that their competitors would be the first to com- plain about it. The Chairman of the Council "as la ratepayer, objected to be a participant in any sweating." May we remind him that he has not the monopoly of righteousness and love of fair dealing between master and men. There has not been one jot or tittle of evidence which justifies the use of the word" sweating," and it should never have been introduced even casually. We have every confidence in the honesty of Councillor Stott's motives, but we are convinced that in regard to the complaint so loudly voiced, neither he nor the Trades Council have any case.

Warrington can take credit to itself fnr setting a good exampl. ir N.S.P.C.C. work. Not only is the local branch of this splendid .institutiou self supporting, but this year a contribution of VS

has been made to the national fund. The societY needs all the help that an appreciative commual can give, and a great enrt is shortly to be reen9 to obtain the wherewithal to open up fresh fielde of work. There are still many places to which the society has not penetrated, and where, no doubt, to use the words of Miss England at the annual meeting of the Warrington branch," the sufferieK of children goes on unheeded." In Warringte. ° the N.S.P.C.C. has doce much to justify its exist': ence, and we hope the success already achievea will be a stimulus to increased zeal on the children's behalf.

The stars in their courses are fighting agaie,et the Protectionists. Last year they confidentile predicted that our foreign trade was on the eve 01 a period of rapid decline. As a fact, despite the disturbing influences at work in the cotton trade, and the events in the Far East, our exports are increasing in a very satisfactory manner. Thus the Board of Trade returns for April show 11T improvement of no less than £3.50,000 on Aptlif 1903, and this notwithstanding the fact that in the corresponding month of last year the returns were swollen by the delivery of a telegraph cable to the value of £900,000. The increased exporu of woollens;forms an especially:encouraging feature of the returns. There can be no question that although our home trade is dull our foreign' customers are increasing their orders from British manufacturers.

This must be very trying to our " Fisca! reformers," who have persuaded themselves that our foreign markets are slipping away from 1r and that our real condition of commercial decse- once is only “masked by a growth in the exports to the Colonial markets. But the figures sheW that our Colonial trade is falling off at present under the influence of the depression in South Africi, and that the expansion of our export trade in the aggregate is due to the larger orders from the much abused foreigners. These are the facts for the past three months:

-EXPORT OF BRITISH Goons. To foreign To British countrie.. poseessione. £. Mill. £. Jan. to Mar., 1902 41.5 26.3

It ~ 1903 44.0 28.8 91 „ 1904 44.9 27.3 Whatever else our Protectionist friends may make of these figures, they' cannot pretend that theY support their contention that our Colonial cos- torners are coming to the rescue of British ma°°. f acturers.

THE DEEPENING OF THE SHIP CANAL. _ TERMS ARRANGED WITH THE CORPORATION.

Interesting Evidence.

The Private Bill Committee of the House of Lords, over which Lord Glantisk presided, resumed considera- tion on Monday of the Manchester Ship Canal Bill, the object of which is to enable the Company to raise the water level from 26ft. to 28ft. and to cArry out various improvement works. Mr. Balfour Browne, K.C., on behalf of the promoters informed the Committee that an agreement had been arrived at with the Salt Union and the Shropshire Union Canal Company. He hoped that terms would also be agreed upon with the Widnes Corporation and the War- rington Corporation. Mr. A. Beith M`Donald, who gave evidence in support lof the Bill, described the effect of the tidal weir con- trolling the upstream waters of the river Clyde, and . expressed the opinion that the raising of the level of the water in the Ship Canal would do no injury to the estuary of the Mersey. The construction of the sluices ' at Warrington should be a benefit to the estuary. Sir F. Forbes Adam afterwards supported the Bill on behalf of the Manchester Chamber of Commerce, and Mr. C. W, Macara, president of the Federation of Master Cotton Spinners' Associations, spoke for the cotton trade, which he said required that every advantage should be given to it, so as to enable the manufacturers to compete successfully with the foreigner. An increase in the depth of the canal was necessary to facilitate the passage of vessels bringing cotton to Manchester. Considerable weight was attached to the evidence of Mr. R. B. Stoker, managing director of the Manchester Liners Limited, who said that the vessels of that Com- pany often had to load light in order to get up the canal; and to that of Mr. Peter Robertson, also inter- ested in the shipping trade, who strongly supported the Company's scheme. The Lord Mayor of Manchester (Mr. T. Thornhill Shann) stated that the Manchester Corporation and the people of Manchester generally heartily approved of the Canal Company's proposal.

THE CASE FOR THE WARRINGTON TRADERS. This closed the Case for the promoters, and Mr. Hughes K.C., then addressed the Committee on behalf of the three firms of traders at Warrington who have petitioned against the Bill. He explained that his clients were not hostile to the Ship Canal, but they contended that the level of the water could be raised two feet without the construction of the proposed works at Warrington. He argued that the object of the Warrington clauses was merely to try to evade the obligations which the Company had undertaken as regarded the dredging of the river Mersey at Warring- ton. The view of the tradet s was that the quantity of silt coming into the river under the new conditions would be very little less than under the existing condi- tions. If the object of the Company was to get sluicing by holding up the tide and letting it out after high water they could get it better from the existing Walton lock than from the proposed new works. In support of his base, Nlr. Hughes called Mr. F. E. Priest, a civil engineer, of Liverpool, who said that. in his opinion, the proposed works at Warrington would have the effect of producing the silting of the river below the site of the proposed dam,and would seriously damage the estuary. In cross-examination the witness said that if the level of the water were increased two feet, the headway at Warrington bridge would be insufficient for large craft using the waterway. MR. HENRY FAIRCLOUGH GIVES EVIDENCE. THE DANGERS OF WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

Mr. Henry Fairclough, managing director of Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons,Limited, said the navigation of the river was the life and soul of their business at Howley and Bank Quay. The position of the mills as regarded the river was an advantageousone. The milling industry of Lancashire had all gone down to Liverpool and theylhad copied his firm by putting mills on the water side. With regard to the berth at the Bank Quay mill, thescour water had always kept it clear. If this reach of the river, this "dead end "as it had been called were to silt up, it would ruin their business. At the present time the firm's largest boat—the" Problem " could conveniently and safely navigate under Warring- ton bridge. The bridge was in a very awkward position and whoever designed at put it in the worst place. They had to go round the bend,anda vessel goin., through the bridge had to be pulled round quite quick get her in straight at all. If the man in charge had not been with him for thirty years he should be very uneasy at her going under the bridge at all. A very slight swerving would cause her to hit the bridge. If the water shoull be raised at all above its present level, the boat would not go under the bridge. They had been very much troubled in the past owing to dredging not being maintained. Boats had been stuck under Warrington Bridge. The clauses inserted in the Act of 1885 for their protection had not proved satisfactory. The com- pany had never carried one out. By Mr. Browne: Do you mean to say that we have never dredged anything away ? Witness: You have never dredged to 8 feet. And you have never been tO arbitration about it yet ?—Yes, I have. To arbitration?—Not to arbitration! I have been before a judge. Yes I know?—l prefer him to an arbitrator. . . _ _

But you see he has been reversed on appeal?—Oh no. Indeed he has. First of all, at your Mersey Mills, what amount of steam power have you got ?—I daresay if it was all in use, the engines would drive 500 h.p. And in recent years have you been increasing your steam-power there ?—I put a new engine down in case the Ship Canal bought this water. Idid not want to sell the horse when the stable door was shut.

So that you have got good enough steam power to go on without the water ?—Yes. Have you in recent years, since the canal was con- structed, been increasing the size of your boats ?—Yes. How much of your traffic goes by the canal, and how much by the river. You said you had a large amount before ?—I have not worked it out, but I daresay we go once down the river and twice urthe canal. You 'said that tha band of the river where the Bank Quay mills are, has always been kept clear and never, been dredged say it has only been dredged once when the wharf was built.

Supposing it was once dredged, then it did not always keep itself clear ?—Are you alluding to the lime now. ? Yes; you remember about that lime ?—Oh, yes. It was dredged on that occasion ?—The lime was dredged out at that time. You remember that it was after it had been deepened that the lime was dredged out ?—Theycould not dredge out without it had been put in. tme see if I can understand your position. At thLe lime special meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce under the presidency of Mr. John Fairclough, for the purpose of considering the proposals made by the Ship Canal Company with reference to the condition of the river, the Council expressed their approval of the gen- eral principle involved in the construction of the weir and sluices. Is that true ?—I cannot say what my brother did. I was not present at the Chamber of Commerce.

You are not your brother's keeper ?—T have not the slightest doubt he voted against it. It might be carried It does not say it was not without prejudice,and it does not say it was unanimous. ,Is your brother here ?—He is not here. A meeting of the Chamber of Commerce can scarcely have been without prejudice. Mr. John Fa,irclough is not here ?—No.

And you stayed away from the meeting in order to come here ?—No, I never went to one yet. Have you been upon the Rivers Committee?—Never. At any rate the Rivers Committee and traders and everyone connected with it seem to have approved of the principle of constructing a river diversion at Lower Walton with a lock and sluices. Would you tell me? have you not yourself from the beginning approved of the principle of our making this diversion and sluices? —I have not.

Why not ?—Because I want to be settled with bafore I approve of anything. Ah, that will do; I think I know what the settlement is; you want Mr. Justice Byrne's judgment ?—I should like it.

Re-examined by Mr. Hughes: You have had to go into a good deal of litigation to get the obligations of the Canall Company performed ?—I am very nearly a lawyer I think now, after ten years of it. I suppose you go down the river whenever you can, in preference to the canal ?—We can go down the river from Warrington Bridge in 21 hours, and we cannot go down the canal in 5 hours.

And is the river much more rapid when you eau go down, and cheaper? Yes, we have no looks to go through.

MR. F. W. MONKS SPEAKS OF THE DANGERS OF THE NEW SCHEME.

Mr. F. W. Monks, managing director of Messrs. Monks Hall and Company Limited, said they had a river front- age rather more than half a mile in length. This frontage was of very great importance. It was a saving in carriage of 50 per cent as compared with sending by rail. The damming up of the river in the waysuggested would in the first place sever their river frontage into two portions. That in itself would be a distinct dis- advantage because at present they could load along any length ; commence loading die boat in one place them move her along land finish loading in another. With the dam between they could not do that,so that that would be a distinct disadvantage to. start with:Another way in which it would damage their interests was, that it would do away with the wharf that they had on the tidal water, which was really of more value to them than a wharf upon still water would Ir. With a wharf on the tidal water they had no restriction as regarded locks'or anything of that kind. The boats could get away as soon as there was water down the river. That enabled the firm to get into Liverpool in time to lock into the LiverPoel docks at the same tide. Any restrictions would mean probably the loss of a tide. By Mr. Browne : You say we have not maintained a depth of Bft.; but that is another question. If we can do it by means of these works at less expense and give you the 8 feet which you say you have not gotupto

The Examiner, Saturday, May 14, 1904

now, will not that be an advantage to you and to us ? Not altogether, no. Why not; you complain we have not given you Sit.? —lt is not altogether a question of the depth of water.

What is it then? I thought it was ?—We have only from one tide to another to load and discharge the steamers. If we have to come round the river this long length, round that end and pass through the locks, that will reduce our time by one or two hours. I have a letter from the Town ulerk. to Mr. Bythell, the Chairman of the Ship Canal Company, dated 15th April, 1901, which reads: "I beg to inform you that the Corporation and traders are unanimously of opinion that the only basis of settlement must be upon the con- dition that the judgment of Mr. Justice Byrne should be restored. If this preliminary point be agreed they would consider with much favour any proposal which, while safeguarding the interests of the Corporation and traders, may enable the Canal Company to comply with their obligations in a manner most economical and satisfactory to themselves." DR. MARKEL'S EVIDENCE.

A CASE OF "LIFE AND DEATH."

When the Select Committee of the House of Lords reassembled on Tuesday, further evidence was called on behalf of the three Warrington trading firms who have petitioned against the Canal Company's proposals. Dr. Markel, of Nlessrs. Joseph Crosffeld and Sons,Ltd., works, examined by Mr. Hughes, K.C., said that at present the wharf at his works was kept entirely clear from silt,'and if it silt-3d up it would be a serious thing for the business. Large quantities of cold water were required for the works for the purposes of condensing steam at low temperature, and, therefore, the tempera- ture of the water was an important element in the business. The company took from the river for this purpose ten tons a minute day and night all the year round, except at holiday times. That water was returned to the river at a lower point, the temperature being raised about 40 degrees. If this dam was constructed and a dead end formed the effect on the works might be disastrous. They might have to apply other means at great expense, or remove the works to another place. 'Other manufacturers were also taking water from this part of the river for cooling purposes. The total amount of water so taken was, he calculated, 30 tons a minute. It was calculated that the total amount of water that would be contained in this dead end would be 80,000 tons. The temperature of the water in the dead end would be raised. Of course that effect would be cumulative, and he was told that there would be seven or eight days without any tidal influence if the new sluices were constructed. His firm had regenera- tors below the ground, and if the water level in the eon were raised this plant might be seriously affected. Messrs. Crosfleld had for years been proposing to extend their works, and a year ago succeeded in acquiring the land in the bend of the river. This land would be cut off by the river diversion, and he did not understand that his firm would be entitled to a right of way over the dam through Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co.'s land. Cross-examined by Mr. Balfour Browne : You take the chemical part of the work of manufacture—l have the control of the entire manufacture, not only the chemical.

And not the-business aspect of the case ?—No, not the selling nor the buying. Who does that, Who is the real business manager ? The buying is done mainly by Mr. J. J. Crosfield, Mr. Roberts acting as consultant in that case; and the selling is done by another director, Mr. Huffam. Because of all theseproceedings that have taken place hitherto Mr. Roberts has been the person who has been in the forefront of the matter ?—Yes, Mr. Roberts has been in the forefront. Ido not know why you come in now ?—Because think this is to prove how our manufacture will be seri- ously damaged if we have this back water, Do you mean to say that before Mr. Roberts spoke and said it would be a great improvement to everybody, he never consulted you or the other directors ?—Cer- tainly. I think you refer probably to the meeting that we had at the Town Hall with the Ship Canal Company Probably ?--.-When this scheme was first put before us. At that time we were all anxious—

To have the scheme ?—No. Allow me to speak. We were all anxious that the Ship Canal should carry out those obligations of always giving us enough water which we had been trying for, for so many years, and if we could failitat3 their doing so—their being able to do so if we could facilitate in any way to bring about this, we naturally would do our best to meet them. Very well then, then we had this put before us; it was quite new to us; we understood that if we agreed to this pro- posal we would not have only the same water, but more water than ever—we would have lift. Gin. I think it is. That seemed advantageous and to be taken as advised was practically in favour of the Ship Canal Company. We said: That is the thing; we will consider the matter then, and if we find no disadvantage we will see whether we can accept, it. You knew of the proposal to mike the dam and river deversion, and you approved them ?—No, we did not apnroveof them. We said if we could we would favour- ably consider them.

Mr. Roberts said at that meeting: "By the present scheme the Ship C mai Company are seeking to carry ou their obligation with regard to the depth and width o the channel by another method"?—Yes. That is whar we understood at the time.

Do you understand it now ?—Yo. S) ma 2,11 better that we see all these disadvantages. Mr. Roberts added that it looked to him not only a feasible project, but one that ought to be supported.— Mr. Roberts is notan engineer nor is he a che nist. The main point which struck him was that he would have more water.

--And Mr. Roberts also said: "If the Corporation are advised by Sir John Wolfe Barry that the scheme is advisable, there can be no question of detriment to Warrington in having what could be practically a dock a mile and a half in length with a minimum depth of water of at least ten feet ?"—Yes, Mr. Roberts under- stood that we were to have ten feet of water, but when we asked Mr. Hunter to agree to ten feet he said " Certainly not. We can gutranteo no such thing. " Mr. Roberts naturally thought that ten feet would be an advantage.

Supposing it had been 10ft. • how would that hay got over the difficulty of you'll drain?—lt would no have got over the difficulty of our drain. How would it have got over the difficulty of your getting access to the land you purchased ?—ire then did not know that we could not get access by the proposed dam, because Mr. Hunter told us we could. How would it have got over the difficulty of the hot water ?--We had not gone intof the matter then of the hot water. When you get a thing like that throun at your head you cannot see all the disadvantages that such a scheme involves.

Mr. Roberts ;I.:;"ss:arci :• "The proposal is, .on the whole, fraught with immense favourable possibilities for the future of Warrington ?"—That is true. But Mr. Roberts certainly does not think that now. Mr. Roberts was "strongly of opinion that the Ship Canal Company's proposals were good. The Canal Com- pany would not be relieved of their obligations by this scheme. They were simply proposing the present scheme to Warrington as a batter way, and to the corn-. pany a cheaper way of dealing with their present obligations, and if Warrington could se 3no objection they certainly ought not to put any difficulties .in the company's way, and they ought to give it their un- qualified support"?—l cannot see what I have to do with this.

Mr. Balfour Browne: Mr. Roberts was speaking for your !Inn?

The witness: He was speaking for our firm ; but cannot Mr. Roberts speak for himself? Call on him. Mr. Balfour Browne: I though he was not to be called.

Mr. Hughes: We will make every effort in the course of the day. MESSRS. CROSFIELD'S TRAFFIC MANAGERS

EVIDE ,CE.

Mr. Moss, traffic manager to Messrs. Crosfleld, was then examined. He said he was in charge of the navigation, and the river traffic was under his control They had nine boats of their ovvn,and did a large carry ing trade. Cross-examined by Mr. Honomtus Lloyd: Before the Ship Canal was made your only means of access of course was by the river ?—Yes. _ _ .

Am I right'in thinking that since the Ship Canal was made you have employed much larger boats ?—Yes. km I right in thinking that since the canal was con- structed you have embarked upon a general carrying business ?—Yes, we have. I do not know if they carried before, I am not quite sure, I was not with Crosfield's then.

So that under the conditions which have been brought about by the Canal Company you are now using larger boats carrying greater tonnage, and you have embarked upon a new form of business—the general carrying busi- ness ?—Yes, but then the whole of our business has gone forward, gone up, we have not stood still, every branch of it has gone forward. The Chairman: If you wanted to use the river now, assuming the future state of things had come about as the Canal Company wish, what is to prevent you going through the proposed diversion and the lock and sluice that I see marked, and so into the river ?—ln that case we could only load up to 81t. Why ?—Because we would only get Bft. of water past the'dam there.

On the shallows you told us ?—On the shallow part there we would get Bft. at a 16ft. tide ; with an 18 or 23ft. tide we get plenty of water there. But I under- stand from the Manchester Ship Canal they will only give us 8 feet inside and the dead water. Mr. Lloyd: That is Bft. atlow water of spring tides. THE MERSEY CONSERVATOR'S POWERS.

Sir George Nares, the acting conservator of the Mersey, was then called. YESTERDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

The consideration of the Manchester Ship Canal Bill which was to have been resumed yesterday has been further postponed to Tuesday next. Mr. Lloyd, on behalf of the promoters, applied for a further adjourn- ment on the ground that the promoters had not received from the Mersey Conservancy the clauses which the committee had decided they should propose for their protection.

What the Corporation has Obtained. We have not yet had an opportunity'. of seeing the clauses to be inserted in the Bill for the protection of the Corporation but we understand that the arrange- ments entered into include the following important matters :

(1) For a depth of water in that portion of the rive affected by the proposed works of 8 feet at all times from a fixed bottom datum line with dredging obligations. - (2) Construction of two 3 feet culverts in the Dam for scouring out impounded water.

(3) The coll3truction by the Comp‘ny of the rising main or sewer and the maintenance thereof until such time as the Corporation are required to take the sewage out of the river.

(4) Vessels may leave Canal at Runcorn. (5) The Borough Surveyor to be sole and absolute arbitrator with respect to all future obstructions in the river.

(6) Independent right.of action given to the traders.

A NEW NORTH WALES STEAMER.

Trial Run of La Marguerite.

The fine paddle steamer La Marguerite, which the Liverpool and North Wales Steamship Co. Ltd. have just added to their fleet, created quite a small sensation at the Liverpool landing-stage on Thursday. Her lofti- ness and spaciousness were apparent and impressive,and she was generally voted a very handsome addition to the passenger steamers of the city. On Thursday La Marguerite made her trial run from Liverpoel and Llandudno, and by the courtesy of the directors of the Company, invitations were sent to some 400 persons,including shareholders, railway and shipping representatives, and journalists from Lancashire arid Yorkshire. There was a great crowd on the landing- stage, and hearty cheering sent La Marguerite on her initial trip at 11.30, accompanied by strains of music from the band of H.M.S. Indefatig- able, which was on board. Fine weather pre- vailed, and the run to Llandudno was made very comfortably and with no haste. An admirable luncheon was served on board,the spacious dining saloon being much admired in the courseof satisfying the inner man. There was a short stay at the popular Welsh watering place, during which the passengers went ashore and the welcoming crowd on the pier boarded La Mar- guerite on a tour of inspection. The return journey commenced at 4 p.m. and,the new stexmer gave a limit of her steaming qualities by running to Liverpool in a level two hours. Beautiful weather prevailed, and pro- bably none of the guests on board had ever enjoyed the North Wales trip more than they did on Thursday. The abundance of promenading accommodation on La Mar- guerite is a distinguishing feature, and one that pas- sengers most heartily will appreciate. Indeed, she is a most commodious and handsomely appointed boat and we doubt not, will soon win great popularity with the Lancashire and Yorkshire crowds. A few details about her history and construction will probably be inter- esting at this stage. La Marguerite was purchased from the New Palace Steamers of London, where she had been on the London and Bologue service and was generally recognised as the premier passenger steamer of the Thames. She was built by the Fairfield Shipbuilding and Engineering Company and represents value to the extent of £85,000. Her dimensions are a follows :—Length between per- pendiculars, 330 feet; length over all, 341 feet G inches; breadth moulded, 40 feet: extreme breadth, 75 feet; depth moilded (to upper deck), 21 feet 6 inches ; draught on service, 9 to 10 feet; gross tonnage, 2,204 ; and indicated horse power, 7,599, She has' two funnels and two masts, the hull is of steel throughout, and all the structural scantling were approved by the Board of Trade. She is divided, in accordance with the recom- mendation of the Bulkhead Committee, into eleven water-tight compartments. The bulkheads thus introduced besides making the vessel practically unsinkable Etrengthen the structure considerably by acting as supports and ties between the decks and framing. She can carry 3,485 passengers on No. 5 certificate, and about 2,160 between Liverpool and North Wales. Fitted with every attention to detail, and with an elegance and completeness hitherto unapproached by any pleasure steamer, La Marguerite has everything to recommend her, even to the most fastidious pleasure seeker. During the trial trip on Thursday the Chairman of the Company made a brief speech. He said it might be asked why the Liverpool and North Wales Company had acquired this steamer for their North Wales service. All companies at the present time must either go ahead or be satisfied to take a second place, and the Liverpool and North %Vales Company had no intention of taking second place. He discussed the merits of turbine steamers but thought a greater astern steaming power was required for narrow waters. With an ordinary steamer they could get a greater speed going astern, and that was a strong point. They could not have got a steadier steamer than La Marguerite, which was another important consideration. ' The vessel was a preat credit to her builders, the Fair- field Engineering and Shipbuilding Company, who had also built the St. Elvies and the St. Tudno. Whether she would be the success she should be depended entirely on the public for the company had now done their share in providing the facilities for comfortable travel. He proposed the toast of "La Marguerite," coupling it with the name of Mr. W. M. Rhodes, of the Fairfield Shipbuilding and Engineering Company. _

Mr. Rhodes said they in the south regretted very much their parting with La Marguerite. which, speaking for himself, he regarded as the apple of his eye. He considered that her sale was a great loss to the London public. However, their loss was Liverpool's gain, and he hoped the people up north would take full advantage of the opportunities offered. He thoroughly agreed with the chairman in his remarks as to turbine steamers, but while they were not yet quite practical in a service like this, he believed the turbine would revolutionise the method of steam propulsion in the future. He con- gratulated the directors and shareholders of the Liver- pool and North %Vales Company upon having acquired La Marguerite so cheanly. Mr. J. J. Metcalf, Mayor of Bootle, proposed " The chairman and directors of the Liverpool and North Wales Steamship Company," and the chairman briefly responded.

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN,

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour

and Social Problems."

MAKING THE MONEY FLY.

"The financial position of this country is worse now than it has been at any time during the last fifty years. Expenditure is admittedly out of hand. The national credit is lower than it has been for twenty years, and, relatively to the credit of other nations, it is lower than at any other period during the last fifty years."—Slß EDGAR VINCENT, M.P. The Budget night in the House of Commons is always interesting to the long-suffering taxpayer. It is the time of our national stocktaking, and the ChancelJor of the Exchequer tells us how much money he has received and where it came from, and how the taxation affected our trade. Then he tells us how much money we have spent, and where it has gone, and what we have got to show for it. And, finally he estimates our income and expenditure for the coming year, and he arranges his taxes and tariffsaccordinaly. We have to go through this ordeal once every year, whether we like it or not, because the House of Commons is the highest taxing authority in the land, and it has the first claim on us. Then it hands us over to the tender mercies of Town Councils,and City Corporations, and highway and sanitary authorities, and Parish Councils, and these greedy cormorants skin us, and fleece us, and rob us of all that is left. We are absolutely it their mercy, and we must grumble and pay. I.—PUBLIC EXPENDITURE HAS BEEN . INCREASING AT AN ALARMING RATE.

For thel last ten or a dozen years the whole nation has been cursed with a spirit of reckless extrava- gance, and an insane desire to live and prosper,on other peoples' money. In the House of Commons a cry has been made for .more battleships, and cruisers, and guns, and for a hirger army and navy. In the City Councils and municipal areas there has been a demand for new tram lines, and electric lights, and public parks, and schools, and educational machinery. If you lift up your voice against this growing expenditure you are laughed at and called a prophet of evil. If you ask where the money is to come from you are told to go and borrow it.

The most serious part of this criminal recklessness is that the people who have to pay these rates and taxes are willing to plunge gaily into debt, and fling money away faster than it comes in, and leave posterity to pay their debts. Let us see how our national expenditure has grown. Sir Edgar Vincent, the Tory member for Exeter, is a recognised authority on financial matters, and he told the House of Commons the other day that "in 1893 our total expenditure amounted to ninety millions. Last year it was one hundred and forty millions. This is an increase of forty millions in. ten years, or at the rate of fifty-five per cent. It is an increase out of proportion either to the increase of our population, or trade, or wealth."

Just think for a moment what this increase means. Fifty-five per cent. would be eleven shillings on every pound, so we are paying thirty-one shillings now for every sovereign we used to spend. If you go back another twenty years, you will find that in 1873 the nation paid its way, and lived in comfort on an ex- penditure of sixty-two millions. So that in thirty years we have multiplied that expenditure by two avd a quarter. This is a rise of one hundred and twenty- five per cent., and for every sovereign we paid then we are paying two pounds five shillings now. This growing extravagance will make a serious hole in any man's pocket, and neither nations nor individuals can stand it long. Our income is not increasing as fast as our expenditure. Last year our taxes yielded £2,724,421 less than we anticipated, and we spent £3,007,000 more than we intended to spend. So we are £5,731,421 to the bad. We make up these deficiencies by piling dn more taxes, that come out of our pockets. Our tea is increased by twopence per pound. Another penny is put on our income tax, and increased duties are levied on tobacco and cigars. So the tea drinkers and smokers and income taxpayers trre raided and robbed to cover our national extravagance. But these are only half our troubles. While the Im- perial Parliament is building costly battleships, that will be broken up and sold for old iron in a year or two, and squandering our money in madcap expeditions to the ends of the earth, our local Parliaments are doing some reckless things under our very noses. Poor rates and education rates, and borough and police rates are going up merrily. In some towns they are as high as nine or ten shillings in the pound,and our rating autho- rites are mortgaging the future by plunging us more deeply into debt. If you think these reckless games can last for ever you are greatly mistaken. -There are limits to human endurance, and menlwill not quietly submit to increased taxation when thousands of our people are unemployed, and wages are falling, and our tradesmen are making smaller profits ; and there are limits to our borrowing powers. We have bled the banks, and the investors of trust money, till we have nearly drained them dry, and those of them who could help us if they would are buttoning up their pockets, or demanding higher rates of interest. And who can blame them? _

Sir Edgar Vincent evidently reads these signs of the times, and in warnitv, the Tories of the folly of their Protection craze anetheir reckless expenditure, he said, "I hope the Government will not be misled by the notion that scientific taxation can produce money from the clouds. It may obscure the incidence, but it can- not lighten the burden." These are wise and timely words of warning, and if we no not promptly take heed to our ways, the avenger that is on our track will teach us all wisdom by bitter and painful experience.

IL-RECKLESS BORROWING HAS UNDER- MINED OUR CREDIT.

If you want to be a successful borrower you must not borrow too often, and you must not ask for too much money, and you must be punctual in the payment pf your interest and the instalments of your debt. If you are going every Monday to your uncle's to pawn your clothes, and every Saturday night to redeem them, you will have your neighbours talking about you, and adver- tising your poverty. Then nlbody will lend you a shilling without duple security and a high rate of interest.

If you get your name up as a chronic borrower, always "stoney broke," and "." short," and living on your ;uncle, your neighbours will want to know what you do with aly money when you get it. If you spend borrowed money in boozing,and making fool's bargains, and backing horses that never win, you will so-On be known as a" wrong 'nil." and your crediters will be on your track, and you will have the "bums " in the house, and you will be chucked out. • This is what happens to individuals when they in- dulge in reckless borrowing and insane speculations,and it is what happens to nations, and City and Town Coun- cils, for the same eternal laws govern every one of us. Our loss of credit is seen in the reduced value of our securities. Ten years ago our Consols and gilt-edged securities were worth twenty-five per cent. more than they are to-day. But our National Debt alone is over seven hundred millions, so this drop in price represents a loss of £175,000,009 to the unlucky investors, and all other gift-edged securities have depreciatectin sympathy with them.

Before we began our wholesale reckless borrowing we had no difficulty in raising national and municipal loans at two and a half per cent., and the lender would give us £lO4 for each £lOO of stock. Our Consols and Government stock at three per cent. were so popular that when Lord Goschen reduced the interest on them he did not materially reduce the market price of them. In those days of peace and prosperity each £lOO of our Consols rose to £ll4. Then the Boer war came, and we squandered the savings of the last thirty years, and borrowed nearly two hundred millions, and the price fell to £B6. Now let us see what this means. If you were executor under a friend's will, and invested his money for security in Consols to the amount .of £114,000, and were forced to sell at the lowest price, yo u would only get £86,000 for the stock. So there would be a dead loss to that estate of £28,000. Now these "gilt-edged"securities, as they are called, are held by all our banks, and insurance offices, and trusts,in very large amounts, and these large holders have had to re-value them, and reduce the price in their books, and when they are sold the sharehold.rs will have to bear the loss.

One insurance company owns up to a depreciation of more than £300,000 in its gilt-edged securities, and it has reducei its bonus and written down the value of its stock. Another wealthy insurance company has written off more than half a million as its share of the loss, and our banks and trusts are following their example. And you can never hope for any recovery in prices so long as the law of supply and demand is being violated so recklessly. Russia and Japan are at war, and spend- ing fabulous sums of money. They are offering twice as much interest for war loans as we are getting on our securities and if they float new loans now, they will absorb all our spare cash, and send up the rate of interest all round, and pull down the value of our best securities.

_ Our loss of credit not only reduces the value of our securities, but it compels us to pay a higher rate of interest. The heavy fall of one hundred and seventy five millions in the value of our National Debt has shaken men's confidence in every other form of invest- ment. Consols used to be reekoned as good as bank notes, or silver and gold, but when they dropped twenty five per cont, in value the holders took it out of those who cisme to borrow again by charging a higher rate of interest for all future loans.

You have a good example of this in the experience of the London County Council. in the days before the war it cou'd borrow money at two and a half per cent., and float the loan at a premium. In these dark days it has to pay three per cent. for its loans, and float them at a discount.

Since May,l9oo,the Council has bozrowed twenty-two

millions of money at three per cent. interest, and its latest price of issue is ninety per cent. If they were all issued at this price, instead of getting the full twenty-two millions, the council would only get, £19,800,000 and the remaining £2;200,000 is a bribe to tempt the subscribers to part with their money. In June, 1896, the Council issued a two and a half per cent, loan at 104 i on each ..-L:,100t, If the twenty-two millions had been borrowed on these terms the Council would have received £22,990,000 as the proceeds of the loan, and this difference of £3,190,000 will have to be paid by the unlucky ratepayers. But the difference of one half per cent.. on twenty- two millions is £llO,OOO a year, and this additional burden will come out of the ratepayers also, so you see our loss of credit increases our expenditure, and lessens our income, and impoverishes every one of us. The Chancellor of the Exchequer says that in the three years ending March, 1903, the local authorities of, this country borrowed, £1.03,000,000, and at the close of 1901 the total outstanding local loans were £412,000,000. There are some evils in this world that will cure themselves if you only give them time enough, and reckless borrowing is one of them. The last great war pulled down our gilt-edged securities more than 25 per cent in value, and another such enterprise would reduce them another twenty-five. But the money lenders are getting alarmed about the security, and they are button- ing up their pockets, and refusing to lend any money on the old terms. I wish the average ratepayer would wake up to the awful perils of the times, and protest against reckless expenditure and wholesale borrowing, and insist on the nation living within its income and honestly paying its way. John Bull is a patient ass, and he meekly submits to have these intolerable burdens piled on his back. If he staggers and falls beneath his financial burdens it will be a bad look out for every one of us. "He that bath ears to hear, let him hear."

===== 0002601_19040514_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
"RAGGING" AT OXFORD.

THE" DAILY MAIL" HOAXED BY SPORTIVE STUDENTS.

Much quiet joy is now beating gently in the hearts of the more sporting members of the 'Varsity owing to the recent revelations of " Ragging at Oxford " printed with such smacking of fat lips by the "Daily Mail." Now that the great scheme has run its allotted course,there seems no reason why people out of Oxford should not be informed of the hoax. It would appear that the D.M. methods of acquiring facts do not quite satisfk the more critical politicians and students of Oxford, and it was thought for the benefit of the people in London and the provinces, that they should be made to share in the keen penetra- tion of their methods that prevails at Oxford. And this is the manner of doing it. A gentleman of great athletic fame here wrote a plaintive letter to the D.M., complaining of the severe ragging to which he had been subjected, and quoting other instances of such gratuitous wrong-doing. The D.M., with that fine and ready sympathy for oppressed humanity that touches one so, dispatched a reporter to the actual scene of the disturbances. There in an undergraduate's room a scene of very great dramatic power was enacted. It was something like this. The reporter was sitting gathering his information in the rooms of a " fair giant" (to borrow the apt expression employed by the D.M.), so characteristic of the public school boy, when the door was suddenly burst in and a shivering youth (suspected at Oxford to be a gentleman of ripe athletic fame) sank down in a chair crying feebly "Just escaped" a very picture of limp exhaustion. Just in time.the oak was sported, and outside might be heard the gnashings of teeth and hurling of coals cast at the oak, on the part of the raggers reft of their prey. It was a moving spectacle, and the reporter took copious notes and. rejoiced he'd come. When the first lot withdrew, another gentleman, very white and terrified, fluttered into the room followed by his two pursuers. They twisted his chair round, but withdrew on a motion Of the fair giant. Much conversation on Oxford ragging ensued between the reporter, his host, and a friend -who came in later to add anything omitted by the other. Men were ragged at Oxford it seemed, for nany new, as well as all the old, offences. Besides the ancient sin of having a walking-stick when in cap and gown, a first year man might not put on patent boots, nor wear cap and gown, except when unavoidable, under surety of ragging. A provincial accent carried graver penalties. Fictitious instances were quoted of gentlemen subject to heart disease being reduced to a perspiration by artificial means, and then tenderly un- dressed and squirted over with 'soda-water syphons. Only public-school *boys are safe at Oxford; yet an instance was adduced of the son of a Peer having his leg bitten in three places. • In amaze- ment the reporter wondered "who then can be saved ?" He asked the afore-mentioned fair giant to what he attributes his immunity from these baneful attacks. The latter yawned, stretched himself in the accepted manner of fair giants,and mentioned a casual interest in boxing. • On the day following these accounts appeared in the D.M. and it was seen that they "had been understated rather than overstated by our correspondent." A. lengthy controversy has been conducted in the D.M. columns by men who have gone down, and outsiders, reviewing ragging at Oxford during the last few decades. Meanwhile it was impossible to buy a D.M. at Oxford : favourite newspaper boys laughed you to scorn—papers were bargained for at 6d. each—and even now if you borrow last Monday's " Mail " from the Steward at the ' Union a small boy keeps guard over you. Successive issues of the paper were attended with equal joy, , Many undergraduates have received letters of warning from anxious parents. They reply that the only thing they've heard of being ragged is a paper somewhere in London. Undergraduates must be excused if they are unduly gay at having " rotted " a great London Daily. and held even so famed a person as its Editor as one of but small account. . A few chosen words to any who wish to know the true state of things here (in contrast with the repre- sentations of prominent journals) might soothe some over-ruffled nerves. It is true that provincial accents meet with some slight scorn; it is true that it is wrong co wear your gown when you can possibly get out of it; to pour your cider-into another man's beer in Hall is not necessarily ungentlemanly, and may not be sconced unless the head of table is hypersensitive ; it is true too that it is not well to over-insist on yourself while yet a Fresher, to mention your mother frequently in conversation, nor to give a wine " open to the college " (to confine it to your year would be a. praiseworthy and sporting thing); it is also believable that Oxford first year might be very trying to many a fine young fellow of noble parts—but one's experience is that a man with any tact and strength of character will come through all these things. Oxford contains the best young men of their year in England. It is conceivable th tto subdue and contain yourself might be part of a training that .no life other than that of the two older Universitieslcan give one. Trinity College, Oxford

J.C.O.

STOCKTON HEATH.

STOCKTON HE .A.TII G :MOEN SO CI ET v.—Arrangements are being made for the members of the Garden Society to pay periodical visits to the leading resident's gardens in the district in order to obtain practical lessons bathe cultivation of choice flowers, plants, fruits and veget- ables, &c. The monthly meeting of the Garden Society was held on Tuesday evening, under the chairmanship of Mr. Charles Plinston. There was a good attendance of members, and much enthusiasm was displayed in local horticultural matters. It is hoped this enthusiasm will result in an excellent display at the show to be held in August. Arr. W. H. Woodcock gave an address on ",Hardy Perennials," illustrated by specimen plants and flowers, which were distributed to the members at the close of the meeting. Mr. %Woodcock showed how by care, foresight, and experience it was possible to have 1 an almost continuous display of flowers throughout the year. The preparation of the beds, their shape and method of treatment, were described, and a list of the best sorts to grow was given. A hearty vote of thanks was unanimously accorded the lecturer. months ago to my friends on the Exe3utive Committee privately. I don't want anyone here, and I don't want anyone in Warrington to think I have been trying to thrust myself forward into the position which 1 had the honour to occupy at the last election, a position I considered it a very great honour to fill, but which, I am very well aware, other people of more political experience and ability would be much better qualified to fill. (Renewed cheers).
