CHINESE LABOUR.

Well now, before I sit down there is another question to which I wish to allude,and with your kind indulgence I should like to read a few extracts bearing upon the question of Chinese Labour iu South Africa, on which Mr. Balfour spoke last night. This is certainly a vary serious questidn for all who are interested in labour and all who are interested in maintaining throughout the world the good fame of the British nation(lear,hear).The first extract is from the" Daily News" and is as follows: " We have obtained a copy of the Cape Colony re- port in reference to the treatment of Kaffirs in the Rand mines which formed the subject of Major Saely's motion in the House last night. On page 5 we read that the inquiry clearly establishes that misrepresentations in regard, to wages are mule by the labour agents, that occasional wrongful deduc- tions are imposed, assaults by European overseers and the native police are of freguent occurrence, and that the system pursued in calculating the time for which wages are due is not intelligible to the native, and is the cause of much of the distrust existing." If these defects are remedied," says Mr. A. H. Stanford, the assistant chief magistrate at Untata, " I have no doubt that some of the ob- jections which the Transkeien natives now have to going to Johannesburg would be removed. But the wages paid are undoubtedly less than before the war, and as long as the natives can obtain more remunerative labour elsewhere the labour supplied to the goldfields is not likely to be much aug- mented from these sources." It is reported that "at various of the compounds the boys complained of being hurried by means of the application by the native police of the gentle stimulus of cowhide.' It is further said that almost without exception the compound police carry sjamboks. At the Village Deep . . . there were complaints of this hustling by the compound police." We further read that "at various of the compounds complaints were made that the compound authorities were in the habit of patine' boys in the stocks "but that, "by the orders of the present Government all stocks had been collected and burnt." This illustrates the kindliness of the randlords. The most serious complaint is however said to be "that men have been paid at a very much lower rate than that promised them by recruiting agent." Instances are given where £3 15s. per month was offered by the recruiting agent and only £2 10s. paid in the mine. "Another complaint" we read, "is that they (the natives) are crowded in their sleeping places, and that there is not sufficient ventilation.'" In another place we read that "there are all sorts of complaints made by boys —that theyaresjamboked and bullied by these stupid indunas—and that when they are sick they are not allowed to go home." The boys complain further that the compounds have their own coffee shops, where they force the boys to buy and not at any other which they may choose,and where they could get groceries at a. cheaper rate." There is apnarently open buying 4and selling of labourers. On page 26 we read, "Whoever requires labourers buys them." On page 27 we read that 'any labourers who were imprisoned are at the expiration of their sentences,so/d to those that wantilabourers." On page 37 we read," I found three places where boys were freely beaten, and stopped wages, viz., Village Deep, Geldenhuis and Railway Compound." Onipage 10 we reach "At three places I found natives had been flogged by the overseers. I per- sonally spoke to the men who had been flogged, and saw the marks on their bodies." On the same page we find a summing up of the entire situation. "I wish to mention," says one of the headmen, "that I have known natives go to work in places where they will receive less wages but good treatment rather than go to Johannesburg." In conclusion, the Blue Book, from which we have made most imperfect extracts, is one long record of ill-treat- ment,cheating and cruelty. For instance what are we to say of the " forced labour on Sunday and during sickness "? Is it not reasonable that the natives should, in their own words, require one day's rest out of the week? "The treatment now is worse than it was before the country was under the British rule "—so says a chief—and he added that the natives do not get satisfaction from the magistrates. Dirty hospitals' the refusal to allow six men to return home, the buying and selling of any men who happeno have been in prison, coupled with an awful death rate, are given as the reasons why the natives shun the mines. The travelling facilities are execrable, and the evidence although conflicting in parts, is as a whole, irrefutable. Mr. Brownlee, who, in presenting his report on the condition of the natives in the mines together with the evidence upon which that report is founded said that the "treatment of natives is generally good." Mr. Herbert Samuel's comment on that is quite sufficient. He says, that "we shudder to think what it would be if it were generally bad." BRUTALITY TO SOUTH AFRICAN N TIVES. My next extract gives Major Seely's opinion on brutality to South African natives. Major Seely moved the adjournment of the House in accordance with the notice given at question time. He said that a sense of profound anxiety had been growing in this country as to whether there was any truth in the persistent rumours, allegations, and even statements that the natives in South Africa were being treated unfairly, improperly, and in some cases cruelly. Now at last an official document had been presented to the Cape Legislature. He read quotations from the reports of the Commis- sioners, some of whom were native chiefs, to the effect that sick men were forced to work, and were beaten and ill-treated tn, Zulu policemen; that the boakyeds of their overseers, and wished to the Republic when they were get back re to s better treated and better paid. One reported that if Inwitahme d the "boys" were fairly treated and paid £3 to £4 a month there would be no difficulty in getting suffi- cient supplies of native le,bour.(Opposition cheers.) The complaint was general that they were forced to work on Sundays, and all the Commissioners agreed that the wages paid were less than those promised. This state of affairs was not worthy of the great country to which we belonged, and required more than ordinary attention of the House and the Government. If they had known all that was in this Blue Book, mach that they had done in the last few months would never have been done. (Opposition cheers.) The high rate of mortality amongst these native miners-15 to 20 times greater than that among miners of the same age in this country—had caused great distrust amongst all mem- bers of the House. They now saw the whole sad story. There was a high rate of mortality before the war, and after it the mineowners determined to increase dividends by reducing wages and refusing to improve conditions. The consequence was a shortage of labour, and as a result of the war they were to find a short cut to avoid the necessary con- sequences of improper conditions for tne labourers. When we looked on the whole sordid tale, we must be ashamed of ourselves. (Opposition cheers.) He asked the Government to pause before they took a step which must perpetuate this shame. (Cheers.) THE STIMULUS OF COWHIDE. Mr. CHURCHILL seconding said the Blue Book was a complete justification for anyone who had voted against the proposal for the introduction of Chinese labour. (Cheers.) There were enough natives in South Africa to work all the goldtnines three or four times over, but they would not come, and the reason was to be found in this document. Mr. Chamberlain had done a good many things in his tenure of office for which a great many people had only been able to give him a modified measure of support, but in one thing he had the House entirely with him—he had greatly reduced the amount of flogging which took place all over the British Em- pire.He had strong personal views for good, which were a survival of other days. (Laughter.) He (gr. Churchill) was not sure that he would go quite as far as the right hon. gentleman, but there was one sort of corporal punishment which the House ought to conde un without the slightest reservation —that administered,not by authority or law, butat the caprice or will of any petty tyrant. (Opposi- tion cheers.) All this indiscriminate bullying and "stimulating of natives' movements by the gentle application of cowhide " of which the Blue Book I spoke' was a gross breach of criminal law, and ought to be visited by severe and exemplary punish- ment. (Hear, hear.) He made no general charge of inhumanity against the mineowners of the Rand, but what with the influence of the Labour Associa- tion, whose character was shown by the Blue Book in an unfavourable light, and the undoubted inal- 3ractices that went on in certain special localities, ohanneshorg and the Rand Mines Iva an extremely bad name among the natives. (Hear, hear.) They were tricked into their period of service, thrashed, made to work when they were ill, and not allowed to go to their friends even if they recovered, not allowed a day's rest in the week, and not paid for overtime. If that did not approach the nature of slavery a new name ought to be added to the English language. (Opposition cheers.) The Colonial Secretary had the reputation of being a

Liberal-minded man. He was once a Liberal. (Laughter.) He was now a Liberal Unionist, which Was not quite the same thing. (Laughter.) The right hon. gentleman did not want to signalise his tenure of office by any degradation of the standard of treatment of natives. Did he not think that the facts disclosed in the Blue Book constituted the case for action and for delay in some of the opera- tions which it was proposed to embark upon—(hear, hear)—and in handing over South Africa for the i;urpose of an experiment sinister in character and tar-reaching in its consequences. Might it not have been worth while for the right hon. gentleman to ask his officers to look beneath their own feet at what was going on in the Rand Mines? Might they not have found in the redress of grievances some solution of the question of the labour supply ' Without going all the way to China ? MR. BALFOUR'S ATTITUDE. 1 I have one more extract I would like to read to you on this question. Addressing a meeting of the Primrose League last night Mr. Balfour said: "Remember that these man, like ourselves, have British traditions, British love of liberty, British hatred of outrage and cruelty, British love of freedom. He would be an insolent and arrogant critic indeed who could suggest that we have a right to take up on any of the great branches of public morality a position of superiority over our fellow-countrymen over the sea." I believe the statements I have read to you are true statements. This is a subject which concerns very closely every Englishman, whether capitalist or wage earner, and especially it concerns the wage earner, and I have one or two questions upon it I want to put to this meeting. Is it honest to promis3 a wage and theq pay less ?-- (No.) Is it humane to flog the natives with cowhide and sjomboks ?—(No.) Should the truck system, which is act legal in this country,be allowed in the Compounds of South Africa?—(No.) We, as a Christian nation, abolished Sunday work and yet compel the natives in South Africa to work seven days a weak. Is that fair? —(Renewed crieg of "No.") I am sure if they tried that sort of game a little nearer home they would soon have to answer for it. (L tughter and applause.) Mr. Balfour is doing his very best to endorse all that these men are doing. The Gayer:l:neat accept the advice of Lord Milner and follow him through all this mis- chievous work, just as they followed him through all the mischievous work in 1839. For my part I urn not going to regard South African affairs through the spectacles of Lord IVlilner. (Hear, hear.) There is too much of the Germaia bureaucrat about that gentleman ; he persuades himself that the public cannot possibly be opposed to anything of which he himself approves, and I am perfectly sick of all the rot that is talked in the yellow press of LOnclon about kow-towing to this great pro-consul. (Cheers.) I think we have had enough of it. (Cheers.) If the Govern- ment are always going to accept the course laid down by the man on the spot, and do whatever he wants, we shall never be free from war in this Empire. The Government must accept their responsibilities as rulers and they must forbid people to go on the bucaneer- ing and plundering expeditions in one part of the Empire, or introduce pernicious customs into another, which simply mean the tearing up of the legacy which Wilberforce has handed down to us. (Hear, hear.) We have had a splendid letter in the local press from Mr. Parker, and he has reminded us how oar liberties have been fought for,and 'won inch by inch,and how proud we ought to be that England is allead of other nations in this respect.ll(flear, hear.) Snrely it is the duty of every Englishman. to set his face resolutely against such work as this in South Africa. (Applause and Cheers). .

THE LICENSING QUESTION. That is the most important matter I have to deal with to-night, but before sitting d3wn I have a little bit from the Manchester "Guardian" on the Licensing Question I think will interest you. It runs "So far from reduction beim., made easier under the compensation scheme, it will become more diffi- cult. Mr. Alfred Davies in his pamphlet calculates that evAn if the levy reach the maximum of 21,000.000 it would take fifty or seventy-five years to effe3t the reduction of one-fourth recommended by the Minority Report of the Licensing Commis- sion. Voluntary surrender of licences would cease altogether. It does not even make it incumbent on Quarter Sessions to reduce licenses at all, although the Majority Report of the Commission agreed that the number of licenses is far in excess of public requirements. It says that the reduction shall not go on faster than the size of the compensation fund warrants it makes the amount of compensation determine the amount of reduction, and while it establishes a maximum it insists on no minimum. It takes away from local justices these powers which can bast be exercised in the light of local knowledge, aqd leaves them the power of deciding whether or not a breach of the law has been committed which needs no local knowledge. It pays compen- sation on the market value of a licence and assdsses contributions on the rating value, which is much lower, and the difference will tend to retard the rate of reduction. It makes a vast present to the trade under the pretence of making a reduction of licences easier, and every clause in the Bill makes reduction more difficult. It serves a tem- porary convenience of the Government at 'tthe cost of restricting for all time the liberty of the State in its power to regulate and control the liquor traffic. When people talk of compensation they think not of brewers but of publicans. But this is a brewers'—not a publicans' bill. Barmen and bar- maids who lose their situations receive no compen- sation."

I call that a vary good summary of what the Bill affects, and that quotation brings home to us the fact that this is not a Bill for the publican but a Bill for the brewer. (Hear, hear.) And I think it is about time that some of the publicans, who are so much tha tools of the brewers,should reld the little Bill introduced by Mr. Broadhurst and Sir John Brunner for the Abolition of Tied Houses. (Hear, hear.)

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

Minor Cases. Warrington Borough Police Court.

SATURDAY—Before Mess. Birchall (in the chair), Hayward, R. T. Fairclough, and Geddes. RETURNING FROM THE RACES.—Thomas O'Neil, hawker, of no fixed residence, was charged with having travelled on the London and North-Western Railway, by the 3 p.m. train from Chester to Warrington on Friday without a ticket with intent to avoid payment. —Ticket-collector Blinston stated that prisoner came up to the barrier at Bank Quay Station, and when asked for his ticket said he had not come by train. He could not produce a platform ticket, and as he had no money with which to pay his fare he was handed over to Detective Davies.—Superintendent Monaghan said the prisoner was a stranger and had evidently been to Chester races.—Prisoner, who pleaded guilty, was tined 20s. and costs, with the alternative of a mouth's imprisonment. IVfoNDAY.—Before the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, Councillor Thornton, Messrs. Birchall and Wright.

GOOD EFFECTS OF A REMAND.—Patrick Shaugh- nessy, of Eldon-street, again appeared in the dock,after having spent a week on remand at Strangeways, to answer to the charge of attempting to commit suicide. Superintendent Monaghan stated that on Saturday,April 30th, prisoner came home from work at one o'clock. After having dinner he went out and got some drink. On returning home he had more drink and began to quarrel with his wife about a County Court summons. Later in the evening he went into an out- building and was found by his wife suspended from a beam by a piece of wire. He was taken down and handed over to the police. He had been in gaol a week and appeared to be much better.—Prisoner promised the magistrates that he would never do such a thing again, and he was discharged. DRUNK WHILST IN CHARGE OF A CHILD.—Kate Dagnall was charged with having been drunk whilst in charge of a child two years of age in Mersey-street on Saturday night.—P.C. Cox said prisoner was staggering about with the child, which was almost naked, in her arms. Sherwas so drunk that she fell down.—Prisoner said she had been to her brother-in-law's wedding and had had drink.—She was fined ss. and costs.

MARGARET OAKES AGAlN.—Margaret Oakes, a well- known character, and William Makinson, who made his first apertrance, were charged with having been drunk •and disorderly in Brick-street at 12.45 a.m. on Sunday. —P.C. Cooper (26) proved the case.—Oakes was sent to gaol for a month and Makinson was fined ss. and costs. DISORDER IN TETE STREETS.—For playing football in Hoult-street on May 3rd the following boys were lined 3s. Gd. each :—Andrew Leah Traynor, 7, Dolman's- Jane; John O'Day, 19A., Back Dolman's-lane; William 'Conway, 29, Dolman's-lane; John Murphy, 6, Dohnan's- lane ; John Egan, 19, Bank-street; Thomas Collins, 4, Stoney-vard ; and William Mason, 17, Ship-yard.— P.C.' s elough and Andrews proved the case.and said that frequent complaints were made about such con- duct.—A fine of 3s. Gd. each was imposed upon two other boys named Thomas Hardman, 17, Lord Nelson- street, and Ernest Warburton, 6, Porter-street, for having made loud and unseemly noises in Napier-street at 7.30 on Sunday night, May Ist. MINOR OFFENCES.—For making use of obscene language, Michael Murray, 14 Silver-street, and John Laydon, 6, Back Haydock-street, were fined 10s. and -costs each.—A. fine of 4sAd. was imposed upon Mary Loydon, 15, flaydock-street, for disorderly behaviour in that street on April 30th.—John Preece, butcher, 33, "Winwick-street, was fined Is. for allowing the chimney of his house to be on fire.—James Mack,3s, West-street. -and William Dutton, 7, Morris'-yard were summoned, for committing a breach of the peace by fighting in Winwick-road on April 30th. They were ordered to pay the cost of the summons (3s. 6d. each.) TUESDAY.—Before Aldermen Sutton (in the chair), and Hutchinson. and Mr. J. Rymer Young. SLEEPING OUT.—Two boys, named Charles William Darnburu, 106, Field-street, Liverpool, and Oliver Fuller 20, Holly-street, Weaste were charged with having slept out the previous night in Cheshire's-yarch—Superintend- .ent Monaghan said he would withdraw the charge, on condition that the boys were handed over to the Police 'Court Missionary, and by him sent home. The magis- trates consented to this course.

ALLEGED ILL-TREITMENT OF N CHlLD.—James Crank was charged on a warrant with neglecting and ill-treating his child, Alice May Crank, aged 22 months on May 2nd and other dates.—Mr. Browne' on behalf of the N.S-P.C.C., said prisoner had only been arrested that morning, and consequently the witnesses were not in attendance —A remand until to-day was granted. Daresbury •Petty Sessions.

.TUESDAY.—Before Messrs. William Long (in the chair), Henry Nield, J. Malin Edelsten and Henry Talbot. A CASE THAT FAILED.—ThOMEIS Mather, 66, Winwick- .street, Warrington, was summoned for having been drunk on the licensed premises of George Simcock, Dog and Dart Inn, Grappenhall,on May 4,1902.—He pleaded not guilty.—P.C. Williams stated that on the night in ques- tion he was in Knutsford-road, Grappenhall, and saw the defendant, who was drunk, going in the direction of the Dog and Dart. Thinking that he might go into the house he followed him. He went into the inn and wit- ness followed and was just in time to see him standing against the bar, the landlord's daughter having refused to serve him. He asked defendant for his name and address which he gave as Thomas Moss, 34, Folly-lane, Warrington. Witness spent two afternoons in trying to serve the summons, but could not find any trace of the defendant. On Wednesday week last he saw the defendant working at some houses near Grappenhall and recognized him again.—Prisoner said he had never been in the Dog and Dart and knew nothing about the matter.—Miss Simcock, daughter of the licensee, said

she could not identify the defendant. —The constable said that when he served defendant with the summons he admitted the offence and asked him not to be "too hard on him at Daresbury."—Defendant said he did not! say anything of the kind, and he strongly denied that he was the man drunk at the Dog and Dart as he had never been there.—The magistrates dismissed the case.' A STOCKTON HEATH OUTING AND ITS SEQUEL.—Joe Lightburn, butcher, 55, Bridgemann-street, Bolton, was summoned for placing broken glass on the roadway at Stockton Heath on AprillBth.—Sergeant Bowyer stated that on tlp day in question be saw a cab coming over the Swing Btidge. When it got on the Stockton Heath side the defendant put his hand out of the window and dropped a beer bottle on to the floor. A piece of the glass cut a little girl's leg. He followed the cab to the Mulberry Tree Hotel and found that there were several other men in the cab "mad drunk." Defendant gave the name of William Partington, but witness afterwards ascertained his real name.—Defendant said he did not throw the bottle out of the window. It must have been someone else.—The Sergeant further stated that the defendant, with some friends, came driving out from Bolton, and sent a telegram to his wife at Bolton Joe dead, come at once." The result was; that the wife came from Bolton to Stockton Heath, and it was while making inquiries into the matter that wit- ness saw the offence under notice. Defendant behaved in such a•manner that he had to be locked up at the time and he had since been fined for drunkenness.—A young man named David Walsh, who was in the cab at the , time and who said he was quite sober, said that it was not defendant who threw the bottle.—Defendant was ordered to pay the costs (4s. 6d.) A REMARKABLE CASE.

Physicians declare that Diabetes is incurable and yet Mrs. Eliza Watkins, of 109, London-road, Clapton, N.E., whose family physician diagnosed her case as one of Diabetes, has been entirely restored. The doctor put Mrs. Watkins under a special Diabetes diet, and from all her symptoms there can be no doubt but that she had this awful disease. Dodd's Kidney Pills cured her, and to-day she says :—" The distressing symptoms of Diabetes have entirely gone." Her letter reads as follows :

"Three years ago I became very ill and had to take to my bed. My regular doctor said I had Diabetes,- and put me on a spezial diet. I was under his treat ment for some time, but got no better, and on account of my age (I was then 72), I was naturally very much concerned. A friend advised me to use Dodd's Kidney Pills, and, in the course of a few weeks, I became aware of a decided change for the batter and was able to get up and about. I continued to take the Pills, having reduced the dose to one or two Pills per day, and I very soon discovered that the old distressing symptoms of Diabetes had entirely gone. " My friends and relatives were much surprised at the rapid change and I can truthfully attribute my remark- able recovery solely to the use of Dodd's Kidney Pills."

OUR LOCAL CHURCHEI

Padgate Wesleyan Church.

The Rev. A. Freeman (formerly minister of the War- rington circuit), preached the annual Sunday School sermons in this church on Sunday when there were large congregations. In the afternoon the Rev. John Gunnell conducted a flower service and gave an address on "Lilies." Lovely bunches of spring flowers were brought by the children, and these were placed by four of the teachers in a frame which had been prepared near the pulpit, and the mass of bloom presented a very pretty appearance. Solos were rendered by Miss Lena Duckworth and Miss Lilian Wright. The flowers were, on Monday, forwarded to the workhouse infirmary. Special hymns were sung by the choir and the children, who lad been admirably trained by Mr. Councillor Brough, assisted by Dr. Bennett. Miss C. J. Bennett presided at the organ. The offertories amounted to £2l.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
Services for the People.

The Rev. H. Dunnico preached at the Parr Hall on Sunday evening, taking his text from Luke 9 xxiv., d "Whosoever shall save his life shall lose it, an whoso- ever shall lose his life for My sake, the same shall save it." The meaning and purpose of life, the preacher said, could best be found out by doing the work that was nearest. He warned his congregation against three types of people, viz., the critic, the theorist, and the sentimentalist. It was not these, but steady, faithful workers who were wanted. Our lives were given to us to develop to the fullest extent in order that they might become useful to other people. A belief in God and in immortality was necessary to life's highest development. Everywhere in history it was seen that the best and noblest men were those who possessed an intense faith in God and the life beyond. In the long run no life was so fruitful as the life of sacrifice and the life spent in the service of God.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was a smaller attendance than usual at the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sunday. Mr. C. Cotton presided and briefly addressed the meeting. An address was delivered by Mr. Wharton, of Manchester, based on the text "Lord what is man?" If they analysed the human body he said, they would find it to consist of like elements to the sun and stars ; every man, in fact, was a little universe in himself. In one sense man was the noblest object in creation; formed in the likeness of God and glorious in the range of his faculties and the height of his aspirations. Man had an inheritance, qualifying for companionship not only with angels but with God him- self. With such great po.sibilities before them, the speaker asked his hearers to be inspited with nobler ambition and to rise to full manhood, living lives worthy of their high calling. Miss Lucy Travis very sweetly sang as a solo the hymn "Home at last; thy labour done."—Mr. B. Farrar, the hon. secretary, appealed for a generous collection, on behalf of the Benevolent Fund, on which there had been many calls of late.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOCKING COLLIERY ACCIDENT AT HAYDOCK.

Inquest and Verdict.

The inquest on Patrick Holmes, single, aged twenty- one, of 236, Blackbrook-road, Haydock, who was killed on Friday morning at the Princess Pit, belonging to Messrs. R. Evans and Co., Haydock, was held at the Waggon Hotel on Monday night. Mr. Brighouse read the report of the accident, and said that Holmes was employed to hook on and receive boxes at the bottom of a steep jig brow. A lad named Joht, Roberts,aged sixteen, was sent to help him. About half-past eight in the morning the two got nine empty boxes together, and Roberts coupled them all up and told Holmes that he had fastened the journey to the motion rope,ready for them to go up the brow. Roberts then signalled to Carmichml, the man at the jig-wheel at the top of the brow, and he removed the scotches from the wheels of eight full boxes standing at the top of the brow, and they rushed down and killed deceased. The empty boxes apparently had not been hooked to the boxes. The Coroner said Mr. Matthews, the Government inspector, had written to him stating that he had made experiments, and could not see how the jig rope could have got loose from the boxes if the hook had been properly put on the box. The only thing he could suggest was that the management should have a manhole or refuge, where the men could safely stand all the time the journeys were in motion.—John Roberts, aged sixteen, of 61, Station road, Haydock, said he had worked in a Darwen mine drawing coal, but Friday was only his second day at Haydock. He was certain that he put the hook of the haulage rope on to the ring of the first box. He had no doubt about it, but the haul- age rope went up by itself,and although witness shouted for the jigger man to stop, the full boxes all over the place, and jammed deceased's head against a prop, splitting it in two.—ln answer to Mr. W. Hutchen, who appeared for the relatives, witness said deceased usually went with him to the rope to see that it was properly fastened,but he was not clear that he did on this occasion John Cawfield, the fireman, said he showed Roberts how to do his work, and put three journeys on before he left him. If witness had been working in Holmes's place he would not have trusted to a strange lad to see that the boxes were hooked on. He told Holmes to go with the lad every time, and see that he hooked it on properly.— The Coroner said he was afraid they must conclude that Roberts did not couple the boxes on to the rope. It looked as if he had forgotten, because Mr. Matthews did not think it possible for the chain to come loose.— The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and exonerated everybody from blame.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIDNES ESTIMATES.

The estimates for the year were presented at the Widnes Town Council meeting on Tuesday. They are as follow :—Highway Committee, £3,516 (increase £18) ; Health Committee, £6,061 (increase £190); General Purposes Committee, £2Ol (increase £3.1); Park and Cemetery Committee, £1,415 (decrease £1) : and Finance Committee, £6,150 (increase £1,783).

SUDDEN DEATHS AT WIDNES. Mary Ellen Wormll, aged forty-nine, wife of William Worrall, Barn-street, Widnes, died in a cab on Sunday evening. She had been in the Shaw-strePt Hospital, and was coming home with some friends. She became ill, and was taken to Dr. M'Lennan, who pronounced life extinct.—Griffen Roberts, aged fifty-five, residing in Major Cross-street, Widnes, expired with alarming suddenness on Saturday night or in the early hours of Sunday morning. At 11.30 he ate a hearty supper of oxtail soup. and retired to bed with his wife. Mrs. Roberts tried to awaken him at 9.30 on Sunday morn- ing. On looking at his face she nottced something wrong, and on getting out of bed found that her husband had expired in the night. WIDNES MAGISTRATES AND THE PRICE OF BEER.

During the progress of the Widnes Police Co-uit--,--on Monday, the magistrates adjourned to consider a letter from the secretary of the Widnes and District Licensed Victuallers' Association regarding the prices submitted by them, and adopted by the magistrates at the last brewster sessions. In it there was a complaint respect- ing licensees who were not at present adhering to the prices, and thus injuring those who were follow- ing the rules from the outset. The. magistrates, after a long discussion, adjourned the bearing: This is a very important matter, and one that is causing much friction amongst the publicans of the town.

MR. GROSFIELD ADDRESSES LIBERAL WORKERS.

THE LAST OF THE SERIES OF WARD MEETINGS.

The Liberal Cancliclature at the Next Election.

Mr. Arthur H. Crosfleld completed his itinerary of the wards on Saturday when the last of the series of ward committee meetings was held. All the Liberal ward committees have now met and appointed their officers. A joint meeting of the Bewsey and Orford Ward Committees was held at the Liberal Club on Saturday. Tea was served, the catering being under- taken hy Mr. Councillor James Evans. An adjournment was afterwards made by the two committees to separate rooms. After the usual business had been transacted, a meeting was heal in the Reading Room, when addresses were delivered by Mr. Crosfield and Mi. C. E. Parker. Mr. Councillor A. PEICOCK was voted to the chair and in the course of his introductory remarks, said that at the meeting earlier that evening they had elected their officers and committee, and everything had gone off in the most satisfactory manner. (Hear, hear.) They had decided to make another addition to their pro- gramme, as they were thinking of holding meetings in the club every month or six weeks to discuss political or municipal matters or whatever might be "on the carpet " at the time. Members would be invited to bring forward any subject they thought fit for discus- sion, so that they might improve themselves on all matters, especially thase affecting Liberal principles. (Applause.) MR. CROSPIELD ROVIEWS THE SITUATION. Mr. A. H. CROSFEELD, who had a hearty reception, said :—I have delivered a good rnanyspeeches lately,and. I don't want to frighten you by pulling out this big bundle of extracts from my pocket, but I am very glad indeed to have the pleasure of speaking to a meeting like this because it is pleasant when you know you are talking amongst friends. (Cheers). This,' suppose,will be about the last meeting we shallhave this political season. 11 will not attempt to make a resum4 of all that we have I done this last autumn and winter,but looking back over this last few months I think we may fairly say that we have made good progress, and that the prospects of our party are brighter than they have been in Warrington for a very long time past. (Hear,hear). There wasa time when one often heard it said that there , was a lack of political educ,ation,and that there was not enough public speaking and political instruction given by the Liberals in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Well, that is a very serious matter, and I am afraid that some time back there may have been some ground for the complaint. But I would respectfully put it to you that if you take the last autumn and the last winter there has been no room for any more complaints of that kind. (Hear, hear.) You have had from the pen of our President many letters on important subjects as they have arisen, letters that have been edifying, full of information and going to the very root of these subjects. Then we have had several admirable addresses delivered to us, such as the statesmanlike speech by Lord Crewe—(cheers)—and those able and powerful addresses with which from time to time we are favoured by Mr. Broadhurst—(cheers),— and last, but by no means least, we have had several mostimportant and most inspiring addresses from our friend Mr. Howes. (Renewed cheers.) I really think that apart from the eloquence and power that you get from both Mr. Broadhurst and Mr. Howes—although my recollection goes back to the early 80's—I must say I have not heard humour on a Warrington platform that is equal to what we have had from these gentle:net., and I don't think you could get it unless you went back to the days of dear old Mr. McMinnies himself. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19040514_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
A TALE WITH AN ANALOGY.

One of my earliest recollections is the tale he told, which I am sure a good many here will not have for- gotten, of those two chaps from Oldham who went to South Africa to gget lion cubs for their sweethearts. (Laughter.) When they got to the cave and saw the lioness walking away from it, Bill said to Tom, "Do thee go in and get those cubs and I'll keep watch." So Tom went into the cave to get the cubs. And then to the horror and consternation of his friend Bill, who was outside, the lioness suddenly turned round and went back to the cave. Bill, not knowing what to do, but anxious to save his friend, rushed up and seized the lioness by the tail as she was entering the cave, and then planting his left foot on one side and his right foot on the other side of the cave held on like grim death. (Laughter.) Tom, who was busy with the cubs, didn't see this, but he called out to his mate, " Bill, wots dark'nin' th' leet ? " " Thou'lt know wots darienip' th' leet," said Bill," if tail breaks." (Loud laughter.) I don't think since that immortal tale was told by Mr. McMinnies, that we have had anything better than some of the tales we have had from Mr. Broadhurst and Mr. 'Howes. lam not sure whether there is not some analogy between that tale of Mr. McMinnies, and the present state of affairs, if you compare the flimsy hold that the Government has upon the country to that lioness's tail—(laughter)—and the destruction that threatened poor Tom, with the awful day of reckoning which is in store for this misguided Government. (Cheers and laughter.) What I have asked at other meetines, and what I venture to ask this meet- ingis,whether Warrington.after a long reign of Toryism does not consider it is quite time for us to take a share in the work that is going to be done throughout the country. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19040514_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE TRADE.

Looking back over the subjects that we have dis- cussed in the press and on the platform pretty thoroughly this last season. first and foremost comes Free Trade. (Hear, hear.) This is certain to be the chief issue at the next election—this great question whether we are t 3 stick to Free Trade and cheap food, or whether we are to be humbugged and mystified with the old played out fallacies of Protection. Cheers.) ,And there is some danger in Warrington of this taking place because interested manufacturers have been making strong. appeals to their workpeople in favour of Protection And in these appeals they have been careful to refer to the ten per cent tax on imported manufactured articles,while leaving in the background the concomitant duty thAt will have to be put upon food for this very good reason, that the Tory party will never stand ta..ation of manufactured goods unless the great agricultural interest, which is the back-b4ne of that party, is similarly treated. (Hear, hear.) I want you to remember that the home trade is about eight times as great as our export trade, and that .if you diminish the purchasing power of every sovereign, and make it worth only 17s. ad. you are going to decrease the de- mand for manufactures, which nitians decreased demand for labour. (Hear, hear.) , The question may be com- plex in some respects. but we must bear well in mind the very simple issue I have put before you, and vote very straight upon that issue when the time comes. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19040514_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION.

Another very important question which will affect the next election is of course education, and I am sure you all respect as I .do the candid and cogent reasoning of the letters which have been written by Mr. Charles E. Parker upon that subject. (Hear, hear.) The other day a friend of mine gave a dinner in London in honour of the Speaker of the House of Commons. I had the privilege of attend- ing, and a very interesting dinner it was. All shades of- political opinion were represented, Free Traders, Pro tectionists, Irish Members and so on. I had the pleasur of sitting next to Major Rasch, and he was telling me of a trip he paid to Russia not long since. While he was in St. Petersburg a friend suggested that he should visit certain places there. Major Ra.sch could not remember the names and asked his friend to write them down so that he could give them to the coachman. "Do you suppose," replied his friend "there is one coachman in St. Petersburg to-day, who can either read or write !" Is not that significant? (Hear, hear.) Does not this little story illustrate so well the procrress that is being made by some nations, and the retrograde position of others ; and it struck me too, as it may strike you, how much such a nation is handicapped if it comes to the grips with another that is go-ahead and enterprising, like the Japanese who put education in the forefrodt of their national work. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19040514_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE UNIONISM.

1 In our discussions we have also dealt with another matter of great and vital importance indirectly to every workingman, and directly to every Trade Unionist,—l ' refer to the Taff Vale decision. (Cheers.) On this sub- ject I believe there is absolute unanimity lathe Liberal Party—(cheers)—and that is one of the healthiest and I most encouraging signs in the present position. It must honestly appeal to every workingman in Warring- ton to see that the right of combination possessed by all employers, is safeguarded and confirmed for the employ4s who require what is so absolutely just, a..d that is equality before the law. (Loud cheers.) With such a position on such a matter I think we Liberals who have had an uphill battle to fight for many a year in Warrington, and those of us who have taken a leading and responsible position in the party, may very well appeal to trade unionists to give us a very strong and determined backing on a question of that kind. (Cheers.) I will just refer for one moment to what John Morley said the other day. He said "Those people must be very blind indeed who do not recognise that now the country is waking up; now that these Jingo mists which have been so illusory are clearing away, labour is going to take a much stronger and much more prominent part in this country's affairs than it has done hitherto." (Hear, hear.) I for one welcome the fact that labour is going to do so. (Cheers.) What makes one look forward with the more confidence and zeal towards the political work ahead of us in the future is the very fact that such admirable men have been chosen as Labour candidates, and that for the most part they show such good judgment and staunch fidelity to sound Liberal principles in giving a lead to the democracy. We may well say of such men that they realise and put "Beinto praotice John Bright's memorable aphorism, moderate in your strength, and strong in your modera- tion." (Hear, hear.) MAGNIFICE'N'T CASES- TO FIGHT FOR.

This me-e-Cin-g-, as I have said already, is the last of the series of ward meetings we have been holding. During this last Autumn and Winter we have covered a good deal of political ground. (Hear, hear.) We do not know when we are going to be in the thick of the battle again, but we do feel that we shall go as one man when the fight does come. (Hear, hear and a voice: "I hope so.") We have half a dozen magni- ficent causes , to fight for, any one of which is good enough to rally, us together and make us put our backs into the work. (fur case is over- whelming, we have justice on our side—(hear, hear)— and we are fighting to give the people of this country opportunities to rise higher and higher in the scale of civilised life. (Cheers.) There is nothing to deter us in the impending conflict. We have grand causes to strive for and inspire us; the work before us is as fine a work as any Crusader in the old days ever undertook, and we only want the thoughtful citizens of our town to put their energy into the work to secure victory. (Cheers.) We want men in every workshop and every factory to help us, to study the letters our President writes to attend to the utterances of the public then who have been down to help us, and who are coming down again, for we have not done yet--(hear, hear)—and to use their influence to further the cause of Progress. (Hear, hear.) The questions for which we are fighting are not mere academic abstractions, but the real issues that affect your own lives and the lives of your own households. With that help we can be confident of the result when the time comes. (Hear, hear). In conclusion, let me say, as I have said to the other Cap- tains of the wards whom I have addressed at these meetings, that it is a great pleasure to me to come into touch with those stalwarts of our party who have held together through the most trying time Liberals have ever known in the recollection of any living man, and that is during the War fever of 1900. (Hear, hear). It is a great privilege to meet men who have shown such fidelity to their principles. (Hear, hear). I cannot drink a toast to you, but I will propose one and that is "Long happiness." I wish you all that in your own privat lives, and whether I continue to play an active part in Warrington politics or not, I do trust every one here will do his best, as I shall to, as John Morley so grandly puts it, "help to swell the tide of opinion, on the force and set of whose current depends the success- ful voyaging of humanity. (Loud cheers.) Mr. C. E. PA.RICOR said they must recognise that the impending conflict was going to be a soldiers' battle, and if the battle was going to be won, it would not be won by the officers but by the rank and file. (Hear, hear.) He supposed they held somewhat supporting views to Mr. Crosfleld in the admirable address he had given them, and he was sure they were glad to recog- nise the increasing force and power of Mr. Crosfleld's addresses. This augured well for the future. In his admirable address Mr.Crosfield had referred in a far too complimentary way to the few letters that he (Mr. Parker) had written to the Warrington press. He wrote several on the subject of education because it was a subject in which he was deeply interested, and whatever new question came to the front, and whatever blunders the Government Commit d, this important question of education must not be lost sight of. They must lay it down as a cardinal principle that inasmuch as the education of the people had been taken over by the people, that the people should have control of that education. ((fear' hear.) It followed from that principle that there should be no bar to their children entering the teaching profession, and becoming in so doing, servants of the State, providing they were educationally fit and competent. (Hear, hear.) He wrote also several letters upon the fiscal question. It was a sabject he had taken a very deep interest in. not lately only, but all through his life. He had studied the question very closely and he had come to the con- clusion long ago' which was confirmed by recent study and inquiry that the Free Trade principle was undoubtedly the best for the country and especially for the general body of the people. (Hear, hear). His wife had had a joke against him for two or three months. When the sugar bounty was abolished, the removal of which practically took away the advantage we got in sugar, whereby foreign countries gave us a present of about one halfpenny a pound—he thought he would follow up his convictions and he bought two or three cwts of sugar, with the idea that the price of sugar was sure to go up. They stored the suer away in the attic and his wife had been laughing at him ever since because the price had not gone up. Fortunately for him the laugh was now turned against her, for he saw from to-day's news- papers that the grocers had met together, and from the fact that they Were making no profit on the sale of sugar, they had decided to put the price up one farthing a pound. fie could no

see how that farthing would be regulated in the salt% of sugar, because a farthing was not a current coin witf us. The probability was, as it generally was in cases? that kind, that poor people who bought stnall quarl„t). ties, would have to pay an additional halfpenny* Howes told them at a meeting held in Friars drool' schoolroom how the extra duty on tea of twopence Pr lb. would be the means of increasing the price of tea te the poor by fivepence per lb. Instead of poor people being able to get a one ounce packet of tea for a peefl! they would in future only get of an ounce for a peeeY and that would mean a loss to them equal to sd. per lb. He thought arguments of this sort would come honle to the people much more directly than letters in the newspapers. (Hear, hear.) He (Mr. Parker) wrote several letters about the South African question and he was very plad to recognise, as they all must have done, the very earnest and convincing ring that there was about Mr. Crosfield's advocacy of this question. (fle3r hear.) We were beginning to see what the real motive' of the capitalists in South Africa were in desiring' Chinese labour. There were two extracts in his (.14r. Parker's) letters, and which Mr. Howes referred to that other night, in which he showed how two directors ~tel one of the important companies of the Rand, gave,:, their reason for objecting to the employment of whites labour in the mines,that the political power which theyba' at present in the Transvaal would passifrom them by the exercise of the vote into the hands of the white labonrera (Hear, hear.) That had been, and was still one of tb! strongest reasons—and they acknowledged it themselve' —of objecting to the employment of white men. (Elelt hear). It was unnecessary.for him to go further. had been a very pleasing thing for him to attend the various meetings they had had. He would like to saY one word about Mr. Crosfield's position in regard a' their association and in rdgard to the Liberal candidature of this town. He thought they all recognised that the/ were under an obligation to Mr. Crosfield for the Poel" tion that he had taken. While Mr. Crosfield would be, as he had told them, very proud to fight their battle had all along said that if a better man could be brought' forward he was willing to step aside an I give all his influence and strength to backing that leis But he (Mr. Parker) felt—and he thought theYo would also feel—that the time was coming Wheli they should not allow Mr. Crosfield to be placed nlue , longer in an indefinite position. The time was coin% ' when it was desirable that the executive should tam' action in the matter to put Mr. Crosfield in a LW! I definite position. He should not like, when the 01147 came, to press the claims of Mr. Crosfield. That gun be left entirely for the Association to decide, but if "" might express his own opinion, he should like to slisr that as he had got to know Mr. Crosfield more intimate during the last twelve months' his opinion of him ha.. been considerably heightened and if the Associatioii decided to ask Mr. Crosfield to become their candidate they would have in him a man, whom everyone of the, would have reason to b 3 proud and have reason to thre" themselves with energy into the work. (Applause.) .4.. A NUMBER : We have no other man who can will 1" He will win this next time. (Applause.) Mr. PARKER.: I think he will. (Hear, hear.) Coe; tinning he said their friend had just put one though,: into his head and it must be a closing one. In tht:ci fight they must go in to win. (Elear,hear.) If they h t that intention before thorn, and put forward all thiL was in their power to accomplish it, then he was certai" I that Warrington could be won for Liberalism. (Alr plause.) Mr. Parker then proposed a vote of thanks Le I the Chairman. Mr. Councillor WILKINSON seconded and the reseW tion was carried. The CHAIRMAN, in acknowledging the complimeet: said there were many encouraging signs in connectie' with Mr.Crosfleld's candidature. Only the other daY„11! was told bya Conservative that he intended to vote le' Crosfield on the next occasion. "What has Mr. ritero point done for us ?" asked this Conservative. 14. never does anything for us. He is merely an ornartee" A man like Crosfield will certainly do somethiak (Hear, hear.) II ~...LIDERAL MEETING AT WIDNES. . -10er. -'• Wad Deacon resided over a meeting of ‘,....T ti;"l!ibli,:_6 I,,yaidnes on Tuesday evening, when Sir :John t veyCandidate for the Widnes fthZki°,ll", ea'aCji I\i, prospective organising secretary of :4411.-"cletl Club rand eax-.M.P. Mr. William Long, J.P., presided at the annual meeting of the Warrington branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Thursday afternoon. There was a good attendance, including Miss England, of the Central Office of the Society, the Revs. Canon Willis and John Yonge,Messrs. W. H. J. Jenkins, A. 11. Lee, J. D. Buckton, McGregor, and W. D. Jeans, jun. (hon. sec.), and many ladies who interest themselves in the society's work. The Hon. Secretary announced the receipt of letters of apology for non-attendance from the president (Sir Gilbert Greenall), the Mayor, Mr. Coxon, and others. It was intimated that Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall wottld be willing to again act in any offices to which they might be nominated.

THE YEAR'S WORK.

The twelfth annual report was presented by the SECRETARY as follows :—The committee have again to report a decrease in the number of cases dealt with in the district during the year ended 31st March 1904. The total number of cases investigated during this period was 284 affecting 867 children of whom 412.4 were boys and 443 were girls. In 236 cases warnings were given, 21 were prosecuted, 18 were dropped, and 9 were other- wise disposed of. The classification of cases was as follows :—Neglect,243; illtreatment. 20; causing to beg, 7; exposure, 6; immoral surroundings, 2; abandon- ment. 1; criminal assault, 1 ; other wrongs, 4. The prosecutions which took place as above stated in 21 cases had the followinc, results :—Eleven persons were sent to prison,2 were fined,l was bound over,and the cases against 12 persons were adjourned by the magis- trates, to eive themthe opportunity of making improve- ments- In most of these cases improvements resulted. No cases have been dismissed. 1566 supervision visits have been made during the year. There are at present upwards of 400 houses under the Inspector's supervision. This diminution in the number of cases has been caused in part by a further reduction in the area of the branch, a separate branch having been created in January last for the Widnes, Runcorn and Frodsham district. This relieves ourlnspector of a consider tble amount of work and will permit of his paying more attention to the colliery district north of the town which requires his attention very frequently. The reduction in the num- ber of cases in the Borough of Warrington is believed to be partly the result of the activity of the Sanitary Authorities in Warrington. There is now and has been for nearly two years a Lady Inspector who is exclusively employed in visiting the houses of the poorer class and our Inspector finds that she has suc- ceeded in bringing about a very general improvement. The Committee are pleased to be able to report a suc- cessful financial year. In spite of the loss of the bulk of the subscribers from the Widnes, Runcorn and Frod- sham district there is a surplus of £5B 12s. 7d., as against £54 14s. last year. The annual ball was again very successful d Mrs. Frost was able to band over from that sourc7 a sum of £4B 17s. 4d. to the Society's funds. The Committee take the opportunity of acknow- ledging their appreciation of Mrs. Frost's good services in this connection. Mass Broadbent has continued to take an active interest in the branch of the Children's League of Pity which has resulted inthe addition of £24 2s. Bd. to the funds. Inspector Hackett's assiduity to his work is maintained and the Committee are well satisfied with it. A note- worthy event during the year has been the passing. of the Act for regulating the employment of children and the issuing of bye-laws under that Act by the Corpora- tion regulating the employment of children in street trading. A considerable amount of work hitherto done by our Inspector in connection with children so employed will henceforth be done by the police. The police in all parts of the district have given the Inspec- tor every assistance and the Director of Education in Warrington the school officers and many of the school masters and mistresses have also given valuable help. There is considerable distress in the town and the Inspector will be glad to receive cast off clothing &c. for distribution among deserving cases. THE FINANCES.

It was reported that be total contributions to the funds of the branch during the year amounted to £265 10s. 5d., and after payment of expenses there was a balance of £5B 12s. 7d. to be devoted to the national work. Although the subscriptions had decreased some- what, largely owing to the formation of thenew branch, the amount to be handed over showed an increase, last year's contribution being £54 14s. The Secretary said economies had been effected in many ways, and as they had not had as many prosecutions as usual, their legal expenses were not so heavy. On the motion of the Rev. JOHN YONGE, seconded by Mr.W. H. J. JENKINS, the report and balance sheet were adopted.

ELECTIONS.

Sir Gilbert Greenall was re-elected presirlent,and the following vice-presidents were elected :—Lady Green- all, Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., Mr. A. H. Lee,and Mr. W. Long. The General Committee was re-elected, and the Executive Committee was appointed as follows :—Miss Appleton, Alderman Bennett, Miss Broadbent, Mr.J. D. Buckton, Mr. G. Dakin,Mr. Vere B. Davies, Mrs. James Fairclough, Mrs. Frost, Mr. and Mrs. Jackson Holmes, Mr. A: H. Lee, Mrs. Long, Mrs. McGregor, Miss Massy, Mrs.lfonro,Miss Morris,Mrs.H. Roberts,and Mr. Wilde.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
GRATIFYING FEATURES OF TUE WORK.

The CHAIRMAN alluded to the fact that in so many I eases warnings were given and;resulted in improvement. That was a very gratifying feature and showed the lines on which the committee and the Inspector had worked. Their object was to bring about moral improvement among the people, if possible without resorting to the law. They were also glad to notice that the sanitary authorities were much m orb active than they were before the institution of the society and that the ladyinspector was doing such a good work. After expressing pleasure at the increase in the sum to be handed over to the national institution, the chairman congratulated Miss Broadbent upon the result of her work in connection with the League of Pity, and also upon th fact that she had been able to take new and important public duties upon herself. They all heartily wished her success. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Long referred in felicitous terms to the services of Inspector Hackett, and said he did not think any association was blessed with a better man as inspector. Everybody seemed to be giving a helping hand to the society now, because as years went on they realized what a valuable work it was accomplishing. Mr. Long concluded by moving a resolution commending the society to the generous support of the public.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
ADDRESS:ON THE NATIONAL WORK.

MissIENGLAND then delivered an interesting address on the national work, and at the outset compli- mented the local branch upon the fact that in four years they had turned a deficit into a surplus. In emphasizing the great need for funds the pointed out that although the society had 1,000 branches, the whole of the country was not yet covered.ln some places the suffering of children went on unheeded because there was no branch' of the society. They wanted more funds from all the branches which were self-supporting, so as to enable them to plough up new ground. Having alluded to the varied character of the society's operations, Miss England said they had no desire to interfere between parent and child or with parental chastisement, but only to prevent wrong. She expressed regret at the break-down through overwork of the founder and director, the Rev. B. Waugh, and said she was sure they all hoped he would soon recover and be able to resume the work. If the public only knew the enormous amount of work that had to be done they would realize that the society was worthy of support. She hoped the Warrington branch would continue to prosper. They were doing splendidly and she would urge them to do their utmost in order that the finances might be in a still more satisfactory condition next year. (applause). The refiolution was carried. PROPOSED BAZAARS.

A IGtter was read from the Central Office stating that it was proposed to hold a series of bazaars with the object of raising ,E 50,000 to place the society in a sound financial position and make it possible to extend the organization over the whole of the population. The hope was expressed that the Warrington branch would take part in the movement, .and an appeal was made for practical sympathy to be extended at the earliest moment. A united effort of the whole of the existing organizations would, it was felt, ensure the success of the endeavour. The matter was referred to the Ladies 'Committee with Mrs. Monro as secretary. POOR CHILDREN'S SUMMER CAMP.

Opportunity was taken by Mr. A. H. LEE and Mr. W. H. J. JENKINS to call attention to the effort which is being made to again provide a summer camp for poor children. Mr. Jenkins said Lady McLaren had placed a, piece of land hear Prestatyn at the disposal of the corn- ittee, and it was in every way suitable for a camp. There was every facility for sea bathing and hundreds of acres of common land where the children might play to their hearts' content. It was hoped that the members of the society would support the movement. Votes of thanks were accorded to the Chairman, Miss England, and the Secretary.

A telegtam received in, New York from Manila says that several hundred red Moms ambushed Lieutenant Harker and 39 of the 17th Infantry on the Bth inst. at Simpatemi Minnanas. The Americans lost two officers and 15 men killed, and five men wounded. A woman giving evidence in a case at Wolverhampton on Wednesday said a policeman said to her mother, who was charged with being drunk, "If you want locking up my good woman, kindly go nearer the town, where there will be less trouble in taking you." A young baker's assistant named BransbY sent to prison for three months at Southwark on Wednesday for embezzling £9, was said to be anxious to get married and had taken the money in order to provide a home.

RE-OPENING OF CROFT CHURCH.

After undergoing extensive alterations and redecora- tion, Christ Church, Croft, was re-opened on Wednesday evening when a special service was held. Thero was a crowded congregation, the Lord Bishop of Live/pool being the preacher. The clergy in attendance were the Revs. Cann Penrhyn, Canon Smith,W. Pollock Hill,W. l3racecamp, S. W. W. Kaye, W. Mayers, and H.J. Wade- son (vim); The Bishop based a powerful sermon on the words While they beheld, He was taken up and a cloud received Him out of their sight." Special music was rendered by the choir, Mr. Spencer presiding at the organ. The appearance of the church has been greatly im- proved by the alterations. The scheme was an elaborate one, including the taking out of the whole body of the church, replacing the old flag floor with wood blocks, the entire reseating of the public portion, a new pulpit and the redecoration. The whole of the benches and pulpit are executed in beautifully figured oak, slightly polished in its natural colour which forms a charmin g contrast to the delicate shade of pale greyish blue of the walls. The style chosen was the perpendicular period of Gothic. The benches have handsomely carved ends of a quiet graceful online tif a tracery design. The choir stalls are designed in keeping only as befits their position and. purpo3e, being more elabor- ate in their outline. The fronts are panelled with tracery. The pulpit is octagonal in shape, richly panelled and moulded, and standing upon an octagonal pedestal of Caen stone. The principal side is decorated with two spirited panels based on a "vine" mofit. The walls have bee./ delicately coloured in a pale .greyish blue, their only relief being a deep frieze running round the i church. The ornamentation in this case s based on the vine. _ _ _ _ _

The whole of the work has been carried out by Messrs. J. M. Greenhalgh and Co. and reflects the greatest credit upon the firm,

Protection in Germany.—A Few Facts

for Workingmen.

Sir,—l have always enjoyed the joke of listening to 'people discussing things they really know very little about, being what one might term well read. That is they propound views of other people, but have not the faculties for grasping the significance of the same. Some of your correspondents although meaning well, do not open up any fresh attacks on the proposed pro- tection policy but simply treat with those that have been explained by men of superior calibre, education and experience. Most people in treating with this question at once fly to statistics, whether forming a case pro or contra to Protection. To my mind the simpliest way to judge is by comparing the homes of the workpeople of two or more nations where Protection is the fiscal policy at the same time making allowances for missed oppor- tunities. The country I will take is Germany, as this country is always being quoted as a successful child of Protection. I might state here,that I learnt these facts Tarn about to give, from actual contact with the German workpeople. The unskilled workman earns from 12s. to 14s. per week. the skilled man receiving from 18s. to 20s. on an average. He eats brown bread ; this is so because he cannot afford white, and not from choice. He eats very little meat, and a large percentage of the men eat dogand cat flesh. A large proportion-, I might say nearly all the workpeople, despise the present form of government and hate the forced service with the army. The country is inundated with spies, because the Kaiser is rather afraid of the ever growing socialistic views of his subjects. All the work- men are red hot socialists, but are frightened to speak out for fear of leze Majesty. If you have noticed the results of the recent Reichstag elections, you will find that these confirm my statement. The wives of • the workmen have to work in the fields and factories, to make both ends meet. Everybody is taxed, even if they be earning but £lO per annum. Is it not a fact that Germany subsidizes two of her most important indus- tries, namely her shipping and sugar growing? Is a country which has to tax its people, so as to subsidize and enable its commerce to live, in a really healthy condition? Is it to be wondered at that the German workmen looks with envious eyes upon English workpeople. Is it to be wondered at, • that -Germany supplies the largest number of emigrants? As to France and America, have thoy not been warned by their leading men, owing to the decline in their birth rate, that "If they are not very careful, they will starv9 thernselveslout ?" Is this caused by married people wishing to enjoy that luxury which Protection together with children cannot afford? I will finish by asking a conundrum: If commerce flourishes better under Protection than Free Tmde,how is it that there is so much foreign capital invested in England ?—Yours truly. EMO.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington's unbeaten record is maintained, the fourth match of the season resulting in a brilliant victory over Bootle. Present prospects are decidedly encouraging, and the eleven are to be congratulated upon their creditable achievements. Bootle had first turn with the willow, and all were dismissed for 102. Warrington responded in no half-hearted fashion, and put together 164 for the loss of only 9 wickets.

H. Williams topped the Bootle score list with a vigorously conapiled 33, and T. S. Johnson contributed a capital 21. T. Wright's performance was the feature of the Warrington innings. He knocked up 43 in faultless style and carried out his bat. On present form he •should prove a valuable acquisition to the eleven. His fine judgment in placing the ball was a praiseworthy feature.

Of the bowlers, W. H. Podmore came out with the best average, his 3 wickets costing a fraction over 4 runs each. E. A. Connor, the captain, secured 4 wickets f0r.36, and Lynes was responsible for 3 at a cost of 52.

Sankey Wire Mills opened the season with a visit to High Legh, the game ending in a draw greatly in their favour. The home eleven were all out for 115 and Sankey had scored 96 with only two wickets clown when play ceased. Evans and Hawthorn batted well for the Wire Mills, and knocked up 42 and 41 respec- tively. Gorton with 61 was the best man on the home side.

Congiatulations to the police! On Thursday they met Walker's Brewery team, and triumphed by a single run. P.C. Stewart was in great form with the bat, and knocked up 20 not out. P.C. Holland did fine work with the ball, capturing G wickets for 24 runs.

Latchford Baptiks had to bow the knee to Moore and Daresbury, the margin being 13. The Rev. F. Clifton Smith's 20 was the highest score of the afternoon, J. Hunt and J. Wirrell rendering best services for the Baptists with 12 and 11 respectively.

Penketh again had to admit defeat on Saturday. Walker's brewery eleven were the conquerors, and ran up 66 against the villagers' 22. J. Arber batted in excellent style for the brewery, and J. Milner bawled remarkably well, taking 7 wickets for 12 runs.

Cheshire Lines Engineers triumphed over St. Helens Primitives by a 29 majority. T. Smith (23) and J. Duckworth (21) were to the fore in the batting department.

Cricket enthusiasts wotild do well to possess them- selves of copies of Mr. Frank Sugg's pocket cricket annual which is full of the most interesting and valu- able infnrmation. In addition to the batting and bowling averages for the leading elevens and all the latest." records," the booklet contains excellent advice ,to young cricketers by Frank and Walter Sugg and facts that all cricketers should know. There are photos .of Warner's all England eleven and other first-class players. One penny is the modest price of the annual.

Cycling. The Warrington Club', run to the picturesque river side village of Hale is generally a very attrac- tive future, but owing to the dull, showery and cold weather only a dozen members made the journey on Saturday. After partaking of tea at the Childe of Hale" an adjournment was made to the bowling green and several good games were witnessed. After a stroll round the village the party turned their faces homewards, and Warrington was reached without mishap.

Tuesday erening's run to Budworth via Arley was also marred by dull weather, and barely a dozen of the braver spirits turned out. The countryside was looking at its best, the fruit trees being in full bloom. The elements were rather more favourable for the return, which was accomplished in good time.

To-day's run is to Delamere and on Tuesday the club go to Glvebzry. Lighting up time to-day, 0-1; Tuesday, 0-7.

Owing to pressureen space we were compelled to emit a report of the Gpening night run and smoking concert at Sankey Chapel House last week. The affair was highly successful and enjoyable, a first rate pro- gramme being submitted by well known local artistes.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

The works competition is ric—iw in full swing and the contests on the King and Queen green each Saturday attract large aesemblies. Two games were decided on Saturday, Houghton's wire works and Thewlis's file works teams being the victors. Houghton's administered a heavy defeat to the forge men, and ran up a majority of 72. There was a close contest between Whitecross netting department and Thewlis's the filesmiths claiming the victory by the narrow margin of 4. Some interesting matches are down for decision to-day. Vernon-street Tannery will meet Rylands's No. 1. London and North-Western Railway will try conclusions with Artingstalls, and Cheshire Lines are drawn against Firth Company. The London and North-West team are the cup-holders, and may be relied upon to do their utmost to retain possession of the trophy.

Saturday witnessed six contests under the auspices ef the Warrington District League. Brooklands met the Liberal Club and after a keen struggle gained the victory by six points. The Horse *and Jockey contin- gent beat Penketh by 83, and the Bull's Head team had the best of the encounter with the Chapel House by a 29 majority. Fearnhead had to admit defeat to the extent of 66 points at the hands of the Cemetery Eotel Club,
