THE CLAIMS OF THE SALVATION

ARMY.

Bazaar at the Co-operative .Hall.

The Salvation Army in Warrington is making a com- mendable effort to raise £2OO by means of a bazaar held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, with the object of providing new instruments for the band. The open- ing ceremony was performed on Thursday afternoon in the presence of a large company. The interior of the hall was a fairyland of delight, with prettily decorated stalls and bright coloured streamers stretching across the hall. A large number of plants had also been intro- duced and these heightened the effect. The work had been carried out by Adjutant Everness, assisted by a willing band of Salvation Army lasses and soldiers. Amongst those present at the opening ceremony were: Mr. and Mrs. Ziba Armitage, Rev. John Yonge and Mrs. Yonge, Mr. A. H. Crostleld, Mr. Alderman Smethurst, Mr. Levi Richardson, Major Garrie (Manchester), Adjutant Everness (Warrington), Mr. G. Dickenson. Prayer was offered by the Rev. John Yonge. In asking Mr. Arthur Crosfield to preside, Adjutant EVERNESS said that would be a high day in the history of the Salvation Army. The Army had laboured in War- rington for 21 years and they were celebrating their 21st anniversary in that happy manner. Their position was a very hopeful one, and much remained to be done.

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD said that anyone presiding over a gathering like that might sum up his duties inn way that had been defined by a certain humorist, who said that anyone who presided at a bazaar should speak honey, look bonny and spend money. (Laughter.) He was not sure whether he could live up to that definition if he contented himself with giving them such an excel- lent prescription. He could only speak to them as a man of the world—he was neither a saint nor a professed saint—and although he felt that it was almost presump- tuous for a man of the world to speak of the splendid work—he would say the grand work—of the Salvation Army, still perhaps they would allow him respectfully to offer a few observations upon that work. (Hear, hear.) They had already listened to a touching and beautiful prayer from Mr. Yonge. The Salvation Army needed the sympathy of all ranks and he was thankful to say all sects in the country. (Hear, hear.) He would like to give them a few facts about the army's operations, because they told a wonderful tale, and showed the marvellous work in the world which the Salvation Army was doing. The facts were : corps and outposts 7,505, officers and employees 15,324, local officers (unpaid 45,730, bandsmen (unpaid) 17,170, countries and colonies occupied 49, circulation of "War Cry" and other papers per issue 1,000,000, social insti- tutions for the poor 636, officers and employees in charge 1830, daily accommodation 34,500, fallen women passed through rescue homes in twelve months 6.196, criminals passed through Prison Gate homes in twelve months 1,956, slum posts 128, cheap beds supplied in 12 months 4,319,728, cheap meals supplied in 12 months 7,891,663, men supplied with work (temporary and per- manent) in 12 months 37,538. (Applause.) That morn- ing he received a letter from Mrs. Crosfield, who was very sorry she could not be present. She had sent him a letter from his eldest sister, Mrs. Ryan, in which she referred to the work of the Salvation Army. Mrs. Ryan wrote :—"I went to a drawing room meeting at Mrs. Hughes' in aid of the Hadleigh Labour Colony in Essex. Colonel Wright of the Salvation Army spoke for nearly an hour giving us facts which were appalling. It was most touching and impressive. He told us that each night, or morning rather at 2-0 a.m. the Salvation Army feeds—outside Drury-lane—a thousand starving men at its own ex- pense, and they do this at £5 a thousand. The meal consists of soup and chunks of bread. He gave a graphic ascription of watching them while all the rank and fashion drove away at 11.30., then all the lights went down, and at one a.m., first one man, then twos and threes came, abject, miserable and destitute. The police constable—quite accustomed to it—turned •up at 1.30., and said, "Now boys, fall in," and he said, "these shadows of men, without talking, without a murmur fall into regular lines three or four deep extending half a mile long." The police constable told him th it if the Salvation Army did not do this,the majority of course would starve, as it was the poor things' only meal in 24 hours. This goes on each night in London! Colonel Wright noticed a man standing amongst them wearing a shabby, torn frock coat and tall hat, destitute also. He drew him out and said, "You must have seen better days. How is this ?" The man said he had been a music hall singer for several years, his voice cracked, he fell ill and this was the result of six months illness—he could get nothing. Colonel Wright asked him, "Are you married ?" "Yes," but his wife and three children were in the Union, they could not tramp the streets like he did. Colonel Wright called one of his officers and said, "Is there nothing we can get for this man to do ?" They found there was a city firm wanting a man to write addresses on envelopes at a pittance a day. "Take this my man" be said," it will give you a foothold and you will be able to get more later and get your wife and children again." " I can'nt sir " was the answer. Colonel Wright said he felt inclined to be very angry, but on questioning him why this delay, the poor thing answered by showing him a blue piece of paper from the Workhouse notifying him of the death of his youngest child, with the words that he would be allowed to attend the funeral, which was next day. He waseso miserable that he only thought of the means of " getting a bit of black" to go to it. Colonel Wright said he was quite silenced by the misery of the whole thing. They got the man employment however, one is thankful to think. I cannot tell you how pathetic and intensely real it was as he told this story quite simply. He said London produces thousands and thousands of these cases. It is the terrible overcrowding which they are trying to fight by establishing these labour colonies in the country. It is not charity he said, they must work. Their maxim was "Nothing for nothing." Some years ago they started at Hadleigh with 3or 4 men only. Nov there are 450 with work- shops &c &c. For 4d. a day they give them breakfast, dinner, hot bath and a bed—but they must earn that 4d., I and so on to higher wages and better work. He said they had no police supervision at all. He was there with one or two other Salvation Officers over all these people—many of whom had been criminals, in gaol over and over again, and all from the submerged tenth, and he said they had never had a case of burglary &c. . . It certainly has impressed one with what" is being done." That extract, continued Mr. grosfield, was an illustration of the kind of cases with which the Salvation Army dealt. It was also an illustration of what followed from the fearfill over- crowding in some of our cities, which begot such terrible misery and squalor amongst those who were sunk in that terrible social quagmire at the bottom of civilisa- tion. He noticed that Mr. Charles Booth was speaking upon the housing question at a meeting in Manchester on the previous evening and moved the following reso- lution :—" This meeting is of opinion that it is neces- sary for the welfare of the community that for every town plans to regulate its extension should be prepared providing a well arranged system of streets of adequate width, the widest of which should be plantedwith trees and providing also parks, playgrounds, and sites for schools and other' public buildings; and, further, that every town council should adopt a comprehensive policy with regard to the inspection of dwellings, the erection of new buildings, the demolition of unwhole- some property, and the provision of facilities for cheap and rapid transit." Mr. Crosfield added that he was afraid no plan of that kind would really enable them to grapple with this hydra-headed monstre, which was the most formidable foe of modern civilisation, until they had gone to the , root of the evil and dealt thoroughly and justly with the reform of the land laws. (Hear, hear.) He would like to tell them a little anecdote bearing upon bazaars. It was said that a certain individual was returning from a gathering something like the present, when on cross- ing a common, he was accosted by highwaymen, who de- manded his money or his life. (Laughter.) The un- lucky man pleaded for mercy and while doing so happened to say that he was returning from a bazaar. "Let him alone," was the command of the leader, "he's been plundered enough." (Loud lauahter.) It now. devolved upon all present to choose wisely, buy freely and pay generously. (Hear,hear.) Mrs. ARMITAGE, in declaring the bazaar open, said they were present to show their great sympathy with that wonderful organization—the Salvation Army, and to rejoice in the great good that it had been able to do, not only in Warrington,but all over England and in'all the world. There was work for all Christians to do. God had given everyone of them some work to do, and those who worked in other parts of His vineyard, could rejoice at the great success that had attended the efforts of those who worked in the Salvation Army. (Hear, hear.) That afternoon they had to help the local officers to get sufficient money to buy the instruments, so that the local corps might complete one part of their organisation. (Hear, hear.) She hoped all the money required would be raised by Saturday evening. (Hear, bear.) The services of Mrs. Armitage, Mr. Crosfield, and Mr.l Levi Richardson (who sang a solo "Mary of Argyll") were acknowledged on the pronosition of Alderman I SMETHURST seconded by Major GARRIE. Mr. CROSFIELD acknowledged the compliment, and the sale of goods then commenced. During the afternoon, Master Tom Barton's boys' orchestra played selections of music. YESTERDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

The Bazaar was again opened yesterday. Alderman William Pierpoint presided and he was accompanied on the platform by Mrs. Pierpoint, Mrs. Garnett, Mr. Councillor Horrocks, Adjutant Everness, Adjutant Samms (Manchester). The CHAIRMAN eulogised the excellent work per- formed by the Salvation Army. He pointed out that when the organization first started 21 years ago it met with approbrium and ridicule but now its objects and work were appreciated in every part of the globe. Mrs. GARNETT,in declaring the bazaar open, expressed the opinion that the social work of the Army and the efforts put forth to lift up the nbmerged tenth, did not meet with that general approval which they deserved. (Hear, hear.) It was necessary that the local corps should have an efficient band. An army without a band would be lacking one of its chief features. She hoped the efforts put forth would prove successful. Solos were efficiently sung by Master Richardson and Sister Everness.

Thursday's proceeds amounted to £lOO, and yester- day's takings increased the total sum raised to £l5O.

WESLEYAN SYNOD AT WARRINGTON.

INTERESTING GATHERINGS.

During the past week, Warrington has been visited by an unusually large number of Wesleyan ministers and laymen, and the reason for this visitation is that the animal synod meetings of the Liverpool Wesleyan district are being held in Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel. It is nine years since the synod was last held in this town. The Liverpool district is about the largest and most important district in Methodism, extending as it does from Morecambe in the North to Aberystwith on the Welsh Coast.

The meetings extended over three days, commenc- ing on Tuesday morning and closing on Thursday evening. Tuesday and Thursday were exclusively ministerial days, when about 150 ministers were pre- sent, but on Wednesday, the ministers were joined by about 200 laymen. Rarely have the meetings of the synod been batter attended, and perhaps never before have so many Wesleyan ministers and laymen been seen in the town.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FIRST DAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

The Synod commenced on Tuesday at the Bold- street Wesleyan Church, under the presidency of the chairman of the district, the Rev. T. Gallandflartley. After the devotional exercises, in which the Rev. A. H. Walker, 8.A., and E. Theodore Carrier took part, the former was elected secretary by a unanimous vote. In returning thanks, he asked that the Rev. H. Harri- son G-ower might act as his assistant. The Rev. G. H. CARNSON', superintendent of the Warrington Circuit, then heartily welcomed the synod to Warrington, and announced the various arrange- ments which had been made for the comfort and con- venience of the visitors. The roll-call revealed the presence of almost every minister in the district. The most notable absentee from the synod was the Rev. Thomas McCullagh who entered the ministry in 1845, and now resides as a supernumerary in Liverpool. Mr. McCullagh was President of the Conference in 1883, an for several years was chairman of the Liver- pool District. The only minister in full work absent was the Rev. F. R. Smith (" John Ackworth," author of the " Clog Shop Chronicles " &c.) of the St. Helens Circuit. He was absent on- account of illness.

The annual schedule of returns presented by the secretary showed that the reported increase of 669 adult. church:members indicated fairly general advance in the district. The number of members was returned as 23,138, with also 2,064 members on trial. During the year 2,434 new members had been fully received 1,116 had removed to other circuits, and 544 had been received from other circuits. There had been 41 em- igrations, 235 deaths, and 923 ceased to be members. There were nowlol ministers in active work,and nearly I,sooclass readers.A resolution was adopted expressing thankfulness for the large increase of adult members, but regretting the great decrease in junior members. There are 28 adult Bible classes in 01;3 district with a membership of 4,2B7.There are 82 Wesley Guilds with a total membership of 5,489. The report of the pro- bationers' examination was presented by the Rev. H. H. rOullagh. The Rev. H. P., Harris secured second class honours and the Revs. F. Stanley Atkinson and R. Bewick third class honours. The Rev. A. T, Kinn- ings reported to the synod the death of the Rev. Henry Smallwood, of Southport, and testimony was borne to his character and faithful service.

In the afternoon a conversation took place on the state of the work of God throughout the district, in which the Rev. J. Whitehead Clegg, of the Severn Valley Mission, gave an interesting account of a successful work which has taken place at Oswestry and in that neighbourhood. The Revs. R. Rossall, of Ornaskirk ; J. Crompton Sowerbutts of] Southport; John Martin, of Lancaster; W. Backhouse M.A., of Great Homer-street, Liverpool; C. B. M'Cullagh, 8.A., of Wesley Circuit, Liverpool; A. H. Walker, 8.A., of Fairfield; and the chairman also took part in a most encouraging and practical dis- cussion.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDNESDAY'S PRNEEDINGS.

The Synod resumed its session on Wednesday under the presidency of the Rev. T. Galland Hartley. The devotional exercises were conducted by the secretary, the Rev. A. H. Walker, 8.A., and Mr. Henry Ball, of Southport. Apologies for absence were received from many gentlemen, including Mr. N. W. Holme, M.P. Letters of sympathy and condolence were directed to be sent to many.

The Synod proceeded to the consideration of home missionary affairs, the total amount raised for this fund in the district being £2,191. The amount obtained in the district during the year was £2,191, a decrease of £24 6s. 11d. Grants were made as follows:—Mold, I £2O; Carnarvon and Bangor, £6O; Garstang, £6O; and Aberystwyth, £3O; Liverpool Mission, £100; Hoylake and West Kirby, £6O; and Severn Valley Mission £l2O. The Rev. JOSEPH JACKSON, of the Liverpool Mission, gave a most interesting statement and effective appeal in connection with the new Central Hall to be erected in Renshaw-street. He stated that the contract had been signed and the foundation stones would be laid in October.-IVIr. S. M. Hutchinson, CC. and Mr. E. Cannington, J.P., supported the appeal. The membership of the several halls at present exceeds a thonsand, with 132 on trial. Three thousand Sunday school scholars are enrolled, and guilds and re-reative I classes are held for their benefit. Regarding the Charles Garret Memorial, it is estimated that a sum of £45,000 will be scent. The large hall is to seat 2,500 and the small hall 750. Religions, social, and philanthropic work will be carried on. At present £13,500 has been received in promises, a grant of £7,500 has been obtain- ed from the Twentieth Century Fund, and by a bazaar next spring it is hoped to raise at least £s,ooo.—Various reports of the Liverpool, West Kirby, and Severn Valley Missions were adopted, showing the striking progress made during the year.—Additional ministers were asked for by the St. Helens,Morecambe,Widnes, and Warring- ton Circuits.—The morning session closed with an inspiring conversation on the spiritual work of the dis- trict, in which Messrs. J. Holden (Southport), H. B. Williams (Birkenhead), A. J. Jones (Bootle), and others took part. An important committee was appointed to confer with the Mold Circuit with regard to the work of the Dee- side Mission at Queen's Ferry. New churches were reported to be in course of erection at Marsh lane, Bootle; Elm Hall Drive, Liverpool ; Seacombe, West Kirby, Chester, Montgomery, Widnes, St. Helens, Prescot, and outhport. The number of building schemes now sanctioned is fifteen, with an estimated outlay of £18,830. Ten new churches are in course of erection at a cost of £36,000, while fourteen others are sanctioned at an outlay of £102,000. The total value of the church property in the district is estimated at £990,130 an increase on the year of £40,000. The foreign missionary income for the past year was reported as £11,277, being an increase of £4OO on the previous year. The simultaneous canvass is being prosecuted in the circuits with vigour, and the results already to hand show a prospective increase of £4OO. The folloWing gentlemen were elected as lay representa- tives to the forthcoming conference at Sheffield:— Messrs. G. Weaver (Warrington), N. W. Holme, M.P. (Lancaster), E. L. Lloyd, C.C. (Liverpool), S. M. Hutchinson, C.C. (Liverpool), J. Griffiths (Chester), John Allen (Birkenhead), J. R. Batty (Southport), W. H. Smith (Whitchurch), A. J. Jones (Bootle),B. Browne, J.P.-(Widnes), and Craine (Liverpool). THE CLOSING MEETING.

The synod reassembled on Thursday morning,the Rev- T. Galland Hartley again presiding. The following ministers were selected to attend the forthcoming con- ference at Sheffield.—Representative Session: The Revs. A. H. Walker 8.A., G. H. Carason, A. T. Kinn- ings, E. Blackall,T. A. Turnoy, F. Haines, G. M. Cobban J. Jackson, J. Whitehead, and H. H. M'Cullagh, B.A. Pastoral Session: The Revs. NV. Backhoase, M.A., T. Austin, G. T. Newton, W. L. Tasker, and A. Rudman. Handley, and H. Whita- more, Chester, R. NV. Charlesworth, Tarporley ; A. F. Olsen, Mold: W. Jones. B.Sc., Runcorn; P. Webster, Widnes; F. P. Gent and NV. N. Robinson. St. Helens; J. C. Martin, Preston ; and W. S. Cooper, Lytham. The synod was concluded with the usual votes of thanks.

PUBLIC TEMPERANCE MEETING

Condemnation of the Licensing Bill.

AN APPEAL FOR ENGLAND'S CHILDHOOD.

The inaugural public meeting held in Bewsey-road Church on Monday evening was in support of the tern- prance cause. There was a fair attendance, presided over by Mr. Frank Harris. The speakers were the Revs. A. T. Kinnings (District Temperance Secretary) and T. H. Wainman (Acting Chaplain to Wesleyan troops in the Boer war) and Mr. Isaac Holden, of Liverpool. The CHAIRMAN said the meeting was one of very considerable importance. He trusted that by a united and enthusiastic spirit they would make the gathering a success, and that its reflection would be cast upon the other meetings to be held during the week. He hoped the visit of the synod would be an impetus for

good in their midst, that their visitors would thoroughly enjoy themselves in the "good old town" and that they in Warrington would profit by the holding of the meetings. They made no apology for meeting together under the temperance banner. That day had gone by. (Hear, hear.) The subject of temperance reform had filled all men's minds with thoughtful concern. All ranks and classes of men, irrespective of any distinction as to their call- ing, whether they be statesmen, ministerial brethren, or commercial men, united in the opinion that the temper- ance movement was an absolute necessity in some form or other. The question had repeatedly been before the legislature with very varied results—(hear, hear)—and he thought it was somewhat timely that they should hold that meeting when the present Licensing Bill was before Parliaihent. He had no inten- tion of enlarging upon the merits or otherwise of that Bill. One 'thing he would say—that he thought it was the duty of every right-minded citizen to at once, with a firm, strong and thoughtful mind, I protest against the suggested interference with magisterial discretion. (Applause'. Mr. Harris pro- ceeded to emphasise the importance of work among the children. Probably the greatest results would be achieved by a wise and proper education of the young on the question. It was due] to those who spent their time in teaching the children that they should receive hearty encouragement and unstinted praise. (Applause.) Mr. Isa.ac Hot.nEN delivered an able address on the Licensing Bill. The question, he said, was not one of politics as fares he was concerned. He had been regrett- ing bitterly during the past month that he had not always supported a certain section of the House of Commons, because it would have been such an immense satisfaction to have changed sides on that subject to show that it was something more important than politics. They had to consider what was best for the country, and take their stand as Christian men and people who had the welfare of the country at heart. They would have to consider the question apart altogether from party politics on the matter of whose pocket it suited. (Hear, hear,) During the past year or two the magistrates had awakened and had been doing their duty. The work had been done solely in the interests of the public by disinterested men who had no object of their own to serve,bot did it simply because it was their duty. If his hearers had read the reports of the last Licensing Sessions, they must have seen from the evi- dence what an enormous amount of good had been done by the magistrates by their action during the last five years. In Liverpool they had reduced drunkenness and indictable offences by one third in ten years. The speaker quoted from "judicial statistics " (issued from the Home Office) a testimony to the beneficial results of the Liverpool magistrates' action. What they had to consider, he continued, was whether the Licensing Bill was going to improve that work and increase it or whether it was going to block it. nay had been fight- ing for some years to put themselves in a position to control the trade. They did not want to rob the trade of anything they had a right to—far from it. They only wanted to make sare that the trade did not rob them of something they had a right to. (Hear, hear.) After fighting for years and spending an enormous a-leant of money, and by dint of hard work on the part of patriotic men who had no interest of their own to serve, they had established four facts upon which the strength of their position depended. The first was that the magistrates acted in an administrative capacity and not as a Court. That had been established in one or two well known cases. It meant that the magistrates had a riet to go round a town to inquire how public houses were conducted and whether they were necessary or not and they could then go to the Court, raise their own objections, and suppress as many licences as they liked. If they were a court, they must only act on the evidence brought to them, aud they had no initiative power of their own. By the Licensing Bill the whole thing was changed. The magistrates ceased to be administrative and were purely a Court of Justice. "That," said Mr. Holden, "Knocks out one of our most valuable weapons." The second fact they had established, continued the speaker, was that licences were for one year and no longer. That had been the case for over four centuries.and it had undoubtedly been established by every Court of Law in the land up to the House of Lords. By the new Bill a licence was made perpetual and could not be taken away simply because it was not wanted. The third fact was that licence holders had no right to compensation either in equity or in law. They found in the Bill,how- ever, that they were to receive compensation and com- pensation with no measured terrns,—full compensation, the whole market value. Fourthly,the magistrates could at present object to licences and then inquire into them. Under the Bill,they would be absolutely unable to do that. There were innumerable other objections V) the Bill. Local justices lost half their duties—and the half which required the most local knowledge. Another serious defect was that the public had no right to be heard in a Licensing Court in future. Moreover it was very doubtful whether even the magistrates who iecommnded the Quarter Sessions to reduce licences had any right of appearing in favour of their own report. The reduction of licences would depend not upon the public necessity as it had done for centpries, but purely upon the state of a particular fund which would be established. The Bill said the value of a licence for compensation was to be reckoned as the difference between the value of a licensed house now (as if the Bill had not been passed) and of the house without a licence. That clause was full of mean- ing. It showed that the promoters of the Bill knew perfectly well that they were going to double the value of all the licences in the country. In order to be plausible they introduced the clause that compensation which was to be given up to the full market value.must be reckoned on the value of a house as if the Act had not been passed. That, as Sir Robert Reid had said, was wholly inoperative and utterly meaningless, There was no machinery set up anywhere for recording the value of licences. Nobody knew what it was. The owners were not, under the Bill, forced to declare the value of houses beforehand. The reason was that if they did so they would not get so much when they came to be compensated, and until the houses were closed they would have to pay rates on the full value—which they , did not pay at present. The compensation fund which was very strictly limited and throughout the country would only amount to £1,206,000 was utterly inadequate to produce anything like a marked effect on licenses. In Liverpool 721 licenses were dropped last year—some because they did not pay. The Quarter Sessions compensation fund for the whole area would produce only £36,500. It would therefore be utterly impossible to reduce licences at the same rate as hitherto. In Liverpool, out of 391 licences refused in ten years, 152 were simply dropped because they did not pay. Under the new Bill enor- mous figures would have to be paid for licences dropped under those circumstances. They heard a great deal about the liberty of the subject, but had not temperance reformers also a right which might be infringed b y the liquor traffic? Must they suffer in silence while their eyes and ears were offended at every corner by the traffic and its results? Surely if there was any conflict in the matter of rights in regard to personal liberty, the liberty of the most law-abiding section of the community ought, at any rate, to be con- sidered. Generally speaking it had, however, been otherwise. They must make their voice heard at once, very generally and with no uncertain sound, or else they were going to be handed over bound hand and foot to the liquor traffic. Mr. Holden alluded to the ante-1869 beerheuses which, he said, were in every large town the plague spots and pests of civilization. It was practically , impossible to refuseithe licence of an ante-1869 house. The Bill proposed to put every public house in the country on much the same footing as the ante 1869 houses. There were, however, only three grounds instead of four hitherto on which they could refuse Ilicences. The Bill was introduced in the interests of a very few of the big monopolist brewers and, incident- ally, he believed, of the whole body of practising barristers and lawyers. (Laughter). It would I be many years before they fully understood the worst in the Bill, and by that time many a poor, struggling barrister would be a rich man (Laughter.) "You and I" concluded Mr. Holden "will have to stand the racket and pay the price. I call upon you all to look at it in an energetic,unbiassed and Chris- tian way. Let us take up the course which we believe to be right, and may God defend the right." (Applause.) The Rev. A. T. KINNINGS, of Southport observed that, whatever a March quarterly meeting or a mixed con- gregation of Radicals, Tories, flscalitis people and all the various political thoughts of to-day might have to say, no one would ever stand between his honest con- viction as to the opposition which was necessary to a Bill that safeguarded the liquor traffic but removed the safe-guards of temperance legislation, and the sobriety of the nation. (Applause). He wanted to turn away from the various aspects of the ques- tion which were often argued and have the honour of holding a brief for England's* childhood. Whilst they had strenuous efforts for the uplifting of humanity which had fallen through excess of alcoholic liquors. whilst they sought among the adult population of the country the great reformation of life which must begin in a regeneration of spirit, and whilst they sought thus for a sober country, a land of purity and righteousness he was bound to say that he pinned his faith to Eng- land's childhood. If a great card inal could say that if he could have under his control the childhood of Eng- land for a few years he would soon make this country Roman Catholic, he (the speaker) did not see why a Methodist preacher should not have his faith that if they could have under their control the childhood of this country, trained in the principles of sobriety and , living a life of abstinence,to-morrow they would give to I this great empire a nation of sobriety, purity, and pros- perity. (Applause.) He always held that every child born into this world had three birth-rights,and the first was a 'sound constitution. Those words of Kingsley, tragic though they were, were very true—that "many children are damned from their very birth." It was no mere platitude of a temperance utterance, it was no mere statement of a total abstinence platform—for that was what they were that night,' and he liked the phrase better than temperance—when he said that physical deformity and weakness, mental failure, and depraved tastes were in thousands of cases inherited. They needed sound education on the great physical aspect of the liquor question. Every child had also. the right to national safeguards. The nation ought to make it,for the youth especially, difficult to do wrong and easy to do right. What were the surroundings of the great masses of children to-thy? Were they sur- rounded by those influences which meant ennoblement of character, dignity of life, and uplifting of thought?

No. In this Christian country the churches taught the children on the Sabbath Day to "remem- ber the Sabbath Day to keep it holy" and sent them out of the churches to find the public- houses and gin palaces open on every hand—a disgrace to a Christian country. Their children were taught to pray" Lead us not into temptation' and the legislation of a Christian country placed temptation on every hand. He said e trnestly and emphatically that thousands upon thousands of children in this land had not a fair chance from a national standpoint. He con- tended that the working classes should have a right to say whether they would have public-houses in their midst or not. If the children were to have national safeguards, they would not only have to talk on temperance platforms and pray about it, but they would have to remember at the next general election the kind of men they sent to Parliament. (Hear, hear.) The Licensing Bill was another attempt at preventing true safeguards for the children of England. In conclusion the speaker emphasised the importance of a sound home influence. He urged his hearers to use every effort to free the country from the curse of drink, because of England's childhood. (Applause.) The Rev. T. H. WAINMAN then delivered an interest- ing address on his experiences with the troops in South Africa. He said that when he returned to this country after having been in South Africa for twenty years he felt ashamed that so little progress had been made on the vital question of temperance reform. In South Africa they could go to Durban and towns of a similar character and they would see nothing like they saw in the towns of this country. He could not remember ever having seen a drunken woman during the twenty years he had been at the Cape. It would be difficult for him to talk about temperance work among the soldiers because the soldiers were not allowed to do as they liked whilst serving in South Africa. He had been chaplain to the troops on two occasions. In 1884 he accompanied Sir CharleE• Warren's force which chased the Boers out of Bechuanaland. Alluding to the Trans- ' vaal campaign the speaker said it was absolutely untrue that Johannesburg was to blame for it. It was true that in a certain street they would find the stock exchange, tariff club, mining houses and so on, and that party might have had something to do with the political agitation of the place at the time. He himself was sneered at as a supporter of the Boers because he would not go to political agitation meetings. He claimed that as a Wesleyan minister and one of their missionaries,he was a man of peace, and he told those people he would have nothing to do with the bringing on of the war. Having described some of the scenes in Johannesburg on the outbreak of the war, the speaker contended that if we had been half as kind to our own kith and kin who were practically kicked out of Johannesburg (although they had done no more in volitical agitation than the people in English towns) as we were to the Boer women and children in the refuge camps, the condition of our own people would have been princely in comparison with what it was. Although he was a friend of the Boers in • one way, he considered that the Boers had absolutely nothing to complain of in regard to their treatment in the refuge camps. Everything that could be done under the circumstances was done for them. The rev. gentleman briefly but graphically described some of the famous engagements in the war. He joined General Buller's forces just after the Colenso disaster and was with them at Spion Kop and the other battles which preceded the relief of Ladysmith. He would ever think of Tommy Atkins with the greatest possible respect and he claimed that the men who fought so gallantly were • worth every effort that could be put. forth for their , salvation and uplifting. (Applause.) : The Chairman and speakers were thanked on, the ' proposition of the Rev. R. WELKIN REES, seconded by , the Rev. G. H. CARNSON.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
OFFICIAL SERMON BY THE REV. DR. BURTON, M.A. In Praise of the Bible.

. The Rev. Henry Burton, M.A., D.D., of Lytham, preached the official sermon in Bold-street church on Tuesday evening. There was a large congregation. The preacher was accompanied on the rostrum by the Rev. G. H. Carnson (supetinteadent minister) and tne Rev. T. Galland Hartley (chairman of the synod). Mr. Frank Hickman presided at the organ. Dr. Burton based his discourse on the text "The seed is the Word of God " (Luke 8 11v.). In these seven monosyllabic words, the preacher said, Jesus gave us the key to the parable of the sower. Was it conceivable that God who endowed His minor creatures with unerr- ing instincts, should leave man who was made in His own image and endowed with the highest faculties and with a soul that was immortal, without any clear direction in revelation of His will or any reference to his destiny? Any revelation that God made to man must be under human conditions, and must be subject to human capacities and reason. When Heaven spoke to man it must speak in an earthly tongue. Therefore the revelation of God must come first to man and then through man to his fellows. The human instrument was indispensable. This introduction of the human element into the Word of God brought into it an element of imperfection. Whatever views of inspiration they might hold, they could not and did not hold that all the compilers of the books in the Bible had been strictly kept from fault and error, specially when it was remem- bered that the holy scriptures were written without any full points whatever or any punctuation marks One mark of the divineness of the word of God was its remarkable unity. The very name—Bible was a tacit recognition of its unity; it was a collection of books, a library. It was a transcendent work not only in its surpassing interest,but in its remarkable unity. Between the first chapters and the last, there were fifteen long centuries. The old testament was written in Hebrew and the new testament in Greek. Many of the writers of the sacred word were unknown; they would never be identified now, and those they did recognise were taken from different walks of life, the learned and the unlearned; each working by himself without collabora- tion, following, as he thought, his own impulses. Not one of the writers knew or even thought he was writing i Scripture. Running through t all they could detect was the inovement of one theme—the theme of redemption. The whole Book was one history of the fall and then the great redemption, one enlarged Messiah and that i Messiah ended in its keynote n the paradise of God, closing where it began. And so throughout all the different parts of the sacred word formed one unbroken whole. The whole temple was built on the pattern of the cross, and enter it where they would, if they were not perversely .blind, they would see before them, standing radiant in its glory,the altar of a supreme sacrifice, the altar of a great atonement. But there was another evidence of the divinity of the word—the ex- cellence of its doctrine. What a code of morals was here! What a high standard of ethics! How straight it ruled the lines of duty, duty to self, duty to family, to neighbour, to country and to God. How strongly it enforced mercy, justice, purity and truth ! Not only did the Scriptures teach the moralities, hut enjoined personal holiness and put before them glorious possibilities. Another mark of the divine- ness of the word was its marvellous influence; restrain- ing, refining and elevating. Let it touch any life, the individual or the nation, and it gave to that life new aspects, new powers, new possibilities, new elevations of thought and purpose never experienced before. We owed our literature, our laws,our discoverips of science , and the amelioration of suffering to this inspired word of God, and so our late beloved Queen was perfectly right when she pointed to the dusky missionary and said" This is the Word, this is the foundation of Eng- land's greatness,England's Bible." The President of the United States said that the atmosphere of this word of God was cleansing and inspiring; while Ruskin acknow- ledged that to his early knowledge of the Bible be owed the best part of his taste for literature and the most essential part of his education. Glad- stone loved to call it the "impregnable rock of Scripture." It was his constant duty, and he wrote in his diary that on nearly all occasions of sharp pressure and trial some word of the Bible had been borne to his heart as if on angels' wings, and there were thousands in the world to-day who paid honiage and reverence to it. No matter what hostile attacks were made on it they only strengthened and widened the sphere of its influence and demonstrated the divinity that was within it, What made it immortal, what gave it an undying and ever growing influence was because it responded to the deepest needs and the loftiest aspira- tions of the human soul, supplying all its wants and needs. They could not hear the message unless they brought themselves into sympathetic touch with it. The service was followed by the sacrament of the Lord's Supper.

===== 0002601_19040514_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC MEETING AT BEWSEY ROAD. The Work and Needs of Methodism.

On Wednesday evening a public meeting was held in Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, Mr. R. H. Posnett, of Runcorn, presiding over a fai r attendance. The speakers were the Revs. James Dunk, of Liverpool, G. Tatalun Newton, if Rhyl, and W. Russell Maltby, of Seaconibe. The CHAIRMAN congratulated the meeting upon the fact that the synod had by a very large vote decided to send their senior circuit steward, Mr. George Weaver to the conference to represent Warrington.He proceeded, to dwell upon the life and work of the late Peter Mackenzie, alluding to his warmth of fellowship and his desire to serve his brethren. Continuing he said that during the synod he had been ery much struck with the brotherly spirit, warmth, and kindliness which had pervaded the meetings. He believed Methodism was doing a great work in this country by its warmth and brotherliness. Let them keep that in and keep the frost out, and in all their work let them retain that spirit which made their Methodism so attractive to the workingman in the street. (Applause.) The Rev. JAMES DUNK delivered an eloquent address in which he dwelt upon the work which lay before the church. They recognized, he said, that the fruit of the Spirit wns in all goodness, righteousness and truth and that there were many beautiful things to be found out- side the church. If they wanted to know where typical human nature was to be discovered they had to look inside the Church of the Lord Jesus. How wonderful d varied were the gradings of the Christian character!

The church had to find out some power to put before the people, some persuasion that would unify the marvellously diversified mass of human character. They should turn their eyes and endeavour to read a little more closely and accurately the heart of the age in which they lived. One of the first things they would discover was that there was a tremendous increase of intensity on all sides. The worldly man was getting more worldly, but he believed it was true to say that the Christian man was getting more Christian. The speaker referred to what were known as " the days of monsters "in past ages. They were, he continued, living in an age of monsters to-day. There was not the slightest doubt that there had been and there was going on a cultivation of the lower sides of life and, if they could only outline it and get the details rigid and clear before them and brood upon that one thing, their hearts would be broken in twain for very sorrow and compassion that such things could be allowed in Eng- land to-day. He need say nothing about the slums of our great cities nor about some of those isolated villages which were worse than the slums. Not only would they find the monsters at that end of life, but they would find them at the other end. If he were to give an illustration of where monsters were to be found he would go to the House of Commons and he would see there a number of men who were not ashamed to do their very best to fasten upon the back, and heart, and vitals of England the damnable drink trade. (Applause.) Yes, this was an age of monsters, and the man who wanted to know where he should be and what he should do must look the monsters in the face. They must remember that there were people on the under-side of the monsters, who were suffering from their greed, from woe and from terror. In illustra- tion of his point the speaker alluded to the battle of Borodino where in the evening it was said, the moaning of the wounded was heard like the noise of the sea. If, he said, they could get every voice to be quiet and listen to the cry of human life now, the wail of the wounded at Borodino would hens sweet to them in comparison as the prattling of a child. Let commerce stay her million wheels, let the pleasure seeker stay his dancing and his song,let beauti- ful ecclesia' the church herself, stay her ecclesiastical doings and duties, and industries, and let her live in absolute silence and listen. Let her think of the hearts of the children that were suffering in wicked homes that night, of the young men and women whose lives were being blighted, of the old men who might have been honoured and their white hairs covered with a crown of righteousness, but who were moaning and sighing out their days, waiting for the Redeemer to bring them into the light and gladness which had been denied them on earth. If they could gather all this together and it could come home to the heart, they would think of the Borodino wounded as of an hour of gladness in comparison with the misery that would force itself upon their hearts in the moment of hush. The speaker urged his hearers to stand up in the name of God and to do what they could for the help of their brethren. They wanted to get back to simple obedience. They wanted more military drill in the Church. Then hey would be able to use their forces better.(Applause). The Rev. G. TALALUN Newton said he was proud to belong to the Methodist Church because of its zontri- bution during the past century to the development and elevation of the human race. They were gathered to- gether in order to stimulate and inspire one waother to a more earnest effort, walking in the footstep of their fathers to improve the state of the nation and to ex- tend the empire of Jesus Christ. He had no doubt whatever that the origin of Methodism was divine, that John Wesley was called into the services which he ren- dered by God Himself. The Methodist Church was a spiritual institutkon and the work which was entrusted to them was the regeneration of society through the con- version of the individual. The bringing of the individual into close fellowship with the living Christ was the great purpose of their church. The great awakening of the past century they longed again to see and to realize in the twentieth .century. He did not stand before them as a pessimist. He knew that there were some men who had but little hope of the progress of humanity. Relying upon the promise of God, revealed through Jesus Christ, they were not pessimists. The spiritual triumphs of the past were so many that they were able to rejoice because of the hopefulness within them. They looked forward to greater liberty, to greater success, and to mightier triumphs than they had experienced in the past. They sometimes got depressed at the difficulties sur- rounding them, but he was absolutely certain that the human race stood upon a higher altitude to-day than it had ever done before. Methodism had always been and must be a spiritual church seeking for the spiritual progress and advancement of the race. Removed from sa.cerdotalism and latitudinarianism it emphasised con- version, fellowship and sanctification. Ho asked his hearers not to be disappointed by ecclesiastical pre- judice and pointed out that to-day 30 millions called themselves Methodists. He was thankful that from this town men had gone forth to preach the Gospel. (Applause.) The REV. W. RUSSELL MALTBY devoted part of his address to the temperance question. Mr. Newton, he said, would no doubt call him a pessimist, but, if our nation had got so far from all reality that it thought the important thing to do to-day was to see that brewers lost no money, what hope had they got? It seemed to him that there was a great and splendid piece of work waiting for Methodism and that there was an army of people who in manifold ways, seemed to have been pre- pared by God to do a mighty work. The pity of it was that only in the smallest degree had they awakened to the fact that they were made for the work. The rev, gentleman urged the necessity of getting hold of the multitudes who were scattered abroad as sheep without a :shepherd, and bringing them to the Shepherd. Sometimes he wished the Almighty would be pleased to burn down some chapels in order that they might look at things afresh. I have said more than I meant to say" he added the next moment. What he felt was that he wanted to know in his heart the message that would reach any man; that he could be able to preach Christ to any man by the wayside, church •or no church. They wanted to address themselves, con- cluded the speaker, direct to the souls of men, caring nothing of what happened to Methodism so long as the Kingdom of Christ was established, and men and women were saved. (Applause). A vote of thanks to the chairman and speaker con- cluded the meeting.

MEETING AT PENKETH. The Aims of Methodism. Mr. C. E. Parker presided at a Synod public meeting held at Penketh on Wednesday evening. In the course of a few observations on the subject of "What is a man worth?" Mr. Parker said it was customary to measure a man's worth by the amount of money he had. This was a very poor measure of a man's worth. A man's worth was a man's character, and the Methodist church must ever be loyal to this great fact. The men whose lives had been of the most worth, and who had done most for this world, were not the men who had amassed money but the men who had been strong in character and loyal to their God. In the eighteenth century there was one man, not rich in this world's. goods,the son of a poor clergyman, to whom divine inspiration, and divine afflatus and strength were given. This man, made of God, aroused England—aroused the moral sense of England, and brought, in a great measure, England back to God. That man died and if they were to ask what that man was worth in the common acceptation of the term, they would find he was worth a few silver spoons and two or three tea-pots, but if they were to measure him by his moral worth—if they measured his life from a spiritual standpoint—they would find that that man had done a marvellous and grand work, and left behind him the church of which they were the representatives, he meant the Wesleyan Church. (Hear, hear). The Rev. F. PLATT, M.A. 8.D., of Colwyn Bay de- livered an address and said if they could realise a little more than they did the peculiar significance that was associated in the religious life of their country with the Methodist Church and its organization, they might be more fit to carry out the purposes which they felt God had appointed a peculiar place in the history of the evangelisation of this country and the world at large. Methodism had still a peculiar province of her own amongst the churches of this land. Methodism had been described as the religion of emotion, but that was not a fair description. He thought the calling and mission of the Methodist people was compressed in the single line " 0 let me commend my Saviour to you." That was their distinctive calling—to go to the people with the message that there was salvation. Methodism was the religion of experience,and to be true, Methodism must be essentially experimental. The Rev. S. T. HousE, of Blackpool said he had at- tended almost 40 synods, and he rejoiced in the fine spiritual tone of the synod meetings at Warrington. One of the things Methodism must do, was th maintainl its spiritual and religious teaching. Alluding to the increase in the membership in the Liverpool district of 669 during the past year, the speaker said he believed it to be the largest increase in any of the Wesleyan districts, but he thought that with all the workers and teachers in the Liverpool dis- trict, the increase ought to be larger. It took the Wesleyan Church 50 years to double its numbers. It was not God's will that the salvation of men should go forward at this slow rate, and he believed that if the Methodist church was true to its mission oven greater results would be achieved. Mr. Isaac Holden said it wris necessary to take our Christianity into our politics. The duty of Christian people was so to live and act among their fellows that the most gospel-hardened would be influenced by the character and example of those who were inspired by Christian principles. The services of the speakers and chairman were acknowledged on the proposition of Mr. Holden, seconded by the Rev. C. Potts, formerly superintendent of the Warrington Circuit, and supported by the Rev. John Gubnell. A public meeting was also held at Padgate, and synod sermons were preached at Gym' and Moore.

MEETING IN SUPPORT OF THE LIVERPOOL MISSION.

The concluding public meeting in connection with the Synod, held in the Bewsey-road Church on Thursday evening, was on behalf of the Liverpool Mission, and there was a gratifyingly large attendance. lathe absence of Councillor Dodds, who had an engagement out of town, the chair was occupied by Mr. Phillip Garnett,

... ..,......n..1.

who, in the course of his address, said probably few - men knew more of the late Charles Garratt, the founder of the Liverpool Mission, than his (the speaker's) late father. He himself had had the pleasure of hearing Charles Garrett and of seeing him in his father's house. All those who had heard him and come into his presence could not help but feel the warmth of his heart. I? conclusion Mr. Garnett commended the effort which is being made to raise £16,000 fore memorial hall in Liver- pool.

- The Rev. T. GALL/IND HARTLEY, chairman of the district, wished to heartily express his intense interest in the work of the mission. The late Charles Garratt exerted a powerful influence on Methodism generally and an exceptionally powerful influence on the lives of some in that district. It was only a fitting thing that in Liverpool city and district they should have a memorial to one who did so much work not only in the. cause of religion but also of philanthropy. The great, hall that was to be commenced in the course of the next few months and completed in several years would they believed be a fitting memorial to the late Charles Garratt. In all good work in the Liverpool district Warrington took a proud position and did liberal things - He had had the joy of realizing how great was the, influence for good on circuit life of the missions of modern Methodism. He heartily commended the Liver- pool mission to the sympathy and generosity of the- audience.

The Rev. A. H. WALKER, secretary of the district briefly addressed the gathering. The work of the Liverpool Mission was interestinglr described by Mr. CHADWICK. He referred to "the' wretched, poverty-stricken, drink-ridden masses lived in in that wretched place—the north end of Liver- pool." If the gentlemen who were anxious to protect the interests of the brewers could spend a week or a- fortnight with him in that district he would be able to produce such arguments that if they had hearts to feel and ears to hear, if they had consciences, and a respect for their fellow-men' they would be compelled to gG. back to the House of Commons and say that every maw who represented his fellow-men in this country ought to do his very best to deliver those wh°' had no power to deliver themselves from the over- abounding influences for evil which were crowded anct crammed on their very doorsteps. Mr. Chadwick referred to the large number of public-houses in the, north end of the city, and to the evil which was wrought in the homes by drink. Many a time he had bees moved to tears to see little children in the streets; crushed, cursed, starved and naked. Mr. Chadwick explained how people had been reformed through the agency of the. mission, and spoke hopefully of the work that lay before them. _

The Rev. JOSEPH JACKSON, superintendent of the mission, addressed the meeting. Liverpool, he said,was an improving place. They had reduced the number of licences more than in any other city. The Lord forbid that the rate of reducing should stop! If the Licens- ing Bill passed it simply meant that they would be able- to touch only a handful of licences, and they would have- to pay pretty heavy compensation. The Lord save them from that calamity! In a short snace of time they had reduced the police force by 200, in spite of the growth of population. They had to thank the licensing magis- trates and to see that theirpowers were not curtailed but enlarged. They also had to thank the Vigilance Com- mittee, and the work that Charles Garratt did for over twenty years and the splendid social schee. he inaugurate m d in the British workman public houses opened in every part of the city. Mr. Jackson said the only healing ministry that could reach the people was through the Gospel of Jesus Christ- "Go in for sanitation as fast as you can" said the speaker "but it is not an equivalent to sanctification. Change a man as much as you like, but you have'nt, accomplished much till you change his surroundings.' The rev, gentleman gave details of the many-sided woric of the mission, telling how it extended to those who' had been in Walton gaol, to dwellers in common lodg- ing houses, and to factory girls. If there was anythimiC they emphasised it was work for the young. They had 3,000 Sunday school scholars enrolled and 300 teachers, nearly all of.whom had been converted in the mission. Mr. Jackson told of how tne mission helped the home- less and starving people who drifted to Liverpool from all over the kingdom and from abroad. The wawa! expenditure on the work amounted to £5,500. In regard to the memorial to Charles Garratt, it waa proposed to build a hall to seat 25,000 people and a smaller hall to accommodate 750. They had signed the builder's contract for £27,383 and they had yet to raise. £16,500 in order to open the buildings free from debt. It was hoped that the memorial hall would be a great centre of evangelizing work. (Applause.) Owing to the lateness of the hour Mr. Gipsy Evens, another enthusiastic mission worker, was unable to de- liver an address. Mrs. Evens, who is possessed of a sweet and powerful voice, very feelingly sang sacred solos. The usual votes of thanks concluded a successful meeting.

THE LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE BAPTIST'

ASSOCIATION.

District Meeting.

On Thursday afternoon and evening the spring Tneetr logs of the Warrington and District Baptist Union branch of the above association were held in the historig Baptist Church at Hill Cliffe. The afternoon proceed- ings were somewhat varied, the first part being taken up' by business of the association,the chief item being the change in the title of the association in order to widen the area of the association's influence, it being decided to bring in the Lymm,Widnes, Golborne and Earlestown Churches. Afterwards a very able paper on "The out- look of the Times" was read by the Rev. J. F. Matthews and was much appreciated by his audience,as is evidence& by the discussion that followed. Tea was then partaken of in the schoolroom and was - much enjoyed by those who sat down at the well-laden tables. In the evening a public meeting was held over which the Rev. J. S. Hughes presided. Vigorous speeches were delivered by the Rev. H. Dunnico, the Revs. J. Morley and Mr. Norman Balmford, during the course of which a resolution was proposed by the Rev. H.Dunnico and secondedby Mr.Clatworthy condemning" several proposals of the Licensing Bill, and it was agree'- that a copy of such resolution should be forwarded to' Mr. Balfour the Prime Minister. - -

• A vote of thanks on the proposition of Mr. Few' seconded by Mr. T. Albinson and supported by Mr- Billinge was then carried and the meeting was shortly" after closed by the singing of the Doxology. Both the ' ater noon and evening meetings were well attended.

Clerks' Association. The Liverpool Clerks' Association was founded forty-two years ago with the idea of enabling Liver- pool commercial clerks to make provision by sub- scription against loss of salary while unemployed. The membership now includes merchants, brokers, accountants, managers, secretaries, salesmen, and travellers; and its original area of operations (six miles from the Exchange) has been extended to twenty miles, so as to bring in St. Helens, Warrington, Wigan, Southport, Chester, and other towns. Its objects, too, have been exten- ded, and the advantages now include a weekly allow- ance to unemployed members, medical attendance and medicine during sickness, reduced terms for specialists for members and their families, facilities for obtaining situations, annuities to aged and dis- abled members, limited annuities to widows and orphans of deceased members, assistance in providing careers for members' sons, and the privileges of a reading-room and an excellent commercial library.

An inquest was held at Sunderland on Monday el' David William Naden, aged 20 years, plater's apprentice- who was fatally injured on the 27 ult. whilst cycling' down the Burnt House Bank, just outside the Felton. Fell. It was stated that the brake of his bicycle refused to act, and he collapsed near the bottom of the incline-- His spinal cord was severed, and he died in the Sunder- land Infirmary on Wednesday. A verdict of" Accidental' death "was returned, and the jury expressed the opinion' that a danger notice board ought to be erected on the, top of the bank, many accidents having occurred' there. Deaths. MYERS.—May 7th, at Allen-street, Warrington, Martha Myers, aged 80 years. DUCK. WORTIL—May 7th, at Grafton-street, Warrington r Thomas Duckworth, aged 7G years. BARBER.—Mdy 10th, at Wash-lane, Warrington, .10119 Barber, aged 70 years.
